--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliography of Joséphin Péladan, transitioning from his prose and philosophical works to his dramatic output. It lists staged performances of his plays at prestigious venues and provides a record of his published and forthcoming theatrical works as of approximately 1906.</meta>

<center>
*Performances have included:*
</center>

**THE SON OF THE STARS**, comedy in 3 acts, on March 19, 1892, and on Easter Sunday and Monday 1893, at the Palace of the Champ de Mars. <note>original: "Le Fils des Étoiles." This play is famous in art history for its incidental music composed by Erik Satie, a close associate of Péladan during his "Rose + Croix" period.</note>

**BABYLON**, tragedy in 4 acts, on March 11, 12, 15, 17, and 19, 1893, at the Palace of the Champ de Mars; on May 28, 1894, at the Ambigu Theatre, and on May 30 at the Parc Theatre, in Brussels. It was performed by Lady Caithness, Duchess of Pomar, in her ballroom, on July 5, 1894. <note>Lady Caithness was a prominent figure in Parisian occult circles and a patron of the esoteric arts.</note>

**OEDIPUS AND THE SPHINX**, tragedy in three acts, on August 1, 1903, at the Ancient Theatre of Orange, performed by the artists of the <term>Comédie-Française</term> <note>The state-sanctioned national theater of France, also known as the "House of Molière."</note> and the Odéon.

**SEMIRAMIS**, tragedy in 4 acts, on *July 24, at the Ancient Amphitheatre of Nîmes*, under the auspices of the Local Initiative Syndicate for the regional interests of Gard, performed by the artists of the Comédie-Française and the Odéon; on January 23, 1905, for the inauguration of the **ANCIENT THEATRE OF NATURE**, at Champigny, under the presidency of the Minister of Public Instruction and Fine Arts, performed by the <term>sociétaires</term> <note>Full members or senior partners of the Comédie-Française acting troupe.</note> of the Comédie-Française; on August 12, 1905, at the **THEATRE OF LA BOURBOULE**, under the presidency of the Minister of the Colonies, by the members of the Comédie-Française.

<page-num>8</page-num>
### SELECTED TEXTS

Vinci is not only an excellent painter, but a true Archimedes <note>Archimedes (c. 287–212 BC) was a Greek mathematician and inventor; the comparison emphasizes Leonardo's scientific and engineering brilliance.</note>; he is also a great philosopher."

We knew, through Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574) was an Italian painter and architect, famous for writing the first real history of art.</note>, the variety of skills of this notary's son. A detestable artist himself, the biographer writes beautifully, and his first page, dedicated to Leonardo, deserves to be reread constantly:

> "We see the rarest gifts descend through celestial influence into human bodies, often naturally, sometimes supernaturally; we see beauty, grace, and talent embodied without measure in a single person, and to such an extent that, whichever way this man turns, each of his actions is so divine that, rising above other men, it is clearly evident that he exercises a gift from God rather than acting through the effort of human art like other mortals. This is what men were able to contemplate in the person of Leonardo da Vinci.

> "Without speaking of the beauty of his body, which cannot be praised enough, he brought into each of his acts a grace more than infinite; he acquired such talent that toward whatever difficulty he pleased to turn, he resolved it without trouble. His strength was very great and joined with dexterity: his spirit and his courage always had a royal and magnanimous character; and the..."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci’s historical reputation, transitioning from general praise to specific contemporary accounts of his manuscripts and his masterpiece, The Last Supper.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

the fame of his name spread to such an extent that not only was he celebrated during his lifetime, but since his death his glory has grown. Truly admirable and celestial was Leonardo, son of Ser Piero da Vinci."

Now, Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the first great art historian, known for his "Lives of the Artists."</note> certainly did not read the manuscripts that Melzi <note>Francesco Melzi, Leonardo’s favorite pupil and heir to his notebooks.</note> showed him; he *saw* them; that is to say, he admired the drawings scattered throughout this truly fantastic grimoire <note>A book of magic or secret knowledge; here used to describe the complex nature of Leonardo's notebooks.</note> which seems more like the notebook of a man who takes notes based on vast readings, rather than those of a single inventor drawing such diverse and important things from his own brain.

The swelling of words is quite appropriate when Fra Luca Paccioli, the author of *Divine Proportion* <note>original: "Divina Proportione," a famous mathematical treatise on the golden ratio.</note> (the figures of which are by Leonardo), exclaims in his dedication to the Duke of Milan: "Already, Leonardo with his sublime hand had expressed the image of the ardent desire for our salvation in the worthy and respectable place of spiritual and corporeal refreshment of the holy temple of the Graces <note>A reference to the refectory of the Santa Maria delle Grazie convent, where Leonardo painted The Last Supper.</note>, to which henceforth all rivals of Apelles, Myron, and Polycletus <note>Famous artists of Greek antiquity, used here as the ultimate standard of excellence.</note> must yield."

Lomazzo <note>Giovanni Paolo Lomazzo, a painter and theorist who wrote extensively on Leonardo.</note> would call him "Hermes-Prometheus"; Paolo Giovio <note>original: "Paul Jove," a physician and historian who wrote a short biography of Leonardo around 1527.</note> traces this portrait of him: "He was of a charming spirit, very brilliant, and entirely generous: his face was the most beautiful in the world. Marvel—

<summary>This page examines the legendary status of Leonardo da Vinci through the eyes of his contemporaries, highlighting the awe inspired by his notebooks and his masterpiece, The Last Supper.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Giorgio Vasari, Francesco Melzi, Luca Paccioli, The Last Supper, Divine Proportion, Paolo Giovio, art history, Renaissance</keywords>

> ...inventor and judge of all elegance and especially *of theatrical delights* <note>original: "deliciarum theatralium." One could multiply these citations as needed without exhausting them. Benvenuto Cellini <note>A celebrated Renaissance goldsmith and sculptor known for his autobiography.</note> says regarding the *Treatise on Painting*: 
> "I do not believe that a greater man ever came into the world."

We did not, therefore, discover Leonardo in the <term>19th Century</term> <note>The author is refuting the idea that Leonardo's genius was a modern "rediscovery," noting he was famous in his own time.</note>. He was admired and loved, both living and dead. Vasari wonders how, possessing almost nothing and working little, he always had servants. Leonardo was seductive. Many who loved him did not realize the almost fabulous character of this man who was, at once, the greatest artist, the boldest scientist, and the most handsome man of his era.

> "The greatest physicist of the fifteenth century, a man who, with a very rare knowledge of mathematics, united to a surprising degree the faculty of plunging his gaze into the depths of nature, Leonardo da Vinci was the contemporary of Columbus. He died three years after him. The artist crowned with glory had devoted himself to the study of meteorology as well as to that of hydraulics and

"If Leonardo da Vinci's ideas on physics had not remained buried in his manuscripts, the field of observation opened by the New World would have been scientifically explored in a great many of its parts, before the great era of Galileo, Pascal, and Huygens <note>Christiaan Huygens (1629–1695), a Dutch physicist and astronomer who made major contributions to optics and mechanics.</note>.

"Like Francis Bacon and at least a century earlier, Leonardo held induction <note>The logical process of drawing general conclusions from specific observations.</note> to be the only legitimate method in the science of nature: 'We must begin from experience, and by means of this, discover the reason.' <note>original: "Dobbiamo cominciare dall’esperienza, et per mezzo di queste, scoprire la ragione."</note>

Before Cuvier <note>Georges Cuvier (1769–1832), a founding father of paleontology.</note>, he attributed the carving of valleys to torrents of water; before Bernard Palissy <note>A French Huguenot potter and engineer (c. 1510–1589) who was one of the first to correctly identify fossils as remains of living organisms.</note> who was a great geologist, he rediscovered the traces of a vanished oceanic world: he anticipated the philosophical division of animal forms by saying of shellfish: 'Animals that have their bones on the outside.' <note>original: "Animali che anno l’ossa di fuori."</note>

One must arrive at the 19th century and even its end, to find an equitable appreciation of this limitless intelligence.

Whatever the courtesy of this charming woman—a model of the virtuous woman of letters—she would not have written in such a respectful and coaxing tone to any other painter; just as Francis I <note>King of France and Leonardo's final patron</note> would not have called even a Titian or an Andrea del Sarto "My father."

> "The aforesaid testator gives and grants to Master Francesco Melzi, a Milanese gentleman, to thank him for the services he has rendered him in the past, all and each of the books that the said testator now possesses, and other instruments and drawings concerning his art and the profession of painter." <note>Excerpt from Leonardo's last will and testament, dated April 23, 1519</note>

But no one suspected the importance of the manuscripts. —mes, all written in the vernacular <note>original: "langue vulgaire"; referring to Italian rather than Latin, which was unusual for scientific works of the time</note> and which, once published, will be of the greatest utility and the greatest charm."

Mr. Uzielli <note>Gustavo Uzielli (1839–1911), an Italian scholar who specialized in Leonardo’s biography and geography</note> has provided a complete history of these precious manuscripts.

Melzi, upon leaving Amboise, retired to Vaprio <note>The Melzi family villa at Vaprio d’Adda</note> where he prepared an edition of the *Treatise on Painting*. Cellini <note>Benvenuto Cellini, the celebrated Florentine goldsmith</note> had purchased, at the price of fifteen gold crowns, a copy which he shared with Serlio <note>Sebastiano Serlio, a pioneer of Renaissance architectural theory</note> "who brought to light, in the form of perspective, the little of it he could understand."

Vasari and Lomazzo <note>Giorgio Vasari and Giovanni Paolo Lomazzo, two of the earliest art historians and theorists to study Leonardo's legacy</note> saw the manuscripts. After the death of Melzi, they fell into the hands of his son Horatio, a simpleton <note>original: "imbécile"</note> who employed Lelio Gavardi d’Isola as a tutor in his home. This individual discovered Leonardo’s books and drawings in old crates in the attic.

He managed to have thirteen manuscripts given to him and brought them to Florence to offer them to the Grand Duke, but the latter died. Lelio Gavardi went to Pisa to study law; there he met Mazzenta <note>Ambrogio Mazzenta, who wrote an important early account of the manuscripts' history</note> who wrote a narrative of this adventure (translated by E. Piot). Following sharp reproaches, Lelio Gavardi, repenting of his lack of integrity, charged Mazzenta with the restitution. But Horatio Melzi made him a gift of everything he was bringing back, adding that many other drawings of

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts following his death, detailing the dispersal of his papers from the Melzi estate to various European collections.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
-># ON LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

Leonardo's papers were scattered "in the rooms of his villa, under roofs in poor condition." <note>This refers to Villa Melzi in Vaprio d'Adda, where Leonardo's pupil Francesco Melzi kept the collection until his death in 1570.</note>

Mazzenta and his brothers took pride in their treasures, and many others obtained pages or notebooks from Horatio Melzi. Among them was Pompeo Arettino Leoni, who offered a seat in the Senate of Milan in exchange for the thirteen manuscripts given to the Mazzentas. The latter returned seven of them at the entreaty of Horatio Melzi.

One of the six others was offered to Cardinal Borromeo [*Treatise on Shadow and Light* <note>original: "Traité de l’ombre et de la lumière"</note> (now at the Ambrosian Library)]: another to the painter Figgini, a third to the Duke of Savoy: the remaining three eventually fell into the hands of Pompeo Leoni, who compiled them into a collection. Cleodaro Calchi inherited this and sold it for 300 scudi <term>scudi: a historical Italian silver coin</term> to Arconati, who later refused an offer of 600 scudi from the Duke of Savoy.

In 1637, Arconati donated the *Codex Atlanticus* <note>original: "Codex Atlantico"; a massive 12-volume set of Leonardo's drawings and writings</note> (1,600 sheets across 393 folio pages) to the Ambrosian Library, along with nine of the manuscripts currently held at the Institute, which were sent to Paris in 1796: the Codex for the National Library <note>The Bibliothèque Nationale de France</note> and the twelve others for the Institute. When the Austrian commissioner reclaimed Leonardo's manuscripts in 1815, they searched the National Library but only found the *Atlanticus*, which was then restored to Italy.

Mr. Léopold Delisle <note>A famous French librarian and historian (1826–1910)</note> will retain the honor of having returned the two volumes to the National Library

They do not nourish themselves with any living thing, and like our Leonardo, they do not permit anyone to harm any living being."

Michelet <note>Jules Michelet (1798–1874), a prominent French historian.</note> goes to the *Mona Lisa* <note>original: "Joconde"</note>, "as a bird goes to the serpent"; "he is fascinated, troubled; an infinity acts upon him through a strange magnetism." Taine <note>Hippolyte Taine (1828–1893), a French critic and historian.</note> discovers a Mephistophelean expression in the *Saint John*.

There are species within humanity between which the unconscious places an antagonism that even the highest culture cannot resolve. What is the use of seeking out false impressions; everyone has been mistaken about Leonardo—I mean all those who have judged him without knowing his manuscripts. <header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<page-num>19</page-num>

timately formed of the master, based on his works. <note>Continuing the sentence from page 18: "legitimately."</note>

What dominates to the point of making one forget all other qualities, in the compositions as in the sketches, is spirituality or rather subtlety. The enigmatic gazes, the ambiguous smiles, the complex expressions, even the fairy-tale background of the *Mona Lisa* <note>original: "Joconde"</note> and the *Virgin and Child with Saint Anne*, manifest, it seems, a spirit strained toward mystery, a dreamer enamored only with his own visions.

Yet, this mage or this mystic reveals himself above all as a physicist and mathematician, and this thaumaturge <term>thaumaturge: a worker of wonders or miracles</term> of the human face believes in neither miracles nor spirits.

His intellectual health equals that of the most famous Hellenes <note>The ancient Greeks.</note>: his true patrons are named Aristotle and Archimedes. No one has dreamed less, if by that one means letting one's thoughts float or concentrating them upon oneself. Reality attracts him, charms him, dazzles him: the arrangement of leaves on branches makes him burst with admiration, and in the seashell he picks up, he sees the history of continents. The instinct of animals and the industry of men captivate him as well.

The manuscript E contains a stunning citation, "he refers to chapter IV of book 113 *of natural things* <note>original: "delle cose naturali"</note>," corroborated by another passage where he speaks, as justification for his

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci's writing style, specifically his mirror-writing, and transitions into a discussion of his various scientific and artistic treatises and his academy in Milan.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>ON LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<sig>2.</sig>

custom. His writing, elegant and very regular, gives from a certain distance the illusion of a very diligent <term>Gothic cursive</term><note>A style of handwriting common in the late Middle Ages characterized by looped and flowing letters.</note>. He was ignorant of Oriental languages and Greek, and knew only very little Latin. One must attribute his manner <note>Referring to his mirror-writing.</note> to the desire—so natural in such an inventor possessed of such an independent spirit—to discourage the indiscretion of others regarding theories that fell under the scrutiny of the <term>Inquisition</term><note>The ecclesiastical tribunal of the Catholic Church responsible for suppressing heresy.</note> and machines whose discovery might be stolen from him.

Did Leonardo conceive the idea of an Encyclopedia? No. The inherent movement of his intelligence led him toward <term>encyclopedism</term><note>The pursuit of universal knowledge across all fields of study.</note>. In reality, we only know as formal treatises the *Treatise on Painting*, the *Treatise on Shadows and Light*, the *Treatise on Anatomy*, the *Treatise on the Flight of Birds*, the *Treatise on the Movement of Water*, the *Treatise on Geometry*, and *Book IV of the World and Waters*.

But we know, by <term>monograms</term><note>Decorative symbols made by overlapping letters.</note> curiously adorned with cords—<insert>Franciscan or otherwise</insert>—knotted in the fashion of Oriental interlacing, that the master had founded an academy in Milan titled with his own name.

There is no doubt that the spirit which, in speaking of perspective, reached as far as celestial mechanics and which even studied alchemy and <term>chiromancy</term><note>Palm reading or divination through the study of the hand.</note>, brought into pedagogy,

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Leonardo da Vinci’s intellectual breadth, moving from his formal treatises to the more personal and technical nature of his notebooks, specifically highlighting his roles as an engineer and military inventor.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

the character of universality in his own research. For the most part, the formulas that astonish us were originally notes for his lectures, just as his jokes, his prophecies of rational animals, his fables, and his plant and animal symbolism provide an insight into the nature of his mind and his way of storytelling and conversing.

The civil and military engineer, the man of arts and crafts, and the mechanic occupy a considerable place in the manuscripts. The small figures that assist in the demonstration—maneuvering the winch of a cannon foundry, or using a perfected bow and throwing terrible grenades—are, from the perspective of drawing, pure marvels. One will not find the machines here: the <term>architonnerre</term><note>original: "architonnerre"; a steam-powered cannon invented by Leonardo, literally "arch-thunder."</note> (*sic*), the large <term>bombard</term><note>An early type of large-caliber cannon used for throwing heavy stone balls.</note> loaded through the breech which a single servant screws and unscrews (*B. 24, v.*), the scythed chariots, the cannonballs that scatter bullets (*B. 80 v.*), nor the problems of <term>ballistics</term><note>The science of the motion of projectiles in flight.</note> similar to this one:

> "Does the power of the cannonball depend on its initial velocity? I ask where the ignited powder gains the most force within the bombard? Is it in the breech or in the barrel, and in what part of the barrel or the breech?" (*I, 133, v.*)

There are, however, vivid images that one regrets not being able to provide, such as these:

> "In the bombard, the powder acts like a man with his back pressed against a wall, pushing an object with his hands." — "The hand of the swimmer that strikes the water and, by leaning upon it, makes his body flee in the opposite direction, imitates the wing of a bird."

To describe Leonardo's manuscript work, one would need several volumes, a thousand illustrations, and highly varied commentaries that no man today could produce from his own resources alone. To truly measure Leonardo, a dozen specialists would have to apply themselves with great labor.

Certain passages, being brief or isolated from their context, remain enigmatic or insignificant. While piety toward this superhuman mortal would applaud a complete translation of his notebooks, very few would find profit or pleasure in it.

"In Lombardy many women have beards." Should this be understood as it is for Brittany, where the woman commands the household <note>A metaphorical reference to "wearing the pants" or holding authority</note>, or is it meant literally? "What do cranes do when they ascend without flapping their wings?" !!

Technically, certain precepts hold no interest: "If you want to make a figure of marble, first make a figure in clay... etc..." What does the defense system applicable to the <term>counterscarp</term><note>The outer slope of a ditch or moat in a fortification, facing the enemy.</note> of a trench matter to us, unless one is writing a history of <term>Poliorcetics</term><note>original: "Poliocertique" (likely a misspelling of Poliorcétique); the art of conducting and resisting sieges.</note>?

of expression: "according to him who then measured" and "according to him who counted the time."

"Leonardo has a conception of the soul so heretical that it belongs more to a philosopher than to a Christian," asserts Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian Renaissance artists.</note>. Undoubtedly, Leonardo is a philosopher, but the biographer is mistaken; the conception of the soul held by the author of the *Last Supper* <note>original: "Cenacolo"; Leonardo's famous mural in Milan.</note> is not at odds with dogma, only with routine.

In the *Codex Atlanticus* <note>The largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings, covering subjects from weaponry to botany.</note> one will even find a recipe for perfuming oneself: "Take good rose water, moisten your hands with it, add lavender and then rub your hands together. This is good." In this vein, one could glean a few curious details, though perhaps a bit trivial when dealing with such a dignified memory. "Illness is a discord of the elements mixed in the vital body, and medicine is the restoration of unbalanced elements."

Is there truly any interest in translating this: "The Rhône leaves Lake Geneva and runs first to the west, and after a course of 400 miles pours its waters into the Mediterranean Sea," or even: "At Bordeaux, in Gascony, the high sea has an ebb of forty fathoms <note>original: "brasses"; an old unit of length based on the span of outstretched arms.</note> and the river swallows back the salt water for more than 150 miles..."

Here then is the interior portrait of Leonardo by himself; here is how he felt, here is what he thought; and if there should be someone indifferent to the beating of such a heart and to the ideas of such a brain, that person belongs to the series of <term>anthropomorphs</term> <note>Literally "human-shaped"; the author implies those who look human but lack higher intellectual or spiritual culture.</note> with whom culture does not concern itself, considering neither the plasticity of animals nor their <term>ideo-grammatic</term> <note>A term suggesting the symbolic or conceptual value of a form; how an image conveys an idea.</note> value.

One does not admire Leonardo as one does a simple Velázquez <note>Diego Velázquez (1599–1660), a master Spanish painter known for realism; the author suggests Leonardo requires a deeper, more philosophical engagement.</note>. One loves him if one understands him, and love always finds ecstasy in a thousand points that are indifferent to the common crowd.

This work was born of a spiritual passion; it is addressed to those passionate about the same object.

Da Vinci represents an enchanted mirror where every man can find reasons for courage and hope. Since humanity has blossomed so fully in his features, one must despair neither of humanity, nor of oneself.

Da Vinci reconciles us with our species and with ourselves! This man for whom nothing was favorable and who was treated according to his merit only once, by the King of France <note>King Francis I, who invited Leonardo to spend his final years at Clos Lucé.</note>; this Aristotle <note>The ancient Greek philosopher; the author compares Leonardo's encyclopedic mind to Aristotle's, though only fragments of their work remain.</note> of whom there remain only aphorisms and exclamations; this artist of whom not a single intact work has survived, and who with a single drawing prevails over all drawings without...

<meta>This page serves as a formal preface or introductory note to a collection of Leonardo da Vinci's writings. The tone is apologetic yet reverent, establishing the editorial philosophy of the work.</meta>

# NOTE

*Is it possible, in a work of this nature, to satisfy both those who demand the texts in their entirety <note>original: "in extenso"</note> and others who will judge the selection to be too extensive?*

*Let the former inform themselves of the number of pages that the fulfillment of their desire would require, even in the largest format, and also of the necessity of multiplying the demonstrative figures <note>referring to Leonardo's technical drawings and diagrams</note>, without which the text cannot be presented.*

*Let the others consider that there is only one way to show a polyhedron <term>polyhedron: a three-dimensional geometric solid with flat faces and straight edges</term>: and that is to show its sides one after another.*

*Let everyone remember that these texts were originally in the state of fragments, even simple phrases, and that we have only reunited scattered limbs <note>a metaphor for reassembling a body of work</note> and not complete discourses from truncated citations.*

*Finally, let this book be received as it is offered: to those who love Leonardo da Vinci, to make him better known to them; and to those who already know him, so that they may love him more.*

*No other honor has been sought here than that of true piety, for geniuses deserve the same devotion as saints: this is a book of devotion.*

<summary>The author justifies the editorial decision to present Leonardo da Vinci's writings as a curated selection of fragments rather than a complete collection, framing the work as an act of secular devotion to a genius.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, editorial philosophy, polyhedrons, fragments, book of devotion, art history, manuscript curation</keywords>

## LEONARDO TO THE READER

**1.** — Seeing that I could not find a subject of great utility or pleasure, since the men born before me have taken for themselves all the useful and necessary themes, I will do like the one who, out of poverty, arrives last at the fair; unable to provide for himself otherwise, he buys the things already seen by others and rejected because of their little value.

Upon this despised and rejected merchandise, coming from many stalls, I will place my meager savings, and thus I will go not through the great cities, but into the poor villages, distributing and receiving the price deserved by the thing I give. <note>Original: "C.A. 119, r." refers to the Codex Atlanticus, folio 119 recto.</note>

**2.** — Begun in Florence, in the house of Bracceo Martelli, on March 22, 1508: this forms a collection without order of many sheets that I have copied, hoping to classify them in their proper place, according to the subject of which they treat. And I believe that before reaching the end of this one, I will have to re-

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci introduces his writings with a humble metaphor of a merchant collecting overlooked ideas and notes that his manuscripts are a disorganized collection intended for future classification.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, preface, Florence, Bracceo Martelli, Codex Atlanticus, writing method, intellectual humility</keywords>

<header>SELECTED TEXTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<warning>Significant bleed-through from the reverse side of the page creates ghosting behind the primary text, though it remains legible.</warning>

...peat the same things several times. If this happens, reader, do not blame me: the subjects are numerous and the memory cannot retain them all. — I do not wish to write something, having already said it — and if I wanted to avoid falling into this error, it would be necessary every time I wished to transcribe a passage, in order to avoid repetition, that I re-read everything I had written; this would delay me significantly, for I have written over long intervals of time, and fragment by fragment. <note>Original: "R. 4." refers to the numbering in Jean-Paul Richter's seminal 1883 anthology of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

**3.** — Let no one read me who is not a mathematician, for I am always a mathematician in my principles. <note>Leonardo believed that nature was governed by mathematical laws and that "no human investigation can be called true science if it cannot be demonstrated mathematically." Original reference: R. 3.</note>

# I

## THEODICY
<note>Theodicy: A philosophical defense of God's goodness in the face of the existence of evil.</note>

Prayers (4-8). — Love is born from knowledge (11). — The infinite (12). — Truth (13-16). — Whom should we honor? (19). — Hypocrisy and Simony <note>Simony: The buying or selling of ecclesiastical privileges or church offices.</note> (20-23). — Jests: "prophecies of the rational animals" (24).

4. — I bless you, Lord, first for the love that, according to my reason, I must bear for you, and then because you know how to shorten or prolong the lives of men. (*S. K. M. III. 64, l.*)

5. — You, O God, sell all good things to men at the price of effort. (*W. An. IV. 172°.*)

6. — Admirable is your justice, First Cause! <note>First Cause: A philosophical term for the self-created origin of all things, usually identified as God.</note> You have not permitted any force to fail in the order and quality of its necessary effects. (*W. An. 242, a.*)

7. — May it please Our Author <note>Leonardo often refers to God as the "Author" or "Prime Mover."</note> that I have well demonstrated the nature of man and his faculties through these descriptive figures. (*R. 798.*)

8. — May it please the Lord, light of all

If you do not know God, you cannot love Him; if you love Him for the benefit you expect from Him and not for His sovereign virtue, you are like the dog that wags its tail and jumps for joy at the one who is about to give it a bone; if the animal knew the superiority of man, it would love him much more. (*R.* 837.)

> 12. — What is the indefinable thing that would cease to exist if it could be formulated? The infinite! For it would be finite if it could be defined!
> 
> For to define is to limit, and limits belong simultaneously to at least several points of extremities: which contradicts the notion of the limitless. (*C. A.* 113, *v.*)

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

13. — Truth alone was the daughter of time. <note>original: "La vérité seule fut fille du temps." This refers to the classical proverb *Veritas filia temporis*, suggesting that truth is eventually revealed as time passes.</note> (*M. 58, v.*)

14. — Falsehood is so contemptible that, even if it speaks of great things concerning God, it strips all grace from the divinity.

Truth is of such excellence that, by praising small things, it renders them noble. (*C. A. 118, r.*)

Obstacles to the truth are transformed into contrition. <note>In this context, Leonardo likely means that facing the truth brings a sense of remorse or a "crushing" of the ego.</note>

Undoubtedly, the proportion of falsehood to truth is as that of darkness to light; and truth is of such excellent essence that even if it is applied to humble and lowly matters, it still, beyond comparison, surpasses uncertain and deceptive elaborations and rises above grand and sublime discourses. For our spirit, even though it holds falsehood as its fifth element, nonetheless regards the truth of things as the sovereign nourishment—not for wandering spirits, but for true intellects.

But you who live on dreams, you take more pleasure in sophistical and barbaric arguments and in speaking of great and uncertain things, than in matters of lesser scope, but of natural certainty. (*Tr. 12, r.*)

15. — Fire destroys falsehood, that is to say, sophistry <note>A "sophism" is a clever but false argument intended to deceive.</note>, and brings truth out of the darkness. The mission of fire is to consume all soph-

<sig>3</sig>

<summary>This page features Leonardo da Vinci's reflections on the intrinsic value of truth, arguing that even humble truths are superior to grand lies or "sophistical" arguments.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, truth, falsehood, sophistry, natural certainty, philosophy of science, light and darkness</keywords>

<page-num>38</page-num>

16. — O contemplator, I do not praise you for knowing the things that nature, ordinarily and by herself, conducts according to her natural laws; but rejoice in discovering the purpose of those things which are conceived within your mind. *(C. 47, v.)*

<note>1. These anti-clerical <term>anti-clerical: opposing the power or influence of the clergy</term> remarks are part of the "fables" or "jests." It seemed characteristic to place them alongside the religious affirmations that precede them. Mockery or invective against the clergy is found in the mouths and under the pens of the most devout, and especially the mystics. One understands that the clergy attempted to identify itself with religion and to impose respect for its person, but a Savonarole <note>Girolamo Savonarola (1452–1498), a Dominican friar and preacher in Renaissance Florence known for his prophecies and calls for Christian renewal.</note> or a Saint Catherine of Siena exceed in violence everything that the enemies of the Church have uttered. It must not be forgotten that Leonardo, military engineer to Cesare Borgia, lived under the pontificate of Alexander VI; and when he says: "the good brothers are Pharisees," one must remember that Saint Francis died on Saturday, October 3, 1226, at twilight, and that the Franciscan Word has been defeated for two centuries by Dominican action. See *The Doctrine of Dante* (Sansot).</note>

**28.** — Man is called by the ancients a minor world <note>The "microcosm"</note>, a just title, for he is composed of earth, water, air, and fire, like the body of the earth, and he resembles it. If man has his bones to serve as a framework and to support the flesh, the world has its rocks which support its soil; if man has within him a lake of blood where the lung expands and contracts for his respiration, the body of the earth has its ocean sea which rises and falls every six hours for its respiration; if from this lake of blood derive the veins which go branching through the entire organism, so the ocean sea fills the terrestrial body with innumerable veins of water: but our globe lacks the nerves which were not given to it; for they are intended for movement. Now, the world in its perpetual stability does not move, and where there is no movement, nerves are useless. But for all the rest, man and the world are similar. (*C. A.* 80, *r.*)

**29.** — If nature had fixed a single rule for the quality of limbs, the faces of all men would be similar, and one could not distinguish them from one another: but she has so varied the five parts of the face that although she has established a general rule for proportion, she has followed none for quality, in such a way that one can easily recognize each person. (*C. A.* 70, *r.*)

**30.** — I have found in the constitution of the human body, as in that of other animals, the most dull and coarse senses: composed of instruments without ingenuity and partly unfit to receive the virtue of the senses.

I have seen in the lion species <term>lion species<note>original: "espèce léonine"</note></term> the sense of smell partaking of the cerebral substance, descending from the nostrils, which are an excellent receptacle for this sense of smell; it is counted among the cartilaginous sacs, which function better than the human brain.

The eyes of the lion species occupy a large part of the head, and the optic nerves communicate immediately with the brain. In men, one sees the opposite: the eye sockets take up little space in the head, and the optic nerves—thin, long, and weak—operate feebly; man sees little by day and less at night, while the aforementioned animals see better at night than by day; and this does not hinder them, because they go out at night and sleep during the day, as nocturnal birds also do. (*R. 827.*) <note>R. 827 refers to Jean Paul Richter's 1883 compilation of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

**31.** — The eye, at a medium distance and under average conditions, is less deceived in its function than any other sense, because it only sees the straight lines that compose the pyramid <note>Leonardo’s "visual pyramid" theory: the idea that light rays travel in straight lines from an object to the eye, forming a pyramid with the eye at the apex.</note> formed by the base of the object and which leads them to the eye.

The ear is deceived about the location and distance

32. — There are four <term>powers</term> <note>These are the "faculties of the soul" in Renaissance psychology.</note>: <term>memory</term> and <term>intellect</term>; the <term>irascible</term> <note>The drive toward difficult goals or overcoming obstacles.</note> and the <term>concupiscible</term> <note>The drive toward simple pleasures or desires.</note>.

36. — It does not seem to me that coarse men, of low manners and little wit, deserve so beautiful an <term>organism</term> nor such a variety of <term>mechanisms</term> <note>original: "rouages," literally "gears" or "workings."</note> as speculative men of great spirit. The former are nothing but a sack where food enters and from which it leaves. They should be likened to a canal for nourishment, for nothing proves to me that they belong to the <term>human species</term>, except for their voice and figure; in everything else, they are quite similar to beasts. (*R. 1178.*)

39. — The common sense <term>common sense: translated from the Latin 'sensus communis', referring to a theoretical brain faculty that integrates all five physical senses</term> is that which judges the impressions transmitted to it by the other senses.

The common sense possesses the mediating movement for the impressions transmitted by the five senses.

The senses move according to objects, sending their likenesses to the five senses which transmit them to the sensibility; and this sensibility transmits them to the common sense: and it, being the judge, sends everything to the memory, in which, according to their power, more or less, they are preserved.

The ancient speculators have concluded that this part of judgment, given to man, derives its cause from an organ to which the five senses refer by means of the sensibility.

They have given the name of common sense to this sense and place it in the middle of the head. This name comes from the fact that it is the judge of the other senses. It...

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s anatomical and philosophical exploration of the human mind. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, completing the description of the "common sense" (sensus communis) and its relationship to sensibility and the physical senses.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
### SELECTED TEXTS

...is moved by means of the sensibility, placed in the middle, between it and the senses.

The sensibility moves by means of the likeness <note>original: "similitude"</note> of things transmitted to it by the surface instruments, or senses, which are placed in the middle between external objects and the sensibility; likewise, the senses move according to the objects.

The likeness of surrounding things is transmitted to the senses, which pass it to the sensibility; the sensibility itself offers these to the common sense <term>common sense: the 'sensus communis', a theoretical brain faculty that Leonardo believed acted as a central processor for all sensory data</term>, through which they enter the memory where they remain, according to their greater or lesser importance.

Which sense is faster in its duty and closer to the sensibility than the eye, the superior and prince of the others? We shall speak of it specifically, leaving the other four aside, so as not to prolong our subject. (*C. A. 90, r.*) <note>C. A. refers to the Codex Atlanticus, the largest collection of Leonardo's notes and drawings.</note>

Now, observe, reader, what we are to believe of our ancestors who sought to define what the soul and life are—unprovable things, for they are not matters that experience can clearly know and prove, since for so many centuries they have been ignored or falsely believed.

The eye, which clearly experiences its own duty, has until this time been defined in various ways by countless authors; I shall prove through experience that it functions in another way. (*C. A. 119, r.*)

<vocab>sensibility, likeness, common sense, memory, the soul, life, experience, the eye, C. A. (Codex Atlanticus)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci outlines a physiological hierarchy of perception, tracing how external "likenesses" travel from the senses to the "common sense" and memory. He asserts the primacy of the eye and champions empirical experience over the speculative definitions of life and the soul provided by ancient authorities.</summary>
<keywords>sensory perception, common sense, optics, empiricism, memory, human anatomy, Codex Atlanticus, philosophy of science</keywords>

> **40. —** *On the Soul*: The movement of earth against earth compresses it, and the part that is struck barely moves.
>
> Water struck by water forms circles around the place that was struck;
>
> Over a long distance, the voice travels through the air.
>
> Even further in fire.
>
> Furthest of all, the spirit travels through the universe.
>
> But that which is finite does not extend into the infinite.
> (*H. 67, v.*) <note>This refers to Manuscript H, folio 67 verso, in the collection of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

> **41. —** Now, behold: hope and the desire to return to one’s homeland and to one’s original state is like the light to the moth; and man, with a continual longing, always yearns for the new spring, and always for a new state, and for the coming months and the new years; and when the desired things arrive, it is too late, and man does not realize that he is thus yearning for his own ruin.
>
> But this desire is the <term>quintessence</term> <note>The "fifth essence" or purest substance, thought in medieval science to compose the celestial bodies and permeate all nature.</note> of the elemental spirits which are found enclosed, by the soul, within the human body; man aspires incessantly to return to his sender <note>original: "mandataire" — referring to the source or creator of the soul.</note>. And you know that this same desire, and this <term>quintessence</term>, is the companion of nature, just as man is the model of the world <note>A reference to the "Microcosm," the Renaissance idea that the human body reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note>.
>
> And man possesses a supreme madness which makes him always suffer, in the hope of suffering no more; and life escapes him while he is still hoping.

<summary>Leonardo reflects on the soul's movement through the universe and the tragic irony of human desire, where the longing for the future is actually an unconscious yearning for the soul to return to its origin through death.</summary>
<keywords>soul, physics of motion, microcosm, quintessence, mortality, human desire, philosophy of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Leonardo da Vinci’s philosophical and anatomical notes, transitioning from his reflections on the soul's longing for its origin to a technical discussion of how the soul interacts with the physical body through the brain and nervous system.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

...ll waiting to enjoy the goods he has acquired at the cost of great effort. (*R.* 1187.) <note>Reference to Jean Paul Richter’s "The Literary Works of Leonardo da Vinci."</note>

**42. —** The soul appears to reside in the <term>judiciary part</term> <note>The "judging" or "processing" faculty of the mind.</note> and the <term>judiciary part</term> appears to be in the place where all the senses converge. This has been called the <term>common sense</term> <note>original: "sens commun" — the Aristotelian concept of the *sensus communis*, a central faculty where different sensory inputs (sight, sound, etc.) are unified.</note>, and this should not be understood to mean the entire body, as many have believed, but only the brain. For if the soul were everywhere and entirely in every part, it would not be necessary for the instruments of the senses to converge at the same place; it would suffice for the eye alone to perform the office of feeling upon its surface, without sending, by way of the <term>optic nerves</term>, the likeness of things seen to the <term>judiciary sense</term>; the soul, for the reason stated above, would be able to understand it on the surface of the eye.

Similarly, for the sense of hearing, the voice resonating in the parent concavity of the <term>stony bone</term> <note>original: "os pierreux" — the petrous part of the temporal bone, which houses the inner ear.</note> found in the ear would be enough, without making any transit to the <term>common sense</term> where the voice meets and addresses it.

The sense of smell, too, is seen by necessity to be constrained to converge with the judgment; <term>touch</term> passes through the nerves and transmits; and these nerves spread out in tiny ramifications under the skin that covers the membranes of the body and the <term>viscera</term> <note>The internal organs of the body.</note>.

The nerves carry command and feeling to the muscles, and together nerves and muscles command these latter to move—

<vocab>judiciary part, common sense, optic nerves, stony bone, touch, viscera, anatomy, physiology, soul, Leonardo da Vinci</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the soul is localized in a specific part of the brain (the "common sense") rather than distributed throughout the body, using the anatomy of the nervous system as evidence. He describes how sensory data from the eyes, ears, and skin must travel to this central point to be processed by the judgment.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, neurobiology, sensus communis, soul, optic nerves, petrous bone, sensory perception, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

---

ment. And they obey, and in obeying they swell as they act: and their swelling shortens their length and pulls them back through the network of the particles of the limbs, ending at the tips of the fingers. They carry to the judgment the reason for their contact.

The nerves with their muscles serve like soldiers to the <term>condottieri</term> <note>Military commanders or mercenary leaders in Renaissance Italy.</note>, and the muscles serve the <term>common sense</term> <note>original: "sens commun" — the central faculty of the brain where all senses meet.</note> as the <term>condottieri</term> serve the captain. Therefore, the joint of the bones obeys the nerve, and the nerve the muscle, and the muscle the cord <note>Leonardo often uses "cord" to refer to tendons.</note>, and the cord the <term>common sense</term>; and the <term>common sense</term> is the seat of the soul, and memory is its ammunition, and sensitivity is its reference. (*R. 838.*)

43. — The soul cannot be corrupted by the corruption of the body, but acts in the manner of the wind, which is the cause of the sound in the organ; now, if the casing is spoiled, no effect results for the wind itself. (*T. 32, r.*)

44. — Whoever wishes to see how the soul inhabits the body has only to look at how the body uses its daily habitation; if it is disordered and confused, then disordered and confused will be the body possessed by the soul. (*A. 76, r.*)

45. — Our body is beneath the heavens, and the heavens are beneath the spirit. (*T. 34, v.*)

46. — The senses are earthly, and reason stands apart from them when it contemplates. (*T. 32, r.*)

<summary>Leonardo describes the body as a military hierarchy where the soul and "common sense" command the muscles and nerves, while asserting that the soul remains uncorrupted by the body's physical decay.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, soul, common sense, muscles, nerves, reason, military metaphor, physiology</keywords>

47. — Every evil leaves behind displeasure in the memory, except for the supreme evil, which is death; for death destroys memory along with life. (*ASH. I. 33, r.*) <note>Leonardo suggests that while we suffer from the memory of past pains, death is the only "evil" that provides a total release by erasing the mind itself.</note>

48. — One will always see upon the earth animals who fight amongst themselves, with the greatest injuries and often death for each party.

Their malice has no bounds; their savage arms cast down the greatest trees of the world's forests; and to satisfy their hunger, the fuel of their desires, they will unleash death, suffering, pain, wars, and devastation upon every living thing. In their prodigious pride, they would rise up against heaven itself, if the heavy weight of their limbs did not keep them pinned to the earth. There is nothing, neither on the earth, nor beneath it, nor in the water, that is not pursued, disturbed, or ruined by them; they pass from one country to another, and the body of this brood becomes the sepulcher <note>A tomb or burial chamber.</note> and the passage for the bodies of all dead animals.

O world, why do you not open up to cast into the deepest pits of your abysses and gulfs—and no longer show to the light—a monster so cruel and so pitiless? (*C. A. 362.*) <note>Though he speaks of "animals," Leonardo is delivering a scathing critique of humanity, describing the human race as a "monster" that consumes all other life and destroys the natural world.</note>

<summary>Leonardo reflects on death as the ultimate end of memory and provides a haunting condemnation of human cruelty and environmental destruction. He portrays humanity as a predatory force that turns its own body into a grave for the creatures it consumes.</summary>

<keywords>death, memory, human malice, war, environmental destruction, Codex Atlanticus, Ashburnham, philosophy of nature</keywords>

the opening of your mouth, which has served as a tomb for so many other animals.

I would say more, if I were permitted to expose the truth in its entirety. But we do not stray from human affairs when we call that a supreme wickedness which is not observed among the land animals; for there are none to be found who eat their own species, except through a lack of instinct. This only happens among predatory animals, such as the lion species, leopards, panthers, wolves, cats, and the like, who sometimes devour their young.

But you, besides the children, you eat the father, the mother, the brothers, the friends; and even this does not suffice. You go hunting in distant islands, capturing other men, and having castrated them, you fatten them up and kill them for your gluttony. Does nature then not produce enough vegetation to satisfy you? Can you not, by mixing them, create composite dishes, as <term>Platina</term> <note>Bartolomeo Sacchi, known as Platina (1421–1481), author of the first printed cookbook, "On Right Pleasure and Good Health."</note> and other authors of gastronomy have written? (*R. 844.*) <note>Reference to the Richter edition of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

If you understand that old age is nourished by wisdom, you will strive in your younger years so that your later ones do not lack for food. (*C. A. 112, r.*)

64. — Cornelius Celsus <note>A 1st-century Roman encyclopedist known primarily for his medical treatises, though Leonardo here quotes his philosophical views.</note>: “The sovereign good is wisdom; the sovereign evil is bodily pain.” <note>Translated from the Latin sentiment attributed to Celsus.</note> Now, we are composed of two elements: soul and body, of which the soul is the better and the body the lesser.

Wisdom is born from the better element and the sovereign evil proceeds from the lesser. The best thing of the soul is wisdom, just as the worst of the body is pain. Therefore, if the supreme evil is physical pain, the wisdom of the soul is the sovereign good for the conscious man: there is nothing that can be compared to it. (*T. 3, r.*) <note>T. refers to the Codex Trivulzianus, a notebook Leonardo used to record lists of words and philosophical thoughts.</note>

66. — The fame of the rich man ends with his life; one remembers the treasure but not the hoarder <note>original: "thésauriseur"</note>: and the glory of the virtue of mortals is quite different from that of their treasures.

How many emperors and princes have passed away, of whom no memory remains. They sought only states and riches, in order to leave a legacy.

How many, on the contrary, lived poor in money <note>original: "deniers," a reference to small silver coins or general currency</note> to acquire virtues: and the desire of the virtuous man was fulfilled differently than that of the rich man, insofar as virtue surpasses wealth.

Do you not see that treasure does not honor its accumulator after his life, as science <note>In this context, "science" refers to knowledge or learned skill</note> does, which always bears witness to and proclaims its creator, because she is the daughter of him who generated her, and not a step-daughter <note>original: "filiâtre"</note> like money <note>original: "pécune"</note>. *(LU. 65.)*

67. — Demetrius <note>Likely referring to Demetrius of Phalerum or the Cynic philosopher Demetrius, known for his biting wit</note> was accustomed to saying that there is no difference between the words and the voice of ignorant fools and the sounds and noises of the belly that come from too much gas. He did not speak thus without reason, for he did not think it necessary to make a distinction regarding the place from which the voice issued, nor to inquire whether it came from the lower parts or from the mouth, for both are equivalent in value and substance, in certain people. *(T. 41, v.)*

74. — When the work satisfies the judgment, what a sad sign for that judgment; and when the work surpasses the judgment, that is worse, as happens to those who marvel at having worked so well. When the judgment surpasses the work, that is the perfect sign; if a youth finds himself in this disposition, without doubt he will become an excellent artist; his works will be few in number, but full of qualities that will stop men in admiration to contemplate these perfections. (*C. A. 90, r.*) <note>Leonardo argues that a true artist's critical standards should always be higher than their current technical ability, ensuring constant growth.</note>

79. — Wood feeds the fire that consumes it.

When the sun appears, which dies in the darkness, you extinguish the lamp that hid it from your necessity and convenience. *(ASH. I. 22, v.)* <note>ASH refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, part of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

80. — Fame rises to heaven, because virtuous things are friends of God; infamy must be depicted below, because all its operations are contrary to God and are directed toward hell. *(ASH. II. 22, v.)*

81. — Pleasure and pain can be represented together and coupled; because one is never separated from the other. They are shown back-to-back because they are contrary to one another, yet placed on the same body because they have the same foundation; for if the foundation of pleasure is the struggle against displeasure, the latter is found at the bottom of varied and lewd joys. One imagines that the reed in the hand of pleasure is vain and without strength, yet its sting is nonetheless poisonous. In Tuscany, we use the reed to support beds; this signifies that there, vain dreams are made, and there a great part of life is consumed and much useful time is lost, especially in the morning when the body can provide a new effort, and where the spirit is moderate and rested; there again many vain pleasures are taken, there again the spirit imagines impossible things; and with the pleasures of the body which are reasons for...

<note>Leonardo often used allegorical drawings to accompany these texts; here he describes a figure representing the duality of human emotion.</note>

<summary>Leonardo reflects on the moral geography of fame and infamy before providing an allegorical analysis of the inseparable relationship between pleasure and pain, specifically criticizing sloth and morning idleness.</summary>

**Description of the Illustration:**
A detailed line drawing of a four-armed, two-headed figure standing upon a flat pedestal. One face is a bearded male, while the other is a female. The various arms hold symbolic objects described in the accompanying treatises: a long flowering staff, a leafy branch (perhaps the "reed" mentioned in the text), and a cluster of crystalline minerals. The figure is surrounded by several "A" and "c" shaped symbols, which likely serve as markers for a detailed anatomical or philosophical legend that is no longer present or is obscured by the page's condition. Faint, mirrored text from the reverse side of the manuscript is visible across the entire composition.

82. — If you say that vision hinders the application of subtle mental contemplation <note>original: "cogitation mentale"</note> that penetrates the divine sciences, and that this disadvantage led a philosopher to deprive himself of his sight; I will answer that the eye, as master of the senses, performs its duty by opposing the confused and lying conceptions which are not sciences, but rambling diversions where one argues with loud shouts and grand gestures. This philosopher should have deprived himself of his hearing as well, otherwise he remained offended, since he desired a harmony where all the senses are silent. <note>Leonardo is likely referring to the legend of Democritus, the ancient Greek philosopher who was said to have blinded himself to avoid distraction from his thoughts. Leonardo, the ultimate observer, strongly disagrees with this rejection of the physical world.</note>

And if such a philosopher closed his eyes to reason better, consider that this act was a consequence of his brain and his reasonings, so that everything might make way for them. Now, could he not close his eyes whenever he entered this frenzy and keep them shut until this fury subsided? But the man was a fool and so was his reasoning, and he stupidly blinded himself. (*LU. 16.*)

(*C. A. 59, r.*) <note>This reflects Leonardo's belief that the soul is not a prisoner of the body, but rather its partner, requiring physical form to experience and engage with the world.</note>

...unite and become nothing more than one and the same object.

The work is the first thing born of this union. If the thing loved is base, the lover becomes base. When the thing united is suited to its uniter, the result is delight, pleasure, and serenity. O monstrous element! How much better it would be for man if you returned to hell; because of you, great forests are left deserted of...

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s observations on the fashion of his time, noting the evolution of styles from his childhood to his later years, followed by a biting critique of the medical profession.</meta>

headdresses were cut in this way; silks of various colors emerged from the main seams of the garment.

Since then, I have seen shoes, berets, <term>pouches</term><note>original: "escarcelles," a large purse or pouch worn at the belt, common in the 15th and 16th centuries.</note>, even weapons worn for attack, the collars of coats, the points of jackets, and the tails of garments, in an infinite effort to mock those who wished to appear handsome, fitted with long and fine points.

Later, they began to enlarge the sleeves, and they became so large that a single one was wider than the jacket itself; then they raised the collar so much that the entire head disappeared into it; then again, they came to dress in such a way that the fabrics could not be supported by the shoulders because they did not rest upon them. Afterward, clothes were lengthened so that men had their arms laden with fabric to avoid trampling it underfoot. Finally, they reached such an excess that they dressed only on one side up to the elbow, and so tightly that they suffered great torment—many even died under the strain; and the feet were so squeezed that the toes passed one over the other and overlapped. (*F. 96. v.*) <note>Refers to Manuscript F in the collection of the Institut de France. Leonardo’s interest in clothing was both aesthetic and ergonomic; he often designed costumes for court festivals.</note>

# IV

## AGAINST HUMANISM <note>original: "Contre l’humanisme". These reflections serve as a defense of Leonardo’s empirical methods against the scholars of the Renaissance who prioritized ancient texts over direct observation.</note>

*Experience, the supreme authority (132). — Against the abbreviators (133-135). — Imitable and inimitable sciences and the incommunicability of painting (142).*

**127.** — Words that do not satisfy the ear of the listener cause him boredom and disgust; and this becomes manifest when the listeners are seized by great yawns. You, who speak to men whose goodwill you desire, when you see these manifestations of weariness, shorten your discourse and remain reasonably silent.

If you do otherwise, instead of the grace you seek, you will obtain only aversion and enmity.

And if you want to know what someone is—

---

<margin>1. These reflections, which end here with an apologetic fragment on the excellence of painting, respond to the attacks of which Leonardo was frequently the object on the part of the men of letters and humanists who saw nothing equal to their own erudition and rhetoric.</margin>

<note>The text ends mid-sentence as Leonardo begins to describe how to judge a person's character or interests through their reactions.</note>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci offers practical advice on public speaking and social awareness, warning that overstaying one's welcome in conversation leads to resentment rather than influence.</summary>

<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

delights, without hearing him speak, speak to him by putting forward several arguments and as soon as you see him become attentive, without yawning or frowning, or other varied expressions, be certain that the matter at hand is the one that pleases him. (*G.* 49, *r.*) <note>Leonardo suggests a method of "social testing" to identify a listener's interests through their body language.</note>

**128.** — *Syllogism*: doubtful speech <note>original: "parler douteux". Leonardo often viewed formal logic with suspicion if it was not grounded in physical experience.</note>. *Sophism*: confused speech, the false for the true. *Theory*: entirely practical science. (*F.* 96, *v.*) <note>Leonardo's definition of "theory" as "practical science" is unique; for him, theory was the "captain" and practice the "soldiers," but they were inseparable.</note>

**129.** — I know that many will say this is a useless work, and words that fall into the category of those which Demetrius <note>Demetrius of Phalerum, a Greek orator. Leonardo likely encountered this anecdote in a contemporary humanist text.</note> claimed should be no more accounted for than the wind that formed the words in their mouths, or that other wind which comes from the parts below.

Men who desire only material riches and pleasures are entirely deprived of the wealth of wisdom, the only certain nourishment and comfort of the soul. For if the soul is more worthy than the body, the wealth of the soul outweighs that of the body.

And often when I see one of those men take a work in hand, I wonder if, like a monkey, he is not going to bring it to his nose and ask if it is something to eat. (*C. A.* 119, *r.*) <note>C.A. refers to the *Codex Atlanticus*, the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings.</note>

**130.** — Good learning <note>original: "Les bonnes lettres". This refers to the *studia humanitatis* or liberal arts.</note> is born of a good natural disposition; and as the cause is more worthy than the effect, a good natural disposition without learning is worth more than a learned man without natural talent. (*C. A.* 76, *r.*)

<vocab>Syllogism: A form of reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises. Sophism: A fallacious argument used to deceive. Theory: Here, the underlying principles of a craft. Demetrius: An ancient Greek statesman and orator. Codex Atlanticus: Leonardo's massive bound set of drawings and notes. Wisdom: Intellectual and spiritual depth. Wealth of the soul: Internal character and knowledge. Good learning: Classical education and literacy. Natural disposition: Inherent talent or character.</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo defines logical terms with a skeptical eye and argues that innate talent and the "wealth of the soul" are far superior to formal education or material greed. He mocks critics who cannot appreciate intellectual work, comparing their lack of discernment to that of animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s spirited defense of his intellectual methods, specifically contrasting the "inventor" who learns from nature with the "reciter" who merely repeats classical texts.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

Marius to the Roman patricians: "Those who pride themselves on the efforts of others do not wish to allow me my own." <note>original: "C. A. 119, r." refers to the Codex Atlanticus, the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings.</note>

132. — They will say that, because I lack a formal education, I cannot properly express what I wish to say. However, they are unaware that my works are the product of experience rather than the words of others; and experience was the mistress of those who wrote well; and I too take her as my mistress and in every case I shall appeal to her. (*C. A. 117, r.*)

If, unlike them, I do not cite authors, my citation is higher and more worthy: experience, the mistress of their masters. They go about puffed up and pompous, clothed and adorned not in their own labors but in those of others, and they deny me my own and despise me—the inventor—though I am so superior to them, who are mere trumpeters and declaimers, reciters of the works of others and otherwise contemptible.

And thus should they be judged and not otherwise esteemed: the men who are inventors, the interpreters between nature and humanity, in comparison to the reciters and declaimers of the works of others. Between them there is the same difference as between an object outside of a mirror and the reflection of said object. The first exists in reality and by itself; the second has no reality. (*C. A. 117, r.*)

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<page-num>77</page-num>

...the major part of what composes the whole?

Impatience, the mother of folly <note>French: *sottise*</note>, admires brevity—as if such people did not have enough time in their lives to reach a full understanding of a single object, such as the human body. And yet, they then wish to embrace the divine intelligence in which the universe is contained, weighing it and diminishing it into infinite parts as if they wished to anatomize it <note>Leonardo frequently used the word "anatomize" to describe the scientific dissection and systematic study of a subject.</note>. (*R. 1210.*)

134. — O human folly! Do you not realize that you have lived with yourself all your life, and you still do not have knowledge of what you possess in abundance—namely, your own madness? And then you, along with the crowd of sophists <note>Sophists were thinkers who used clever but fallacious arguments; Leonardo often contrasted them with true scientists.</note>, deceive yourselves and others, despising the mathematical sciences—which contain the truth regarding things within their domain. And then you slip into the realm of miracles, writing about knowledge that the human spirit is not capable of grasping and which cannot be demonstrated by any natural example. And it seems to you that you have performed a miracle when you have falsified the work of a speculative mind. You do not see that you fall into the same error as one who strips a plant of the ornament of its leafy branches, along with its fragrant flowers and fruits.

Thus did Justin, the abbreviator of the histories written by Trogue Pompée <note>Marcus Junianus Justinus (Justin) was a Roman historian who famously condensed the vast "Philippic Histories" of Pompeius Trogus into a shorter collection.</note>, who relates with skill all the excellent deeds of the ancients, which were full of admirable details; he composed a

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci criticizes the "impatience" of scholars who condense complex subjects into shallow summaries, arguing that such abbreviations destroy the essential beauty and truth found in detailed study. He specifically defends mathematics and observation against those who prefer to rely on "miracles" or intellectual shortcuts.</summary>

These people are the brethren of beasts; in their procession are dogs and other animals full of rapine, and they keep company together, always running straight ahead, following the innocent animals who, in the time of great snows, are led by hunger to houses to beg for alms, as if from their guardian. A race <note>French: *engeance*—often used disparagingly to describe a "brood" or "breed" of people.</note> ungrateful to nature, because they are only clothed in an accidental appearance, without which they could be confused with the herd of beasts. **135.** — The abbreviators of works do injury to knowledge and to love. What is the value of one who abbreviates the parts? (*R. 1210.*)

**136.** — Scattered things will complete themselves and will thus take on such power <note>French: *vertu*—in this context, it refers to an intrinsic power or efficacy.</note> that they will restore lost memory to men; I mean the papers that are made of separate parchments and carry the account of human things and deeds. (*I. 65. v.*) <note>Refers to the manuscripts held at the Institut de France.</note>

**137.** — Happy are those who will lend an ear to the word of the dead: to read good works and put them into practice. (*I. 64. r.*)

**138.** — Bodies without souls <note>Leonardo is likely referring to books or written manuscripts here, which lack a "soul" or living breath but contain the essence of the author's mind.</note> will give, through their maxims <note>French: *sentences*—judgments or moral observations.</note>, useful precepts for dying well. (*C. A. 362, r.*) <note>Refers to the Codex Atlanticus.</note>

**139.** — Feathers will lift men toward the heavens, just like birds: — by means of letters written with pens. <note>French: *plumes*—Leonardo plays on the double meaning of the word, which signifies both a bird's "feathers" and the "quills" used for writing.</note> (*I. 64, v.*)

**141.** — Nothing more can be written by seeking out the new. <note>Likely suggesting that the fundamental truths of nature have already been observed, or perhaps a comment on the cyclical nature of human thought.</note> (*T. 14, r.*)

What is this thing that is so greatly desired by men, yet cannot be known once it is possessed? Sleep. (*S. 228.*)

**142.** — The *imitable* sciences are those in which the disciple can make himself equal to the author and likewise bear his own fruit. These are useful to the imitator, but they do not equal in excellence those others which cannot be bequeathed as an inheritance, like primary matter.

Among these inimitable sciences, painting holds the first place; it cannot be taught to one whom nature has not gifted, unlike mathematics, where the student receives exactly as much as the master teaches...

Forms cannot be copied in the way letters are, where the copy is worth as much as the original; painting cannot be cast like sculpture, where the mold reproduces the original. As for the power of the work: it cannot be reproduced in countless copies like printed books; it remains noble, honoring its author, always precious and uni- <note>The text breaks here; the word is likely *unique*.</note>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, manuscripts, painting, mathematics, sculpture, printed books, imitable, inimitable, sleep</vocab>

and does not produce daughters who are her equal. And this uniqueness makes her more excellent than those things which are published for everyone. <note>The author refers to painting as a "noble science" that cannot be copied by others in the way text can be reprinted.</note>

Do we not see the great kings of the East go about veiled and with their faces covered, believing that they would diminish their prestige by making their presence public and showing themselves? Furthermore, do we not see the paintings representing the divine Deities <term>Deities: here referring to holy figures or saints</term> being kept covered with curtains of very great price? They are only uncovered during the great solemnities of the Church amidst songs and music; and as soon as they are revealed, the great multitude of people who have gathered immediately throw themselves to the ground to adore and pray, for such paintings are believed to restore lost health and grant eternal salvation, just as if that deity were living and present.

This happens in no other science and for no other human work. And if you claim that it is not the power of the painter that acts, but the idea attributed to the thing represented, I will tell you that in such a case the human imagination could be satisfied by remaining in bed, instead of going to arduous and perilous places, as we see people do for pilgrimages.

If, nevertheless, these pilgrimages take place continually, who decides them, without necessity? Certainly you will confess that this image <note>original: "simulacre"</note> achieves something impossible for writing, by representing the effigie <note>original: "effigie"; a likeness or representation of a person</note>

# V
## SCIENCE
->BEGINNING OF A DISCOURSE ON THE EXPERIMENTAL<-
->METHOD<-

143. — We call science that mental discourse <note>original: "discours mental"; Leonardo refers to the internal logical reasoning process.</note> which takes its origin from the ultimate principles, beyond which nothing more can be found that forms part of the said science.

Geometry, for example, which studies continuous quantity <term>continuous quantity: a mathematical term for things that can be measured without gaps, like lengths or surfaces</term>, beginning with the surface of bodies, finds its origin in the line, which is the limit of the surface. And we are not satisfied with this, because we know that the line has its limit in the point, and that the point is the smallest thing we can conceive.

Therefore, the point is the first principle of geometry, and there is nothing, neither in nature nor in the human mind, that can be the principle of the point.

If you say that in the contact established upon a surface by the supreme sharpness of the tip of a stylus <note>original: "poinçon"; a sharp tool used for engraving or marking</note>, you discover the creation of the point:

<summary>Leonardo defines science as a logical progression from fundamental principles, using geometry to illustrate how complex forms are built from the point, the smallest possible concept.</summary>
<keywords>science, experimental method, geometry, continuous quantity, first principles, the point, logic</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric argument from page 82 regarding the nature of the point and transitions into Leonardo's famous definition of the scientific method based on experience.</meta>

this is not true at all: — this contact is a surface that surrounds its center, and this center is the residence of the point. This point is not made of the matter of that surface, nor is he, nor any point in the universe. They exist in potential even when they are united — or rather, they have the potential to unite — but they do not comprise any part of a surface. Suppose you imagine a whole composed of a thousand points which divide some part of this quantity into a thousand; one could well say that this part will be equal to its whole; and this is proven by the zero or nothing, the tenth figure of arithmetic which is traced as a 0 to express nothingness. But this 0 placed after the unit will make it say ten, and if one puts two units <note>The text likely refers to adding a second zero to the unit (100).</note> they would read as a hundred, and thus the number will grow indefinitely by tens wherever it is added; and yet it, in itself, is worth no more than nothing, and all the nothings of the universe are equal to a single nothing, as to their substance and their existence. (*LU. 1.*) <note>LU. 1 refers to the Libro di Pittura (Book of Painting), the primary source of Leonardo's theoretical writings.</note>

### BY LEONARDO DA VINCI

...always where reason is lacking, dissertation <note>The original French *dissertation* here refers to empty verbal debate or speculative discourse lacking empirical proof.</note> takes its place, which does not happen with certainties. We shall say, therefore, that wherever there is arguing, there is no true science: for truth has but a single conclusion, and once this conclusion is found, litigation is destroyed forever. If it can be reborn, it is a talkative and confused science, and not born of certainty.

True sciences are those which experience has caused to penetrate through the senses and which impose silence on the tongues of arguers; they do not nourish their investigators with dreams, but proceed successively upon the first and true known principles and, with a true sequence, arrive at a conclusion, as we see in mathematics. (*LU. 33.*) <note>References like *LU.* and *I.* refer to specific codices or manuscripts, such as the *Libro di Pittura*.</note>
