does not accord with theory: and such an agreement is impossible.

This stems from the poles <note>The "poles" here refer to the fulcrum or pivot points of a weighing scale</note> of the balance, by means of which the science of gravity <term>pesanteur: the physical quality of weight or heaviness</term> is formed. These poles, according to the ancient philosophers, were poles placed according to the nature of the mathematical line; and there are no mathematical points anywhere, for these points and lines are incorporeal <note>Incorporeal: lacking physical form or substance</note>.

Practice treats them as corporeal, because necessity demands it, seeking to support the weight of the scales along with their own weights.

I have found the ancients to be in error regarding this judgment of weight, and their error comes largely from their science; they used, for the most part, mathematical or abstract points—that is to say, incorporeal ones. (*C. A.* 93, *v.*)

Sight, through rapid operations, discovers an infinity of forms at a single point; nevertheless, it understands only one thing at a time. Consider this case: you, reader, will see this entire written page at a single glance and will immediately judge that it is full of various letters, but you will not know at that same moment what these letters are, nor what they mean. You must go from one word to another, and line by line, if you wish to gain knowledge of these letters—just as to reach the top of a building you must climb step by step; otherwise, you would never reach the summit.

I tell you then: he whom nature turns toward this art, if he wishes to have a true knowledge of the forms of things, shall begin with their details, and shall not move to the second before having the first in his memory and in his practice; doing otherwise, he would waste his time and lengthen his studies considerably. Remember that diligence must come before speed <note>original: "prestesse" — quickness or facility of execution</note>. (*ASH. I. 28, r.*) <note>ASH. refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, part of Leonardo's notebooks held in France.</note>

163. — There is no effect in <unclear>nature</unclear> without a cause: grasp the cause and <unclear>do not</unclear> worry about the experiment <note>original: "expérience" — in this context, Leonardo suggests that if one understands the underlying law of nature (the cause), the experimental result is already certain.</note>. (*C. A. 147, v.*) <note>C. A. refers to the Codex Atlanticus, the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings.</note>

164. — Before conducting any experiment, and before I proceed further, my intention is to first present the experiment and then to demonstrate through reasoning why this experiment is bound to such a mode of ope- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "opération" (operation or execution).</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that true understanding requires a step-by-step approach, comparing reading a page to the gradual mastery of art and science. He emphasizes that understanding the "cause" of a natural phenomenon is more fundamental than the experiment itself.</summary>

...ration. And this is the true rule followed by observers of natural effects; and although nature proceeds from reason and ends with experience, we must follow the opposite path: for, by proceeding from experience, as I have just stated, we shall discover the cause through it. (*E. 55, r.*) <note>E. refers to Manuscript E, one of Leonardo’s notebooks held at the Institut de France.</note>

Science is the study of things that are possible, whether present or past. Prescience <note>In this context, "prescience" refers to the theoretical study or foresight of potential outcomes.</note> is the study of things that could be. (*C. A. 86, r.*) <note>C. A. refers to the Codex Atlanticus.</note>

165. — The smallest natural point is larger than all mathematical points. This can be proven. The natural point is a continuous quantity, and everything that is continuous is infinitely divisible; whereas the mathematical point is indivisible, having no quantity.

Intellectually, every continuous quantity is infinitely divisible.

Among the greatness of the things within us, the existence of nothingness <note>original: "le rien"</note> is the principal, and its office extends to things that have no being; its essence is found near time, between the past and the future: nothingness proceeds from the present.

The void <note>original: "le néant"</note> has i<unclear>ts</unclear> parts equal to its whole, and the whole is divisib<unclear>l</unclear>e or indivisible, and the sum produces no part, as in multiplication or in its total regarding subtraction—as arithmeticians demonstrate by the tenth character, the 0, which represents nothingness. Its power does not extend to natural things. <note>Leonardo is observing that while the concept of "zero" or "nothing" is essential for mathematics (the "tenth character"), it does not exist as a physical "thing" in the natural, material world.</note>

<page-num>91</page-num>
# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

---

What we call <term>nothingness</term> <note>original: "néant"</note> exists only in time and in words: in time, it exists between the past and the future, retaining nothing of the present; it also exists among words, referring to things that do not exist or are impossible. Nothingness is the opposite of being, and where it might exist, there would be a <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vide"</note>. (*ASH. II. 27, v. — R. 918.*)

166. — That which is <term>divisible</term> in fact is also divisible in potential, but not all quantities that are divisible in potential are divisible in fact. (*C. A. 119, r.*)

167. — These rules (of <term>experience</term>) <note>Leonardo uses "experience" to mean empirical observation and scientific method</note> are the proper means to discern the true from the false. They persuade men to promise themselves only possible things and to act with greater moderation; thus you reveal the ignorance that would otherwise cause you to fall into discouragement and melancholy when you see no result. (*C. A. 119, r.*)

168. — What is <term>force</term>? I say that it is a <term>spiritual virtue</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "spiritual virtue" referred to an immaterial, active power rather than a religious quality</note>, an invisible power, which, under the accidental condition of an external violence, is caused by <term>motion</term>, and is placed and infused into bodies that are, by their nature, at rest: it gives them an active life of marvelous power. (*T. 36, v.*)

169. — What is <term>force</term>? A spiritual, incorporeal, invisible power which, with a brief life, appears in bodies that an accidental violence finds outside of their <term>natural inertia</term>. (*G. 75, r.*)

<summary>Leonardo defines metaphysical concepts like nothingness and the vacuum before transitioning into the physics of force, which he describes as an invisible "spiritual" power that activates resting matter. He also emphasizes the importance of empirical experience in avoiding the frustrations of ignorance.</summary>

<keywords>nothingness, vacuum, divisibility, scientific method, experience, force, spiritual virtue, motion, inertia, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

170.— This violence communicates to bodies an active life of a marvelous power: it constrains every created thing to change its form and position and runs with fury toward its own exhaustion, diversifying itself, in its course, according to various causes.

Slowness increases it and speed diminishes it. Born of violence, it dies of its liberty; and the greater it is, the more quickly it dies and the more violently it destroys everything that opposes its own destruction. It seeks to conquer and abolish the obstacle and dies of its victory, succumbing to it itself.

Force increases through the resistance of the obstacle and charges with fury against everything that opposes its death.

Every constrained thing constrains in its turn.

The body in which it manifests increases neither in weight nor in volume; fatigue strengthens it and rest exhausts it.

The body in which it is incarnated loses its liberty, and often it engenders, through the effect of movement, a new force. (*A. 34, r.*) <note>Leonardo is describing the laws of dynamics and inertia; "violence" here refers to an external force acting upon an object at rest.</note>

171. — Force has three offices which are subdivided to infinity: pulling, pushing, and immobilizing. (*A. 34, v.*)

172. — Force is entirely and everywhere the same, and in all its parts. (*A. 34, v.*)

173. — All spiritual powers <note>original: "puissances spirituelles"; referring to immaterial physical forces like gravity or momentum</note>, as they move further from their source, occu-

If the elements revealed themselves to one another with the shapes given by Plato <note>Referring to the "Platonic solids" from the *Timaeus*, where Plato assigned geometric shapes to the elements (e.g., the cube for earth, the tetrahedron for fire)</note>, a vacuum would be produced between them. 174. — Every action can only be exercised through movement. (*G. 73, r.*)

176. — Weight, force, and accidental movement, as well as percussion <note>the impact of one body against another</note>, are the four potential accidents <note>In the philosophical language of Leonardo's time, an "accident" is a non-essential property or event that happens to a substance</note> through which all visible works of mortals have their being and their end. (*L. 68, r.*)

177. — Nothing without sensibility moves by itself. Its movement comes to it from elsewhere. (*A. 34, r.*)

178. — Every natural and continuous movement desires to maintain its course along the line of its origin, in whatever place it may be; even if it varies, it remains tied to its origin. (*F. 74, v.*)

179. — All movement tends to maintain itself; every body in motion continues to move as long as the impulse of the power of its motor is preserved within it. <note>This is a precursor to the modern law of inertia.</note> (*R. 859.*)

180. — Everything violently maintains its own existence. (*W. 13, r.*)

-rial and the granddaughter of spiritual movement. She is the mother and origin of weight. <note>original: "matériel... petite-fille du mouvement spirituel." Leonardo often personified physical forces to describe their relationships and hierarchies.</note>

This weight is finite within the elements of earth and water, and this force is infinite. Through it, countless things are moved, which make themselves into instruments when they are able, or else which this force generates.

Force, along with material movement, and weight, along with percussion <note>the impact of one body against another</note>, are the four potential accidents <note>In Renaissance philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential property or event that happens to a substance, rather than its core essence.</note> from which all human operations derive their being and their death.

The force of spiritual movement originates from the movement that courses through the limbs of sentient animals and swells their muscles; these muscles, once swollen, act upon the nerves to which they are joined, and from this the strength of human limbs results.

The quality and quantity of a man's force can generate another force, proportional to it, but all the greater as the movement from one to the other is longer. *(E. 22, v.)*

183. — Violence <note>original: "La violence"; here Leonardo uses the term to mean "impetus" or "forced motion" rather than physical aggression</note> is a virtue <note>original: "vertu"; in a scientific context, this refers to a "power" or "inherent property"</note> born of movement and transmitted by the mover to its object, which travels as long as the violence lasts.

184. — Violence is the impulse of movement transmitted from the mover to the object.

Every impulse tends to perpetuate itself or desires its own continuity.

That every impulse desires to continue itself is proven by the impression made by the sun upon the eye of the beholder, and in the impression of the sound occasioned by the hammer of the bell-striker. <note>Leonardo is comparing physical momentum to the "after-image" in the eye or the "reverberation" of a sound.</note>

Every impulse desires to last, as is shown by the violence of movement upon the moved object. (*G. 5, v.*)

185. — Water of itself has no stillness, nor movement, unless it descends.

Water by itself does not stop, unless it is contained. (*F. 30, v.*)

186. — The water that moves in the river is either drawn, or it is pushed, or it moves of its own accord.

If it is drawn—I mean led—who leads it?

If it is pushed, who repels it?

187. — No human investigation can call itself true science if it does not pass through mathematical demonstration!

190. — No certainty exists where one cannot apply a branch of the mathematical sciences, or which is not connected with the said sciences. (*G. 96.*) <note>G. refers to Manuscript G, one of Leonardo's notebooks held at the Institut de France.</note>

191. — Every body that nourishes itself goes from continual death to continual rebirth; nourishment can only enter through certain pathways when the previous nourishment is consumed, and when it is consumed there is no more life; and if you do not return a nourishment equal to that already granted, life will diminish; if you suppress it, life will be destroyed. But if you return as much as is destroyed in a day, then life is reborn instead of being consumed, in the manner of the light of a candle nourished by the moisture <note>original: "l’humeur"; here referring to the liquid wax or tallow that feeds the flame</note> which feeds the flame. This light, in its very rapid course, is nourished by the tallow until it dies at its consumption; and the splendid clarity ends in black smoke, and its death lasts as long as that smoke lasts, and the continuity of this smoke equals the continuity of its food, and in a moment the light dies as soon as the movement of its nutrition ceases. (*ASH. I. 89, r.*) <note>ASH. refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts.</note>

192. — Heat is the cause of the movement of moisture, and cold of its immobility, as is seen in the cold region which stops the clouds in the air.

205. — Before turning a specific case into a general rule, one must repeat the experiment two or three times, observing whether the same effects occur each time. (*Manuscript L, folio 72, recto*)

206. — If one lets fall several bodies of equal weight, one after the other, at equal intervals of time, the differences in their distances will be equal (1).

The experiment for this theorem should be conducted in this manner, namely: taking two chestnuts of equal weight and appearance, if one lets them fall from a great height such that, at the start of their motion, they touch each other; the experimenter will be at the bottom to observe if their fall has kept them in contact or not.

This experiment shall be repeated, so that no accident prevents or distorts the proof and so that, if the experiment were false, it does not deceive the one who observes it. (*Manuscript M, folio 57, recto*)

207. — There is a part of astronomy <note>original: "l’astrologie"; in the Renaissance, this term was often used interchangeably with astronomy. 208. — Some commentators blame the ancient inventors from whom grammars and sciences were born, and launch a full-scale attack against the inventors of old because their own laziness has prevented them from finding anything; they write so many pages only to oppose their masters with false arguments.

210. — Sometimes I shall conclude from effects to causes, and at other times from causes to effects, adding to my conclusions certain truths which, although not included within them, may nevertheless be deduced therefrom. One must not blame those who, in the methodical pursuit of scientific development, invoke general rules derived from a previously established conclusion. <note>Codex Atlanticus 200, r.</note>

211. — The other proof that Plato gave to those of Delos <note>A reference to the "Delian Problem" or doubling the cube; Leonardo distinguishes here between physical construction and pure intellectual proof.</note> is not geometric, because it proceeds by means of instruments, rulers, and compasses, and because experience demonstrates it to us; but this one is entirely mental, and consequently geometric. <note>Manuscript F. 59, v.</note>

212. — The treatise on <term>mechanical science</term> <note>The study of forces and the laws governing motion.</note> must precede the treatise on useful inventions. <note>Manuscript G. 7.</note>

213. — <term>Instrumental science</term> <note>The application of mechanics through tools or machines.</note> or mechanics is the noblest and rises above all others through its utility. It explains how all animated bodies possessing motion perform their operations.

Their movements arise from their <term>center of gravity</term> <note>The point at which the weight of a body is balanced in all directions.</note> which is placed at the center of equivalent weights, and from this point originates the extension or retraction, the release or the contractility of the muscles. <note>Manuscript G. 7.</note>

214. — I shall treat such a subject. But before all else, I will perform some experiments, because I wish to present the experience first. I will demonstrate

## VI

# NATURE

**215.** — Naturally, every thing desires to maintain itself in its own essence. <note>Manuscript Ashburnham II, folio 4, recto.</note>

**216.** — In the universe, everything strives to preserve itself in its own proper mode. A flowing stream of water seeks to maintain its path, according to the power of its cause, and finds itself constrained, by opposition, to complete the length of the path it has begun through a circular and twisting motion. <note>Manuscript A, folio 60, recto.</note>

**217.** — When one thing, being the cause of another, produces no effect through its own movement, the movement of the effect must necessarily follow the movement of the cause. <note>Codex Atlanticus, folio 169, verso.</note>

**218.** — Many times, a single thing is subject to two forces: <term>necessity</term> <note>In Leonardo's physics, "necessity" refers to the immutable laws that govern the behavior of matter</note> and <term>power</term> <note>Referring to an active, often external, energy or force</note>. Water falls and the earth absorbs it out of a need for moisture; the sun evaporates it, not out of need, but through power. <note>Manuscript T, folio 39, verso.</note>

**219.** — All elements removed from their place...

without knowing why—bending my back into an arch, and passing my tired hand over my eyes, with my right hand I create darkness for myself by lowering and closing my eyelids.

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

Nature, being a woman and taking pleasure in continually creating lives and forms, know that these are the expansions of her earthly matter; she is more willing and hurried to create than Time, which was placed there to consume. This is why so many animals serve as food for one another; and to satisfy a similar desire, she sends forth pestilential vapors <note>likely referring to plagues or miasmas</note> upon the great multiplications and congregations of animals, and especially upon men who increase too much, so that other animals do not eat them; and once the cause is removed, the effects no longer occur.

Therefore, this earth desires continuous multiplication and strives not to fail in its need for life. This demonstrates the reason why effects hide their cause; animals are the example of the world's life. (*R. 1219.*) <note>Reference to the Richter edition of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

231. — In a dead thing, there remains a disaggregated life which, when absorbed by the stomachs of the living, becomes once again sensitive and intellective life. (*E. 48, r.*)

232. — Whatever it feeds upon, the body goes continually from death to rebirth; the food enters those places where the past food has died; and if you do not provide a nourishment equivalent to that which was received, life diminishes and is subsequently extinguished. (*H. 89, v.*)

233. — Nature has placed, in the move—

<summary>Leonardo describes Nature as a creative force engaged in a constant struggle with Time, using death and even plagues as tools to balance the overpopulation of the living. He views the consumption of food as a process where "dead" matter is reincarnated into sensitive and intellectual life.</summary>

—ment of man, all the necessary parts. If they are struck, man feels pain; and he feels it in the legs, the forehead, and the nose. 234. — If nature has placed pain in the vegetative soul <term>vegetative soul: a contemporary term for the basic biological functions of life, such as growth and nutrition</term> endowed with movement (animality) for the preservation of the instruments <note>referring to limbs or organs</note> that movement could diminish or injure, the vegetative soul without movement <note>plants</note> does not have to undergo the shock of objects against it; therefore pain is not necessary for plants, which are broken without feeling pain as animals do. (*H. 60, r.*)

235. — The works of nature are far more worthy than words, which are the work of man. Between human work and natural work, there is the same proportion as that of man to God. (*LU. 14.*)

236. — O Time, rapid plunderer of created things, how many kings, how many peoples you have scattered, and what mutations of state and what varied histories followed, since the marvelous form of this fish died in the cavernous and winding grotto. Now, undone by time, it lies in this deserted place, gaunt and naked, its bones serving as a frame and support for the mountain placed upon it. (*R. 954.*) <note>Leonardo is reflecting on a fossilized fish found in the mountains, recognizing it as evidence of ancient geological change.</note>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI

237. — The water that touches the river <note>original: "au fleuve," likely referring to the banks or the surface point of contact</note> is the last of that which is passing and the first of that which is coming: so it is with the present moment. (*T. 34, r.*) <note>T. 34 refers to one of Leonardo’s notebooks held at the Institut de France.</note>

238. — An atom, were it as swift as imagination or a glance, would surpass the height of the stars. Its journey would be infinite; for that which can diminish without end would become infinitely swift and travel an infinite path (for every continuous quantity is infinitely divisible). This opinion, condemned by reason, is consequently also condemned by experience.

Therefore, you observers, do not trust authors who have sought, with only their imagination, to set themselves up as interpreters between nature and man; but follow those who have exerted their efforts not on the signs of nature, but on the effects of her experiments. One must recognize how experience deceives those who know nature poorly. For many times it appears similar, and at many other times it shows great variety, as is demonstrated. (*I. 102, r. and v.*)

239. — Animals are the example of universal life. (*Br. M. 156, v.*) <note>Br. M. refers to the British Museum, now known as the Arundel Manuscript.</note>

239. — All the branches of trees, at every level of their height, when joined together, are equal in thickness to the main branch from which they sprout: the sap of the main branch divides just as the branches do. (*G. 34, v.*) <note>Leonardo is describing his "Rule of Trees," an early observation of botanical scaling and hydraulic architecture.</note>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, time, atom, imagination, experience, nature, animals, sap, trees</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo reflects on the fleeting nature of the present moment and argues for the supremacy of empirical experience over purely theoretical or "imaginary" science. He also notes a mathematical rule for the growth of tree branches based on the flow of sap.</summary>

<keywords>fluid dynamics, present moment, empiricism vs theory, infinite divisibility, universal life, botanical scaling, Leonardo's Rule</keywords>

240. — The sun gives spirit and life to the plants of the earth, which nourishes them with its moisture. I conducted an experiment by leaving a small root on a squash and nourishing it with water; it brought all its fruits to perfection, about sixty squashes of the long variety. I applied my mind with attention to such a life and I recognized that the night dew, penetrating abundantly through the attachment of the large leaves, nourished the plant along with its children or eggs <note>Leonardo uses "eggs" as a metaphor for the developing fruit</note>. Every branch and every fruit is born at the base of its leaves, which serves as its mother by carrying the rainwater and the dew to it. (G. 32, v.)

241. — The straight lines of plants curve and present their convexity toward the south; there, the branches are longer, larger, and thicker than in the north: the sun draws the sap toward the part of the plant that it faces. (G. 36, v.)

242. — The circles of cut branches <note>tree rings</note> indicate the number of their years and their thickness, showing whether the year was humid or dry. (LU. 829.)

243. — O powerful and already animated instrument of the artist Nature, your great strengths are not for your own use, but you must abandon a life of rest and obey the laws that God and time have given to generative nature. (R. 90.)

244. — The tree, wherever it is stripped of its bark, sees that nature—which has foreseen this—carries a greater quantity of sap to that wound; and at the damaged spot, a thicker bark grows than elsewhere. And the sap is so active that, joined with local aid, it rises for the benefit of the reaching branches, with various sproutings and swellings, like boiling water. (*Codex Atlanticus 76, r.*) <note>Leonardo describes the tree's "callus" or healing tissue as a conscious response by nature.</note>

245. — Nature has placed the leaves of the latest branches of many plants in such a way that the sixth leaf is under the first, and so on successively, if nothing opposes this rule. <note>This is an early observation of phyllotaxis, the mathematical arrangement of leaves on a stem.</note>

This was done for a double utility: first, the branch is the fruit of the following year, being born from the bud of the "eye" <note>term: eye; the axillary bud from which new growth emerges</note>, which is above and in contact with the attachment of the leaves; the water that bathes this branch can descend and nourish this bud and catch the drop in the concavity where the leaf is born; and the second advantage: in growing such branches the following year, one does not cover the other since they are born facing five different directions, and the sixth is born above the first, at a great distance. (*LU. 398.*)

246. — This nature, so seductive and so rich, varies so much that among trees of the same species, not one is found that resembles another; and not only the tree, but the branches, the leaves, and the fruits are...

<summary>Leonardo explains how trees heal their bark through the movement of sap and describes the spiral arrangement of leaves, noting how this pattern optimizes water collection and sunlight exposure.</summary>
<keywords>botany, sap, bark regeneration, phyllotaxis, leaf arrangement, buds, plant morphology, Codex Atlanticus</keywords>

247. — All things that appear in the spring are born under the snow and remain covered, appearing only in their season; this applies to deceit, which cannot remain hidden. (*I. 39, v.*) <note>Leonardo often used natural metaphors to describe human morality.</note>

248. — What drives you, O man, to abandon your city home, to leave your parents and friends, and to go off through mountains and valleys into rural places? If not the natural beauty of the universe, which, upon careful consideration, you enjoy only through sight.

If the poet wishes to argue with the painter because he does not capture such sites described by the poet—who stays at home without feeling the overwhelming heat of the sun—is it not more useful and less painful because one writes in the cool, without movement or the peril of illness?

But the soul cannot enjoy the benefit of the eyes, the windows of its habitation, nor receive the sight of beautiful places, nor contemplate the shadowy valley furrowed by the loops of winding rivers, nor the varied flowers whose colors form a visual harmony, and all the other things that the eye alone perceives. But if the painter, in the cold and sad season of winter, shows you these same landscapes and others where you have had your pleasures; if near some fountain you can see yourself again, a lover with your mistress, in the flowery meadow, under the sweet shade...

<note>This passage is part of Leonardo's "Paragone," a series of arguments asserting the superiority of painting over poetry and other arts because it appeals directly to the "noblest" sense: sight.</note>

<summary>Leonardo reflects on the inevitability of truth emerging like spring growth and argues for the superiority of painting in capturing the restorative beauty of nature.</summary>

251. — The practice of slitting the nostrils of horses is a ridiculous thing that deserves only laughter. If you say to me: "Why has nature given nostrils to the animal since the mouth suffices for breathing?" I will answer you that the nostrils are made to ensure breathing while the mouth is busy chewing food. *(C. A. 76, r.)* <note>Leonardo frequently criticized traditional practices that lacked a basis in anatomical understanding; he argues here that the horse's anatomy is already perfectly designed for its needs.</note>

## VII

## ASTRONOMY

252. — Authors claim that the stars possess their own light <note>original: "lumière propre," referring to intrinsic luminosity or the belief that stars shine like the sun rather than reflecting light like the moon.</note>, arguing that if it were otherwise for Venus and Mars, when they pass between our eye and the sun, they would obscure as much of the sun as if they were hiding it from our sight. This is false, for it is proven that a shadow placed in the midst of a light source is surrounded and covered by lateral rays, maintaining that light in such a way that the shadow remains invisible. <note>Leonardo is describing an optical phenomenon where a small object does not cast a visible silhouette against a massive, distant light source because light rays "wrap around" it.</note>

This is demonstrated when viewing the sun through the branches of leafless plants from a great distance; the branches do not appear to take up any part of the sun in our eyes.

The same happens with the aforementioned planets which, despite having no light of their own, do not steal any part of the sun from our sight.

Second proof. — They say that stars appear brighter at night the higher they are, and that if they did not have... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing on the following page.</note>

<vocab>Astronomy, stars, intrinsic light, Venus, Mars, sun, lateral rays, planets</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo disputes the contemporary belief that planets and stars generate their own light, using an optical experiment involving tree branches to explain why planets do not appear as dark spots when passing in front of the sun.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, optics, planetary light, Venus, Mars, solar observation, light rays, celestial bodies</keywords>

of their own light, the shadow made by the earth interposing itself between them and the sun, we would see them darken, we would no longer see them, and they would not be perceived by the terrestrial body. They have not considered that the pyramidal shadow <note>Leonardo uses "pyramidal shadow" to describe the cone-shaped shadow (umbra) cast by a spherical body like a planet or moon.</note> of the moon does not reach the stars it passes in excess; the pyramid diminishes according to the space occupied by the body of the star, and it remains illuminated by the sun.

253. — If you look at the stars while avoiding their flickering (as you will do by looking through a very small hole, made with the extreme point of a fine needle, and placing this hole almost touching the eye), you will see the stars so small that nothing could appear smaller: and truly the long distance weakens their radiance, even though many are infinitely larger than the earth and the water. Think of what our world would appear to be at such a distance. Consider then how many stars could be inserted in length and width between those you see, so sparsely scattered in the nocturnal space.

I cannot help but strongly blame those ancient authors who said that the sun is no larger than it appears.

Among those was Epicurus; he derived this opinion from a light source placed in our air and equidistant to the center: whoever sees it, never sees it any smaller at any distance. (*R. 860.*) <note>Reference to Jean Paul Richter's "The Literary Works of Leonardo da Vinci."</note>

...men as gods, such as Jupiter, Saturn, Mars and others, have committed a great error. Seeing also that man is great in relation to our world, which appears as a small star—that is to say, a point in the universe—and seeing also that men are mortal and putrid and corruptible in their graves, the "Sphere" and "Marullus" <note>Leonardo refers to Goro Dati’s *La Sfera*, a popular astronomical poem, and Michele Marullo, a Renaissance poet known for his *Hymns to Nature*.</note> with many others praise the sun. (*Folio 5, recto*)

254. — **Epicurus** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher (c. 341–270 BCE) who famously argued that the sun is exactly the size it appears to be to the human eye.</note> says that the sun has the size it appears to have: therefore, what appears to be one foot wide, it is as if we were holding it. It would follow that the moon, when it eclipses the sun, would not be as large as it is. Knowing that the moon is smaller [than the sun], it would be less than a foot, and consequently, when our world eclipses the moon, it would be less than the said foot.

Thus, the sun being one foot wide and our earth casting a pyramidal shadow upon the moon, it is necessary that the luminous cause of the shadow-pyramid <note>The sun</note> be larger than the opaque cause of this pyramid <note>The earth</note>. (*Folio 6, recto*)

255. — **Epicurus** perhaps sees the shadows of columns, reflected on the walls placed before them, as equal to the diameter of the column. Given that—

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s argument that the sun is inherently hot, using optical experiments with mirrors and water-filled spheres to prove that heat can be transmitted through cold mediums without the medium itself being the source.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>ON LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

---

The concave mirror being cold, yet receiving the rays of the fire, reflects more heat than the fire itself.
The glass ball, full of cold water, sends forth its rays, when near the fire, hotter than the fire itself. 

It follows from these two experiments that the rays coming from the concave mirror or the ball of cold water are hot by virtue <term>virtue: from the Latin *virtus*, meaning the inherent power, property, or functional essence of a thing</term> and not because the mirror and the ball are hot.

It is the same with the sun which, passing through bodies, heats them by means of its virtue. And it has been concluded that the sun was not hot—the sun whom this same experiment shows as a hearth of heat: and this is demonstrated by means of the mirror and the ball which, being cold, absorb the rays of the fire's heat and make them into hot rays because the primary cause is hot; it happens the same way for the sun which, being hot, while passing through cold mirrors reflects great heat. 

It is not the brilliance of the sun that heats, but its natural heat. *(G. 34, r ; F. 34, r.)* <note>G and F refer to the specific codices or notebooks in Leonardo’s manuscripts, such as those held at the Institut de France.</note>

259. — The solar rays pass through the cold region of the air and do not change their nature; they pass through glasses full of cold water and, for that matter, through whatever transparent place they pass, it is as if they were penetrating the air itself. *(F. 85, v.)*

260. — The earth is not in the middle of the cir- <note>Leonardo here begins a discussion on the position of the Earth, likely challenging the strictly geocentric view that the Earth is the absolute center of every celestial orbit.</note>

<vocab>concave mirror, glass ball, solar rays, natural heat, virtue, optics, Leonardo da Vinci</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo uses the principles of optics to argue that the sun is naturally hot, demonstrating that cold lenses or mirrors can concentrate heat from a hot source without being hot themselves. He further notes that solar rays maintain their nature even when passing through the cold upper atmosphere or water.</summary>
<keywords>optics, solar heat, concave mirrors, atmospheric temperature, physics of light, scientific method</keywords>

cle <note>This completes the word "circle" from the previous page.</note> of the sun, nor at the center of the world, but it is in the midst of its elements which accompany it and are united to it. 261. — The spots that one sees on the full moon never vary during the motion that the celestial body makes in our hemisphere. (*F. 85, r.*)

262. — If the moon is dense, it is heavy and cannot be supported by the space it occupies; it should therefore descend toward the center of the universe, unite with the earth, and, failing that, its waters should fall: this, which should naturally occur, does not happen. For the moon is clothed in its own elements—water, air, fire—and supports itself in space just as our earth does, with its elements, in that other space. And dense bodies do within their elements exactly what they do in ours. (*R. 902.*) <note>R. 902 refers to Jean Paul Richter's 1883 compilation of Leonardo's literary works.</note>

263. — *The moon*: 1st, Nothing very light is opaque.

2nd, Nothing lighter remains beneath that which is heavier.

3rd, Does the moon have a place <term>place: here referring to 'site' or a fixed physical position</term> in the midst of its elements or not?

If it does not have a particular place, like the earth within its elements, why does it not fall to the center of our system?

If the moon is not in the midst of its elements and does not descend, is it lighter than the other element?

And if the moon is lighter than the other element, why is it solid and not transparent? (*F. 41, v.*) <note>F. 41, v. refers to Manuscript F, folio 41 verso, held at the Institut de France.</note>

264. — The moon is dense and heavy. How does it maintain its position, the moon? (*R. 892.*) <note>R. 892 refers to the Richter collection of Leonardo’s writings.</note>

265. — Nothing dense is lighter than air.

We have proven that the part of the moon that shines is watery <term>watery: Leonardo theorized that the moon's reflective surface was composed of water or oceans</term>, and, facing the body of the sun, it reflects the splendor it receives; and as if this water were without waves, it appears small, but with a brilliance almost similar to that of the sun; now it is necessary to seek whether the moon is a heavy or light body; if it is heavy, let us admit that its own earth, at such a height, acquires a degree of lightness — now water is lighter than earth, and air than water, and fire than air, and so on successively; it appears that if the moon had density, as it does, it would have gravity; and having gravity, the space where it is located could not support it, and consequently it should descend toward the center of the universe and unite with the earth. And if at least its waters were to fall, and leave it, and flow toward the center, the moon would remain stripped and without luster. Therefore, not following evident reason, it is manifest that the moon is clothed in its own elements, namely: water, air, and fire, and in itself and by itself is supported in space, just as our earth does with its

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, moon, astronomy, density, gravity, elements, watery, reflection, sun, earth</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the moon must be a solid, dense body composed of its own elemental system (water, air, and fire) to explain why it remains suspended in space rather than falling to Earth.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, celestial mechanics, gravity, lunar composition, elements, Leonardo da Vinci, planetary motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's scientific inquiries into physics and cosmology, specifically addressing the nature of sound and the movement of celestial bodies.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
# SELECTED TEXTS

elements in another space, and that it is the function of heavy things within their own elements, just as heavy things act within our own elements. (*Folio 69, verso*)

266. — Every sound is caused by air reverberating within a dense body; and if it occurs between two heavy bodies, it is the ambient air surrounding them that acts, and this conflagration <note>The author uses "conflagration" here to describe the intense heat or wearing down caused by friction</note> consumes the rubbed bodies. Therefore, it would follow that the heavens, in their friction, having no air between them, generate no sound <note>original: "Harmonie pythagorique des sphères." This refers to the Pythagorean "Music of the Spheres," the ancient belief that celestial bodies produced a divine harmony through their movement.</note>.

And if this friction were real, the heavens, in the many centuries they have completed their revolutions, would have been consumed by the enormous speed they deploy every day; and if they did produce a sound, it would not spread, since the sound of a percussion made underwater is felt very little, and less and less as the bodies become denser. Between smooth bodies, there is no friction, and thus there can be none in the contact or rubbing of the heavens; and if these heavens are not smooth at the point of their frictional contact, they would be globular and rough, and therefore their contact would not be continuous, and it would be as if they generated a vacuum, which does not exist in nature.

One concludes that friction would have consumed the boundaries of each heaven and by as much

<summary>The author argues against the ancient Pythagorean theory of the 'Music of the Spheres,' using principles of physics to claim that celestial movement in a vacuum without air cannot produce sound or friction.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, celestial mechanics, Music of the Spheres, friction, vacuum, atmospheric density, Pythagorean theory, cosmology</keywords>

(*Codex Atlanticus 122. verso*)

# VIII

# GEOLOGY

## DISCOURSE ON THE DELUGE AND FOSSILS

262.— The body of the earth resembles that of animals, woven by the ramifications of veins, all connected together, and constructed for the nourishment and the vivification of the planet as well as its creatures. (*R. 100.*) <note>Leonardo frequently used the "microcosm" analogy, believing the Earth functioned like a living organism with its own circulatory system.</note>

263. — Nothing is born where there is no sensitive, vegetative, and rational life: feathers grow on birds and change every year; hairs grow and change on the bodies of animals, except in certain parts like the whiskers of lions, cats, and the like; the grass in the meadows and the leaves on the trees are, every year, largely renewed. We might say that nature has a vegetative soul, and that its flesh is the soil, and its bones are the layers of rock formations <note>original: "ordres d’agrégations des roches"</note> which develop into mountains; and its tendons are the <term>tuffs</term> <note>Tuff is a type of rock made of volcanic ash; Leonardo views these porous rocks as the connective tissues of the Earth.</note>; its blood, the veins of water; the lake of

<summary>Leonardo presents his theory of the Earth as a living organism, drawing direct anatomical parallels between biological life and geological structures like rocks, soil, and water.</summary>
<keywords>geology, microcosm, anatomy of the earth, vegetative soul, rock formations, hydrology, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

266. — The earth is a star. The earth, by means of the aqueous sphere <note>the layer of water or oceans</note> which covers it for the most part, takes on the likeness of the sun and shines in the universe, just as all the other stars do. (*R. 286.*)

267. — Next, show how the twinkling of certain stars originates in the eye, and why this twinkling is more vivid in one person than in another; and say that if the twinkling came, as it appears, from the star itself, it would be the effect of an extreme dilation in the body of the star; being therefore larger than the earth, such incessant movement would be too rapid to dilate the magnitude of the star; prove next that the surface of the air does not touch the surface of the fire <note>referring to the Aristotelian "sphere of fire" once thought to exist above the atmosphere</note> and this latter at its boundary receives the solar rays: everywhere this similarity exists with the great celestial bodies during their rising and setting, and with the small ones on the contrary, when they are in the middle of the sky. (*F. 56, v.*)

268. — Since the earth is not in the middle of the sun's orbit, nor at the center of the universe, but in the midst of the elements, its companions united with it; and since it stands with the moon when the moon is with the sun, below us; thus our earth with its aqueous element appears and performs the same function as the moon does for us. (*F. 25, v.*)

270. — My book aims to show how the ocean, along with the other seas, makes our world shine like the moon by means of the sun; and to those at a great distance, it appears to be a star, and this I prove. (*Codex Atlanticus 112, verso.*)

271. — The yolk, or the yellow of the egg, remains in the middle of its white <note>original: "albumine"</note> without sinking toward any side; whether it is lighter or heavier, it is equal to its white. If it were lighter, it should rise above all the white and come to rest against the eggshell; if it were heavier, it should sink; and if it were equal, it could stay at one of the ends just as well as in the middle or at the bottom. <note>Leonardo uses the egg as a biological model to understand how the Earth might be suspended in space or how materials of different densities interact.</note> (*R. 902*).

272. — Earth is heavy in its own sphere, but even more so when it is in a lighter element.

Fire is light in its own sphere, but even more so when it is in a heavier element.

No simple element has weight or lightness within its own sphere. <note>Leonardo refers to the Renaissance belief that the universe was arranged in concentric spheres of Earth, Water, Air, and Fire. An element only "feels" heavy or light when it is out of its natural place.</note> (*F. 56, verso.*)

273. — The movement that heavy bodies make toward the center is not the result of an attraction that draws the body to the center, nor of an inherent tendency, like that of a magnet which attracts things to itself. (*F. 69, verso.*)

**274.** — Why does a weight not resist in its own place?
— It does not remain, for lack of resistance.
— How will it move?
— It will move toward the opposite of the center <note>The "center" here refers to the center of the Earth, the point toward which all heavy things strive.</note>.
— And why not along another line?
— Because a weight that meets no resistance descends downward by the shortest path, and the lowest point is the center of the world.
— And why will this weight fall quickly?
— Because, not being an animate thing, it does not travel by diverse lines. (*C.A. 153, v.*) <note>original: "Codex Atlanticus, folio 153, verso." Leonardo argues that inanimate objects follow the most direct geometric path, unlike living creatures.</note>

**275.** — *The end of the world*: The aqueous element <term>aqueous element: water, one of the four classical elements</term> remaining trapped between the rising banks of the rivers and the sea within the growing earth: and the surrounding air having to encompass the softened machine of the earth: its mass, which stands between the water and the element of fire, will become immobile, reduced, and deprived of necessary moisture.

The rivers will remain without water, the earth will no longer show its fresh foliage, the fields will no longer be adorned with reborn plants, the animals will find no fresh grass to graze upon and will die: food will fail the birds of prey, lions, wolves, and other animals that live by hunting; and humans, after many expedients, will abandon life; and the human race will cease.

Thus, the fertile and fruitful earth, abandon—

**277. — *On the Deluge*:** Based on the two layers of shells, one must say that the earth, by sinking, plunged beneath the sea and thus the first layer was formed; the deluge created the second. **277 *bis*. — On this point, there is a doubt:** was the deluge of Noah's time universal <note>meaning global</note> or not? It appears not, based on the reasons we shall state.

In the Bible we find that the predicted deluge lasted forty days and forty nights of continuous and universal rain, and that this water rose above the summit of the highest mountains. If the rain was universal, it surrounded our earth with spherical waves; and a spherical surface has all its parts equally distant from the center of the sphere. Hence, with the sphere of water being in this condition, it is impossible for the water to move upon itself, because water does not move within water unless it descends. Therefore, if the water of this deluge spread out in this way, it is because it had no motion of its own. And if it spread out, by its own move-

278. — If you say that the shells, which are seen today at such heights at the borders of Italy, far from the seas, are found there because the Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah.</note> left them there—I will answer you, that if you believe the Deluge surpassed the highest mountain by seven cubits <note>original: "sept cubes" (seven cubits), a traditional measurement used in Genesis.</note>—as he who measured it wrote!—these shells, which are always found near the seabed, should have remained on the mountain tops and not so near the base of the mountains, and everywhere at the same height, layer upon layer.

And if you say that these shells, being accustomed to staying near the sea beds, followed the rising of the water to its greatest height as it grew; the answer is that the shell animal is no faster in movement than a slug would be out of the water, and perhaps even less so, because it does not swim, but makes a leap, or braces itself, and thus will travel three or four cubits <term>brasses: an old unit of length based on the reach of the arms</term> in a day. Therefore, at this pace, the shellfish would not travel from the Adriatic Sea to Montferrat in Lombardy (a distance of 250 miles

From the Val d'Arno towards Arezzo, a second lake was formed which emptied its waters into the first one, at the spot where Girone is seen today; it occupied the entire valley for a distance of forty miles in length. This valley received, at its bottom, all the earth brought down by the turbid water; one can still see this sediment remaining very high at the foot of Pratomagno <note>A mountain range in the Tuscan Apennines.</note>, where the rivers have not yet worn it away. This lake joined with that of Perugia.

Many shells are found where rivers flow into the sea, even though in such places the water is not as salty because of the current of fresh water mixing there.

Evidence of this is seen at the place where the Apennine Mountains pour their rivers into the Adriatic Sea; for the most part, they show among the rocks a quantity of shells mixed with sea sand, and all the hollow rocks in that area are filled with shells.

...full of shells and oysters in its pools; the shells do not extend toward the Val di Nievole <note>A valley in Tuscany between Lucca and Pistoia.</note>, because the fresh water of the Arno does not reach that far.

The shells did not leave the sea because of the Flood, because the water that pours off the land passed through there, drawing the sea toward the land and touching the bottom. For water coming from the land has a swifter course than that of the sea, and having more force, it flows under the sea water, stirs the bottom, and carries along all movable things it finds, such as shells and their like; and just as the water coming from the land is more turbid than that of the sea, it is similarly more powerful and heavier.

Therefore, I see no way for shells to be found in nature, unless they were born there. <note>This is a revolutionary scientific observation: Leonardo concludes that fossils are the remains of living organisms that lived and died in situ when the mountains were still the seabed.</note>

If you tell me: the river Loire, which passes through France, covers more than eighty <note>original: "ottante," an Italianism for eighty</note> miles of country because the ground is very flat; and there, the sea rises by twenty fathoms <term>brasses</term> <note>A unit of length based on the reach of a man's arms, roughly 6 feet.</note>, and shells are found on this flat ground eighty miles from the sea—I will reply that the ebb and flow in our internal seas <note>The Mediterranean Sea.</note> do not cause as much change, because at Geneva <note>Leonardo likely meant Genoa (*Gênes*), as Geneva is landlocked and Genoa is a major seaport subject to the Mediterranean's (admittedly small) tides.</note> nothing varies, at Venice little, and little also in Africa; and where it varies little, it occupies little land. *(R. 987.)*

strated, we shall conclude that the surface wave cannot carry shells heavier than water.

If the Deluge had to carry the shells three or four hundred miles from the sea, it would have carried them along with various other things; yet we see, at such a distance, the oyster together with the scallop and the squid-fish and all the other shellfish which are clustered together, all found dead; and the isolated shells found distant from one another, just as on the seashores: this is seen every day.

And if we find large-sized oysters with their valves still joined together, this signifies that they were left by the sea while still alive, when the Strait of Gibraltar was cut open <note>Leonardo suggests that the Mediterranean was once a closed lake that rose until it breached the Strait of Gibraltar, eventually changing the sea levels.</note>.

I have seen in the mountains of Parma and Piacenza the multitude of shells and red corals, still attached to the rocks. When I was making the great horse in Milan <note>The "Gran Cavallo," a massive bronze equestrian monument commissioned by Ludovico Sforza, which Leonardo worked on for years but never finished in bronze.</note>, a certain peasant brought a large sack of them to my workshop. He had found them, and many were preserved in their original casing.

Found under the earth and in the deep caves of the rocks, worked pieces of wood, already blackened, were discovered in my time at Castel Fiorentino; and these woods in that deep place originated from the silt thrown up by the Arno.

horns of oxen and sheep, and in the branches of plants that have never been pruned in any part.

Having, with such signs, demonstrated the duration of their lives in a manifest way, it must be confessed that such animals did not live without movement to seek their food; and one sees in them no instrument suitable for penetrating the earth or the rock where they are found embedded.

In what way could one find, within a large snail shell, the fragments and parts of many other kinds of shells of varied nature if this snail, already dead on the seashore, had not been tossed by the waters of the sea, like the other light bodies that it throws upon the land?

Why does one find so many fragments and shells on the shores and within the folds of the stone, if they had not previously been covered on the shore by a silt <note>original: "limon"; fine sand or clay carried by running water.</note> cast up by the sea, which subsequently petrified?

If the predicted Deluge <note>The biblical Great Flood.</note> had brought them to such a place from the sea, you would find these shells at the foot of a single mountain and not in many others.

We must then enumerate the annals of time <note>Leonardo views the layers of the earth as a historical record or "annals" of the planet's age.</note> when the sea multiplied the deposits of sand and mud, bringing them from nearby rivers and discharging them from their beds into its own.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic refutation of the Great Flood as the cause of fossil distribution. <page-num>142</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

And if you were to say that several deluges were necessary to produce these deposits of shells, you would have to assert that such a deluge occurred every year.

And if you mean to say that this deluge carried hundreds of thousands of shells out of the sea, this cannot be; for the deluge was caused by rain, and naturally, rain swelling the rivers causes them to sweep what they carry toward the sea, and they do not pull dead things from the mountains to bring them to the sea <note>Leonardo points out the logical flaw: floodwaters flow down from mountains to the sea, they do not push heavy objects from the sea up into the mountains.</note>.

And if you say that the deluge raised its waters above the mountains, the movement of the sea was so slow in traveling against the current of the rivers that it could not have supported, above it, objects heavier than water. If it had been able to support them, as the water receded, it would have scattered them in various places.

How shall we explain the corals which, near Monferrato in Lombardy <note>A hilly region in Northwest Italy known for its fossil-rich soil.</note>, were all found corroded and attached to the rocks uncovered by the river current?

These rocks are entirely covered with things of the nature and family of oysters, which we know to be deprived of movement and which must always be attached by one of their valves to the rock, opening the other to feed on animalcules <note>term: animalcules; microscopic organisms or tiny creatures, from the Latin 'animalculum'.</note> that pass in the water and which, believing they have found good pasture, become the food of the shellfish.

One does not find sand mixed with seaweed in a state of petrification <note>The process of turning into stone.</note>, since the seaweed—

280. — Shellfish are animals whose skeleton is on the outside. (Cited by Humboldt <note>Alexander von Humboldt (1769–1859), the polymath and geographer who recognized Leonardo’s early scientific insights.</note>.)

281. — If the land of the antipodes <note>term: antipodes; the parts of the earth directly opposite to each other; here Leonardo refers to the opposite side of the globe.</note>, which supports the ocean, were to rise and reveal itself sufficiently above the sea while remaining almost flat, over time mountains, valleys, and rocks would be formed. The mounds of silt and sand from which the water drains—when they remain exposed after a flood—allow us to answer the question. The water, as it flows away, would begin to create streams in the lowest parts; these streams, by hollowing themselves out, would receive the surrounding waters, increasing in width and depth along their course, and would become the beds of torrents. On both sides, banks would rise and dry out, creating stones in layers of varying thickness from the silt brought by the rivers. (*F. 11, v.*)

<summary>Leonardo describes the process of erosion and sedimentation, explaining how receding waters carve out riverbeds and how silt deposits eventually harden into stratified rock layers.</summary>

### IX

## WATER AND AIR

**282.** — Water, the vital humor <note>term: humor; in ancient and Renaissance medicine, humors were the fluids—like blood or bile—that governed the health and function of a living body.</note> of the terrestrial machine, moves by means of its own natural heat. (*R. 965.*)

**283.** — Water is what one calls the vital humor of this arid earth; it moves through the branching of its veins, acting against the natural course of heavy things; and properly, it is water that moves the humors in every species of animated body. (*H. 77, r.*)

**284.** — Water is the great carrier <note>original: "voiturier"; literally a wagoner or transporter.</note> of nature. (*K. 2, r.*)

It hollows out the soil and, in its first rush, transports large stones; then smaller ones, whose corners it wears down by rolling them; and finally coarse sand, fine sand, and silt. (*R. 977.*)

**285.** — The water that gushes from the mountains is the blood that vivifies the mountains and exits from their flanks or comes through them; nature aids its life, being abundant in the increase destined to repair the lack of desired moisture;

<margin>vital humor, terrestrial machine, branching, carrier, silt, desired moisture</margin>

<summary>Leonardo draws a direct analogy between the circulation of water through the Earth's "veins" and the circulation of blood in the human body, defining water as the primary mechanical and life-giving force of nature.</summary>
<keywords>hydrology, vital humor, erosion, microcosm, terrestrial machine, geology</keywords>

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

290. — Ask, when a circle meets, in expanding, the circle that corresponds to it, whether it enters into its waves by cutting them or if, at the points of contact, things are reflected at equal angles. <note>Leonardo is contemplating the physics of wave interference—how two ripples in water interact when they collide.</note> What a beautiful and subtle problem! (*A. 61, r.*)

291. — How does the mass of water that surrounds the earth take a spherical shape? This theorem is difficult, but I will nevertheless say what it seems to me.

Water, covered by the air, naturally desires to remain united in its sphere, because there it is deprived of gravity. This gravity is twofold: first, its whole has a gravity attached to the center of the elements (<term>centripetal</term> <note>original: "centripète"; here Leonardo refers to a force pulling toward the center of the Earth.</note>) and another which tends toward the center of this water’s sphericity (<term>centrifugal</term> <note>original: "centrifuge"; here Leonardo uses the term to describe a force acting relative to the surface or center of the water's volume.</note>). This is as if these two gravities did not exist: I see only a half-sphere which remains at the center.

But I know of no human means to provide a scientific formula for it, other than to say—as of the magnet that attracts iron—that it is one of those occult virtues <note>In the Renaissance, "occult" meant "hidden." Leonardo is acknowledging that some natural forces, like magnetism or gravity, cannot yet be explained by visible mechanics.</note> or properties that are so numerous in nature. (*C. A. 75, v*).

292. — Note how the movement of the surface of the water resembles that of a head of hair; the movement of hair is in two parts: one corresponds to the weight of the hair and the other to its curve. Thus water has its turns and detours; sometimes it obeys the movement of

293. — Water is the great carrier of nature; it conducts the earth from the mountains into the humid valleys, making them fertile and cleansing the surrounding air. <note>original: "F. 14, r." refers to Manuscript F in the Institut de France.</note>

294. — Among the worst causes of earthly damage, it seems to me that rivers, with their ruinous floods, hold the first rank; and not fire, as some have claimed. For fire ceases to devour as soon as it no longer has fuel; the movement of water, which is maintained by the inclination of valleys, only ends and dies at the lowest point of the valley. Fire is caused by fuel, and the movement of water by the slope. The fuel of fire is disjointed and separated, and thus it dies as it spreads out. The declivity of the valley is one, and the damage becomes general, like the ravaging course of the river, which in the company of its tributaries ends in the sea—the universal low point and the sole rest for the wandering <term>peregrination<note>A journey or pilgrimage; here used to describe the long, purposeful cycle of water.</note></term> of river water.

But with what language and what vocabulary could one express and describe the incredible and frightening ravages, the inexorable malices committed by the deluge of great rivers? How shall I be able to say it?

...water, by means of an instrument. Why I do not write of my method of remaining under water for as long as one can remain without food, and why I do not publish or disclose it? It is because of the evil nature of men, who would use it to commit assassinations even at the bottom of the sea, by breaking ships and sinking them along with the crews who live within. ...a book of inanimate things that descend through the air without wind, and another of those that descend with the wind. (*Manuscript F, folio 53, verso*)

307. — First perform the anatomy of the wing, then that of the <term>quills</term> <note>The central shafts of the feathers</note> without the feathers, then that of the quills with the feathers. (*Manuscript E, folio 51, recto*)

308. — I have divided the treatise on birds into four books: the first treats of flight by the beating of wings; the second of flight aided by the wind; the third of flight in general, including bats, fish, and insects; the fourth of artificial flight <note>Leonardo's term for man-made flying machines</note>. (*Manuscript K, folio 3, recto*)

309. — The bird makes itself heavy or light in the air, according to its will. (*Manuscript E, folio 48, verso*)

309 *bis.* — Just as a man supports himself with his feet and back between two walls when sweeping chimneys, so does the bird with the spread of its wingtips against the air upon which they lean. (*Manuscript E, folio 46, recto*)

310. — There is as much force required to move the air against a stationary object as there is to move the object against stationary air. <note>This is an early observation of the principle of relative motion in aerodynamics.</note> (*Manuscript E, folio 21, recto*)

311. — The movement of the bird propagates from the leading edge of the wing to its tip: just as a sailor, with a turn of the helm, changes the direction of a vessel. (*Manuscript E, folio 52, verso*)

312. — The bird is an organism that operates by mathematical means; it is within the power of man to replicate all the movements of a bird, though not with equal strength;

this refers only to the power of balance. I shall say, therefore, that this instrument composed by man lacks nothing but the soul of the bird, which must be modeled after the soul of man.

The soul would undoubtedly obey the limbs of birds better than the soul of man would, especially when separated from him in movements of an almost imperceptible equilibrium. But since we see the bird provide for a great variety of movements of a perceptible order, we can judge, by this experience, that the most perceptible could be understood by man, and that he could largely provide a replacement for this instrument of which he would have made himself the soul and the guide. (*Forster Manuscript II, folio 52, verso*)

313. — Small birds, being without thick plumage, cannot withstand the great cold of the high altitudes where eagles and other large birds live, clothed in several layers of feathers.

Furthermore, small birds, with their weak and thin wings, support themselves in the lower air which is heavy <note>Leonardo refers here to air density; lower air is denser and provides more lift</note>, but not in the upper air which offers little resistance. (*Codex Atlanticus, folio 66, verso*)

315. — The air moves like a river and carries the clouds with it: just as running water carries all floating things. (*Manuscript G, folio 10, recto*)

316. — The wind is in every way similar to water in its movement. (*Manuscript A, folio 60, recto*)

317. — In all its movements, water has a great conformity with the air. (*Manuscript A, folio 61, recto*)

318. — Sounds move away from their causes in circular movements, always keeping them as their center. <note>Leonardo is describing the propagation of sound waves, comparing them to ripples in a pond.</note> (*Manuscript A, folio 61, recto*)

319. — Every blow struck leaps back at an angle equal to that of the percussion. <note>This is an early observation of the law of reflection: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.</note> (*Manuscript A, folio 19, recto*)

320. — Sound returns to the ear by an oblique line similar to the line of incidence; it is reflected like a luminous ray in a mirror. (*Manuscript A, folio 19, verso*)

320 *bis*. — It will take its first flight, the great bird, upon the back of its great swan, filling the universe with amazement, filling all writings with its fame, and giving imperishable glory to the place of its birth. <note>original: "il grande uccello" (the great bird). This is Leonardo's famous prophecy regarding his flying machine, likely intended to be launched from Mount Ceceri (Swan Mountain) near Florence.</note> (*Riccardian Manuscript 1428.*)
->(*The flying man.*)<-

320 *ter*. — The object that strikes the air exerts a force equal to the air that strikes the object. <note>This anticipates Newton’s Third Law of Motion: for every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction.</note> You see that the beating of wings against the air supports the heavy eagle in the highest and rarest air; you also see the air fill the swelling sails and push the heavily loaded ship.

From these proofs, you can know that man, with his great wings acting against the resistance of the air, will be able to victoriously overcome it and rise above it. (*R. 1126.*)

321. — To provide the true science of the movement of birds in the air, it is necessary first to provide the science of the winds, which explains the movements of water and of itself; and this <term>sensible science<note>By "sensible science," Leonardo means knowledge derived from direct observation and the senses, rather than purely abstract theory.</note></term> will itself serve as a ladder to reach the knowledge of creatures flying in the air and in the wind. (*E. 54. r.*)

322. — When you assemble the science of the movements of the air, remember to place under each proposition its <term>means<note>original: "moyens." Leonardo refers to the experimental demonstrations or practical applications that prove his theoretical points.</note></term>, so that this science may not be useless. (*F. 2. v.*)

323. — The <term>témoins<note>Literally "witnesses." Leonardo is likely referring to the secondary feathers or the specific structural attachments at the wing joint that respond to air pressure.</note></term> attached to the humerus of the wing in birds are of an ingenious kind, allowing for an easy bending for the straight-line impact so frequent in the furious flight of birds: it is much more convenient in a direct ascent to fold a tiny part of the wing than the entire wing. (*E. 52. v.*)

324. — The flying man must be free from the waist up to be able to balance himself, just as one does in a boat, so that the center of gravity within him and within the machine can shift as necessity requires. (*R. 1122.*)

### X

# ANATOMY AND OPTICS

325. — And you, who judge it preferable to watch an anatomy <note>A public dissection or anatomical demonstration</note> being performed rather than to see these drawings, you would be right, if it were possible to see in reality everything that these drawings show you in a single figure; in which, with all your genius, you will see and understand nothing but a few veins; whereas I, in order to have true and full knowledge of them, have dissected more than ten human bodies, separating all the limbs, consuming <note>The author likely means "stripping away" or "meticulously removing"</note> into very small parts all the flesh that was found around these veins, without shedding blood, except for the almost imperceptible blood of the capillary <term>capillary: the smallest, hair-like blood vessels</term> veins. A single body does not last the time necessary; it is necessary to proceed, from one to another, through several bodies to arrive at complete knowledge; and often to repeat the process twice to find the differences <note>The author is referring to anatomical variations between individuals</note>.

And even if you had the love for such things, you might still be prevented by your stomach; and if that did not prevent you, you would be afraid to spend the hou—

<summary>The author justifies the necessity of anatomical drawings by explaining the grueling, multi-body process of dissection required to map the human vascular system accurately.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, dissection, scientific illustration, blood vessels, medical observation, human body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci's defense of his anatomical studies, moving from the physical hardships of dissection to the intellectual and artistic requirements of his work, eventually comparing the human body to a map of the world.</meta>

res nocturnes in the company of mutilated and
opened corpses which are terrifying to behold; and if
you should even overcome that, you will still lack the
skill in drawing necessary for such a representation.

If you possess the skill in drawing, will you know perspective?
Will you also have the method of geometric demonstration
and the calculation of the forces and behaviors of the
muscles? Finally, you will lack patience: and you will
not be diligent.

Whether I have all these things or not, the one hundred
and twenty volumes I have composed will testify, and
to create them I have allowed myself to be stopped neither
by avarice nor by haste, but only by time. *Farewell* <note>original: "Vale" - a traditional Latin closing</note>. (*R*. 796.)

326.— Therefore, with twelve figures, the <term>cosmography</term> <note>The science of mapping the general features of the universe or the world</note> of the minor world (*man, the <term>microcosm</term>*) <note>The ancient philosophical idea that the human being is a "little world" reflecting the structure of the entire universe</note> will be shown to you in the same order followed by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century geographer and astronomer</note> in his *Cosmography*. Thus, I will then divide the body into members, as he divides the world into provinces; I will describe the function of the parts for each side, placing before your eyes the knowledge of the entire figure and power of man and the local movement that connects his parts.
(*R*. 798.)

327. — This appears clearly if you watch the movement of paralytics, those with fever, or those who are frozen; their limbs tremble—head and hands—without the permission of their soul, which, with all its strength, could not prevent their limbs from trembling.

328. — Nature has ordained, in man, the "officious" muscles <note>original: "muscles officieux"; meaning muscles that perform a specific service or function</note>, the pullers of the nerves, which can move the limbs according to the will and desire of the common sense <note>original: "sens commun"; in Renaissance psychology, the "sensus communis" was the part of the brain where all five senses were integrated</note>, similar to officers delegated by a lord through several provinces and cities, who represent him in these various places and obey his will. This administration, which in more than one case obeys the order given by the mouth of the lord, often acts on its own in a similar case, without the lord having had to manifest his will. (*C. A. 119, r.*)

329. — The heart is the most powerful of muscles... I have described the position of the muscles that descend from the base to the tip of the heart and the position of the muscles that start from the tip of the heart and go to its base. (*G. 1, v.*)

330. — The ears of the heart <note>original: "oreilles du cœur"; now known as the auricles or atria</note> are outposts that receive the blood escaping from the ventricle <note>term: ventricle; the lower chambers of the heart that pump blood out to the body or lungs</note>, from the beginning to the end of the contraction, for if such blood did not partly escape, the heart would not be able to contract. (*G. 1, v.*)

331. — The blood that turns back, when the heart reopens, is not that which closes the doors <note>original: "portes"; referring to the heart valves</note> of the heart. (*R. 850.*)

332. — The blood of animals is always in motion, starting from the sea of the heart and rising to the summit of the head. (*G. 2, r.*)

333. — You shall make a study of the hands <note>Leonardo often uses the word "hands" to refer to the forepaws or front extremities of animals</note> of each animal to show how they differ; as in the bear, which has the ligaments of the digital tendons joined together upon the instep <note>original: "cou-de-pied"; the arch of the foot</note>. so that when faced with something unforeseen and terrifying, a man does not bring his hands to his heart, the source of life, nor to his head, the dwelling place of the lord of the senses, nor to the ear, nor to the nose, nor to the mouth, but rather before the threatened sense; he closes his eyes, tightly squeezing the eyelids which suddenly turn him away to the other side; not feeling sufficiently reassured, he places both hands as an advance guard against that which disturbs him.

336. — The <term>pupil</term> <note>The dark circular opening in the center of the iris that regulates light.</note> of the eye changes in size according to the brightness or darkness of the objects presented before it.

Nature, in this, has watched over the <term>visual faculty</term> <note>The biological ability to see and process light.</note>; when it is affected by an overabundance of light, she restricts the pupil, and when it is hindered by darkness, she increases its light, much like one does with the opening of a purse. Nature acts like a man who, having too much light in his house, half-closes the window, more or less according to necessity; and who opens it wide when night comes, to see better inside his house. Nature proceeds by a <term>continual equation</term> <note>Leonardo’s term for a constant, proportional adjustment or balancing act.</note>, tempering and regulating the growth and diminution of the pupil in proportion to the light.
