337. — The eye, whose function is so well demonstrated by experience, has been defined up until my time by an infinite number of authors in a manner that I judge to be erroneous. (*C. A. 117, v.*)

338. — The eye could not, in a month's time, send its visual power to the height of the sun. <note>Leonardo is arguing against the ancient "extramission" theory, which suggested the eye emits rays to "touch" objects; he believed light travels from the object to the eye.</note> (*ASH. 2, l. v.*)

339. — The air is filled with pyramids of radiating straight lines which originate from every point of luminous bodies, and which form angles that become increasingly acute as they move further from their point of origin. (*R. I. 63.*)

340. — The <term>crystalline sphere</term> <note>original: "sphère vitrée"; Leonardo refers here to the lens or the vitreous humor, which he believed acted as a secondary lens to flip images back upright.</note> serves in the middle of the eye to right the images that cross one another within the opening of the pupil, so that the right becomes right again, and the left becomes left again, by the second intersection which occurs at the center of the crystalline sphere. (*D. 3, v.*)

341. — The pupil of the eye diminishes in proportion to the increase of the light that acts upon it; conversely, the pupil increases in proportion to the decrease of the clarity of the day or of any other light that acts upon it. (*E. 17, v.*)

342. — The pupil of the eye, in the open air, changes dimension with every degree of the sun's movement; and with the variations of the pupil, a variation occurs in the visual perception of the same object, although often the comparison with surrounding objects does not per—

343. — Every body that moves with speed seems to color its path with its own hue. The lightning that tears through the dark clouds with its rapid course resembles a luminous serpent. Give a glowing ember a circular motion, and its circle will appear to you as if made of fire. This is because the impression is swifter than the judgment. <note>Leonardo is describing the phenomenon known today as "persistence of vision," where the brain retains an image for a fraction of a second longer than the eye sees it.</note> In passing from light to shadow, the shadow appears darker until the eye has lost the impression of the light. (*A. 26, v.*)

344. — Gaze upon the light and admire its beauty. Close your eye and look; what you saw at first is no more, and what you will see next is not yet.

Who is it that remakes it, if the maker is in continual motion? <note>Leonardo reflects here on the ephemeral nature of photons and the passage of time; the "maker" may refer to the source of light or the sun itself.</note> (*F. 49, v.*)

345. — Just as a body, moving slowly through the length of its opposing movement, turns with the path and then makes a broader stroke—and as continuous and brief movements are of little value—so it is with study on the same subject. Conducted at long intervals of time, it allows for a more perfect judgment, and one better perceives one's own errors. Such is the method of the painter’s eye in critiquing his own work. <note>Leonardo frequently advised artists to step away from their canvases, believing that looking at one's work with "fresh eyes" after a break revealed mistakes that were invisible during the heat of creation.</note> (*C. A. 122, v.*)

346. — Why does the eye see the object of its dreams more precisely than it does with the imagination when one is awake? <note>original: "lorsqu'il est debout"; literally "when he is standing," meaning when he is upright and awake.</note> (*R. 1114.*)

<summary>Leonardo examines the "persistence of vision" through moving embers and lightning, while also offering practical advice to painters on the necessity of taking breaks to improve artistic judgment.</summary>

## XI
# REFUTATION
### OF THE
# OCCULT SCIENCES

347. — The most stupid of human discourses, and that which must be held as such, is that which addresses itself to credulity regarding <term>necromancy</term> <note>original: "nigromantie"; literally "black divination," often associated with conjuring the dead or demonic magic</note>, the sister of <term>alchemy</term>, which nonetheless gives birth to simple and natural things. But <term>necromancy</term> is more blameworthy than <term>alchemy</term>, insofar as it gives birth to nothing except a thing identical to itself, namely: the lie.

There is no lie in <term>alchemy</term> <note>The author views alchemy here as a form of experimental chemistry rather than a mystical pursuit</note>, which is the administrator of the simple products of nature and whose office cannot be produced by nature itself, because within nature there are no organic instruments with which she can operate what man operates with his hands; and thus he has made glass, etc...

But this <term>necromancy</term>, the banner and true ban-

<summary>The author presents a sharp critique of occult sciences, specifically distinguishing between alchemy, which he views as a practical craft that assists nature, and necromancy, which he dismisses as a fraudulent pursuit of lies.</summary>
<keywords>occult sciences, alchemy, necromancy, nature, human ingenuity, deception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s skeptical argument regarding the "art" of necromancy. Having listed the terrifying military and natural powers such magic would grant on the previous page, he now explores the personal gains—wealth and flight—before concluding with a philosophical rejection of its reality based on human nature and the divine order.</meta>

of any fleet? <note>This sentence completes the rhetorical question from the previous page regarding maritime battles and the control of winds.</note> Certainly, he who commands such impetuous powers would be the lord of peoples, and no human genius could resist his implacable forces.

Hidden treasures and gems buried deep in the womb of the earth would all be revealed to him. He would have himself carried through the air from East to West and to all the most distant corners of the universe...

But why dwell on this further? What thing could still resist such a <term>cunning artifice</term> <note>original: "artifice"; in this context, Leonardo refers to the "constructed" or "manufactured" claims of magical systems</note>? Almost nothing, except for the abolition of death. And if magic were true, why has it not remained among men who desire it so much, having no regard for any deity?

And this is of infinite consequence for man, who would abolish God along with the entire universe to satisfy his appetites.

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s philosophical inquiries into the laws of nature, specifically debunking the supernatural and the possibility of a vacuum.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>
<header>ON LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

If magic has not remained among men despite being so necessary to them, it is because it never existed and never will exist.

By definition, the spirit is that which is invisible within a body: and in the other elements there is no such thing as an incorporeal thing, for where there is no body, there is a vacuum, and the vacuum does not exist in the elements, because it would be immediately filled by them. (*Richter 1213.*) <note>Leonardo refers to the Aristotelian concept that "nature abhors a vacuum."</note>

346. — I wish to perform miracles! Having less than other, more tranquil men: those who wish to grow rich in a single day live for a long time in the greatest poverty, as happens and will eternally happen to the alchemists, to those who wish to create gold and silver, to those minds who believe that dead water gives active life to itself with continuous motion, and to the supreme fool, the <term>necromancer</term> <note>original: "nigromant"; one who claims to communicate with the dead</note> and the <term>enchanter</term> <note>original: "incantateur"; a practitioner of magic spells</note>. (*Richter 766.*)

347.—We have, precisely for this side of the question, the definition of the spirit: — a power joined to a body, which cannot govern itself, nor perform any local movement whatsoever.

And if you say that the body governs itself; that cannot be within the elements, because if the spirit is an incorporeal quantity, this quantity is called a vacuum and there is no vacuum in nature; and, given the necessity of things, it would be immediately filled by the collapse of the element where the vacuum was produced.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci argues against the existence of magic, alchemy, and perpetual motion, grounding his critique in the physical impossibility of a vacuum in nature.</summary>
<keywords>magic, spirit, alchemy, necromancy, vacuum, physics, elements, Richter collection</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s physical and philosophical argument against the existence of incorporeal spirits. Having previously defined the spirit as an "incorporeal quantity" that would constitute a vacuum, he now applies the laws of gravity and fluid dynamics to prove that such an entity could not exist or move within the natural elements.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

Therefore, by the definition of weight which states: — gravity is an <term>accidental power</term> <note>original: "puissance accidentelle"; in Aristotelian physics, a force that is not inherent to the object's essence but caused by its displacement from its natural place</note>, created by one element drawn from another or pushed into another — it follows that no element, which has no weight within its own element, is a point in the superior element more subtle than itself, as is seen. A portion of water has no more weight or lightness than another: but if you draw it into the air, then it will acquire weight and this weight cannot sustain itself; hence its fall is inevitable, and the thing that falls into the water, no matter where, displaces that water.

Thus it would happen for the spirit, finding itself among the elements; it would continually generate a vacuum in the element where it found itself, and for that reason, it would be necessary for it to flee incessantly toward the heavens until it exited the <term>elementary zone</term> <note>The region of the four terrestrial elements (earth, water, air, fire) below the celestial spheres.</note>. (R. 1213.)

348. — We have proved that the spirit cannot remain within the elements without a body, nor move itself by a voluntary act, except within itself. At present, we shall say that if the spirit takes an <term>aerial body</term> <note>A body made of air, a common concept in Renaissance demonology to explain how spirits might become visible.</note>, it is necessary that it incorporate itself into that air, because if it were not united to it, it would be separated and would fall to generate a vacuum, as is said above.

Therefore it is necessary, if it wishes to remain in the air, that it infuse itself into a quantity of air; and if it unites with the air (<gloss>volatilizes</gloss>), two inconveniences follow, namely: it will render that quantity of air too light

<summary>Leonardo applies the laws of buoyancy and gravity to argue that a spirit without a body would be forced out of the atmosphere like a bubble, concluding that any "spirit" would require a physical, aerial form to exist on Earth.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, elements, vacuum, spirit, aerial body, physics, fluid dynamics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s scientific dismantling of the possibility of incorporeal spirits. Having previously argued that a spirit would naturally rise out of the atmosphere like a vacuum, he now examines the physical instability of a spirit attempting to inhabit a body made of air.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

---

of air with which it has united, and for that reason, the air, by its own nature, will carry it upward; it will not remain within air that is heavier than itself. Furthermore, the scattered <term>spiritual virtue</term> <note>original: "vertu spirituelle"; the vital force or essence that animates a being</note> will disperse and lose its nature, and thus be extinguished.

Let us add a third disadvantage: this aerial body taken by the spirit is subject to the penetration of winds, which incessantly disunite and separate the joined parts of the air, turning and swirling within the other air. Therefore, the spirit infused into the air would be dismembered and truly torn and broken by the dismemberment of the air in which it is infused. (*R. 1214.*)

349. — It is impossible for a spirit infused into a quantity of air to be able to move that air, and this is manifest: the spirit makes the quantity of air in which it is infused light. Therefore, this air will rise to a height, above the other air, and this will be by the air's own lightness, and not according to the will of the spirit; and what if this air undergoes the action of the wind? (*R. 1215.*)

350. — Wishing to show whether the spirit can speak or not, it is necessary to define what the voice is, and how it is produced: we shall say, therefore: the voice is a movement of air rubbed against a dense body; or a dense body rubbed by the air (which is the same thing). This friction of the dense with the air condenses it, rarefies it, and creates resistance; and furthermore, by the speed of the <term>rarefied</term> <note>original: "raréfié"; matter that has become less dense, the opposite of condensed</note> moving within the slow rarefied, they condense

<summary>Leonardo argues that a spirit cannot inhabit an aerial body because it would be physically torn apart by winds or float away due to buoyancy. He begins a scientific definition of the voice as a mechanical friction of air against solid matter to prove spirits cannot speak.</summary>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s argument from page 169, moving from the physical impossibility of a spirit maintaining its form in the wind to the impossibility of a spirit producing sound. He applies his observations on acoustics and fluid dynamics to prove that without physical mass (quantity), a spirit cannot manipulate air to create a voice.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

...one and the other, through contact, and produce a sound and a great noise.

And the sound or murmur is made by the **rarefied** <note>original: "raréfié"; here Leonardo refers to a substance that is thin, low-density, or gaseous</note> which moves within the rarefied with little movement, like a great flame that generates sounds in the air; and the great noise is made by the rarefied within the rarefied when the rapid rarefied matter penetrates the mobile rarefied matter, like the flame of fire exiting the <term>bombard</term> <note>an early type of cannon</note> and bursting in the air; and also the flame coming from the clouds which tears the air to generate thunder.

We shall say, therefore, that the spirit cannot generate a voice without a movement of air; and there is no air within the spirit, and if there were, it could not expel it; and if it wishes to move the air in which it is infused, it would be necessary for the spirit to multiply it; yet, it cannot do so if it has no **quantity** <note>physical volume or mass</note>, for the reason which says: — nothing rarefied moves if it does not have a stable place from which it takes its movement; and especially when having to move an element within an element that does not move of its own accord except by uniform vaporization at the center of the vaporous matter, as happens with a sponge squeezed by a hand held underwater; from which the water escapes on some side with an equal movement through the openings of the fingers of the hand that press it.

### Whether the spirit has an articulated voice and if it can be heard?

And what thing is hearing and the voice? The wave...

<summary>Leonardo argues that a spirit, lacking physical mass (quantity), cannot produce sound because sound requires the physical manipulation of air, comparing the mechanics of noise to cannons, thunder, and a squeezed sponge.</summary>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s scientific dismantling of the supernatural. Having argued on the previous page that a spirit lacks physical "quantity" or mass to move air, he now concludes that a spirit cannot possess a voice or perform physical actions without the mechanical structures of a body, such as bones and nerves. He then shifts his critique toward the pseudo-sciences of his day.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

---

...of the voice goes through the air, just as the image of objects goes to the eye.

O mathematicians, shed light on this error!

The spirit has no voice, because where there is a voice there is a body; and where there is a body, there is the occupation of space, which prevents the eye from seeing things placed behind that space.

Therefore, this body fills all the surrounding air—namely, with its images. <note>original: "images"; in Leonardo's optics, objects emit "species" or images that travel through the air to the eye</note> (*C. A. 1872.*)

351. — There can be no voice where there is no movement or percussion of air, and there is no percussion of air where there is no instrument, and there is no such thing as an incorporeal instrument.

This being established, a spirit can have neither voice, nor form, nor strength; and if it takes on a body, it will be unable to penetrate or enter where the exits are closed. And if someone says: — by a gathering of air and by drawing it to itself, the spirit takes on a body of varied form and by this means speaks and moves with force — to that I say: where there are neither nerves nor bones, there exists no <term>operative force</term> <note>the mechanical power required to produce physical work or movement</note> for any movement made by these imaginary spirits. (*B. 4, v.*)

352. — **Physiognomy**: Of the fallacious physiognomy <note>the practice of judging character from facial features</note> and palmistry <note>original: "chiromancie"; fortune-telling through the lines of the hand</note> I shall not treat, because there is no truth in them; this is manifest; and such <term>chimeras</term> <note>fanciful illusions or fabrications</note> have no scientific foundation.

<summary>Leonardo argues that a spirit cannot have a voice or physical power because sound and movement require a material body with bones and nerves. He further dismisses physiognomy and palmistry as baseless superstitions lacking scientific merit.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, anatomy, mechanics, spirits, physiognomy, palmistry, scientific method, Codex Atlanticus</keywords>

It is true that the features of the face partly show the nature of man, his vices, and his temperament. But within the face:  
*a)* The signs that separate the cheeks from the lips  
of the mouth, and the nostrils from the nose, and the  
eye sockets are prominent in men who are  
cheerful and always laughing; those who have these  
features poorly defined are, on the contrary, given  
to meditation.  
*b)* Those who have the parts of the face with great  
relief and depth are beastly people, vio-

*c)* Those who have lines placed between the

*d)* Those who have the transverse lines of the  
forehead strongly marked are men  
who lament a lot in secret or in  
public.

One can say as much for many traits.  
But what of the hand? You will find a great  
army exterminated in a single hour by  
the sword, and not one will have in his hand the same  
lines as another; it is the same for a  
shipwreck. <note>Leonardo is using logic to debunk palmistry: if the lines of the hand predicted one's fate, everyone dying in the same disaster would need to have identical "death" lines.</note> (*LU. 292.*)

## XII
# ESTHETICS
### *Apologetic and didactic sections*
### *on painting.*

#### APOLOGY FOR PAINTING

355. — It is with justice that painting complains of not being counted among the liberal arts <note>The "liberal arts" traditionally included grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy; Leonardo argues painting deserves this high status</note>, for she is a true daughter of nature, and she operates through the eye, the worthiest of our senses.

Yes, you are in the wrong, O writers, for having left her out of the liberal arts, for she does not limit herself to the works of nature alone; she applies herself to an infinity of things that nature cannot create. <note>original: "LU. 27." - likely a reference to the Codex Urbinas</note>

356. — And because writers do not possess a true knowledge of the science of painting <term>science picturale: the systematic study of light, shadow, and perspective</term>, they have been unable to describe her titles or her constituent parts, for the artistic work and its purpose cannot be demonstrated in words.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci defends painting as a superior intellectual discipline, arguing it should be classified as a liberal art because it communicates through the eye and transcends nature.</summary>
<keywords>esthetics, apology for painting, liberal arts, science of painting, visual vs. verbal, nature and art</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of painting as a noble science, following the argument that writers have unfairly excluded it from the liberal arts due to their own ignorance of its principles.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

Thus, by the fact of their ignorance, she <note>Painting</note> has remained behind the privileged sciences. This takes nothing away from her divinity.

And truly, they had a reason for not ennobling her; she is noble in herself, without adding another language to her, just as we do when facing the excellent works of nature. If painters have not described her and reduced her to principles, it is not the fault of painting; she is no less noble for that, despite the fact that some painters profess to be men of letters, because their lives are not long enough to learn their own art.

Shall we say that the virtue of plants and stones is not within them, because men have failed to recognize them? Certainly not: we will recognize that plants have their nobility within them, without the help of human tongue or letters. <note>original: "LU. 34." - Reference to the Codex Urbinas</note>

357. — Painting has a purpose communicable to all the generations of the universe; this purpose is subordinate to the visual faculty and does not pass through the ear to reach the common sense (1) in the same way that it passes through the sight.

Therefore, painting has no need for any language as an interpreter, unlike literature;

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting possesses an inherent divinity and nobility that requires no verbal justification, asserting its superiority as a universal language that communicates directly through the eyes.</summary>

representing a father of a family, the grandchildren began to caress him, even though they were still in swaddling clothes; and likewise the dog and the cat of the house did the same: and such a spectacle was a marvelous thing to behold. <note>original: "LU. 7." — Reference to the Codex Urbinas Latinus 1270, the primary source for Leonardo's Treatise on Painting.</note>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Paragone" (comparison of the arts), moving from the emotional impact of painting on children and animals to a philosophical argument regarding the superiority of sight over hearing and painting over poetry.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

358. — The eye, which is called the window of the soul, is the primary way by which the <term>common sense: an internal faculty in Renaissance psychology that coordinated the data from the five physical senses</term> can contemplate, extensively and in all their splendor, the infinite works of nature.

The ear follows next, which is ennobled by listening to the account of what the eye has seen.

If you—historiographers, poets, or mathematicians—have not seen things with your own eyes, you will not be able to report them well in your writings; and if you, poet, were to depict a story through the "painting" of your pen, and the painter with his brush, the painter will provide more satisfaction and make himself better understood. If you call painting "mute poetry," the painter can answer you that poetry is "blind painting." Now decide: which is the worse infirmity, to be blind or to be mute?

If the poet is as free as the painter in his inventions, his fictions do not give as much satisfaction to men as those of the painter. For if poetry applies itself to depicting forms, actions, and places with words, the painter manifests himself by <term>counterfeiting: in this context, the word means "imitating" or "reproducing" with high fidelity</term> the forms themselves, through their own likenesses. Now, see: which is closer to the man—the name "man" or his own human figure? The word that designates a man varies in different countries; his form is changed only by death.

<vocab>The eye, window of the soul, common sense, nature, historiographers, poetry, painting, mute poetry, human figure</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the eye is the superior organ of perception and that painting, as a visual medium, is more universal and truthful than the word-based art of poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Paragone, window of the soul, common sense, painting vs poetry, visual perception, universality of art</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Paragone," specifically the debate regarding the superiority of painting over poetry. Leonardo is responding to the argument that poetry is more "durable" or lasting than painting.</meta>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<page-num>177</page-num>

If you were to say: — poetry is more durable; I would reply that the work of a coppersmith is more durable, for time will preserve it better than yours or ours: however, this is a poor joke; and painting, by working on copper with glass colors <note>Leonardo refers here to enamel painting or perhaps oil on copper, which he believed could resist the decay of time better than canvas or fresco</note>, will become more durable still.

By our art, we deserve to be called the nephews of God. If poetry rises to moral philosophy, painting practices natural philosophy <note>Natural philosophy refers to the study of the physical world and its laws, which Leonardo believed the painter mastered through observation</note>. If the former describes the operation of the mind that reflects, the other, with the mind, operates through movements. If one terrifies nations with its infernal fictions, the other also produces a similar effect with the same things. If the poet and the painter challenge each other to depict beauty, ferocity, a brutal wickedness, or a monstrosity: there is the same proportion from the imagination to the effect as there is from the shadow to the body that casts it: and this same proportion is observed between poetry and painting: because poetry speaks to the imagination with letters, while painting actually places an image before the eye, from which it receives the likeness as if the things were natural. Poetry does not provide this likeness and does not act upon the senses through the path of the visual power, as painting does. (*ASH.* I. 19, *v.*) <note>ASH refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, part of Leonardo's notebooks</note>

359. — Painting represents, for the senses,

<meta>This page continues the "Paragone" or comparison of the arts, specifically focusing on Leonardo's argument that sight is the most noble sense and painting the most noble science because it deals directly with the works of nature rather than the artificial constructs of human language.</meta>

with truth and certainty, the works of nature, which words and letters cannot do: but letters prevail over painting in truly representing speech. Is it not a more admirable science, that which represents the work of nature, than that which gives us only the work of the operator—the work of men—such as words, poetry, and its forms, which manifest through human language? (LU. 7.) <note>LU refers to the "Libro di Pittura" or Treatise on Painting; Leonardo argues that painting is "divine" because it recreates the things created by God, whereas poetry only recreates human words.</note>

360. — The eye, through which the beauty of the universe is revealed to our contemplation, is of such excellence that whoever would resign themselves to its loss would deprive themselves of knowing all the works of nature. The sight of these works allows the soul to remain content within the prison of the body, thanks to the eyes, which represent to it the infinite variety of creation: he who loses them leaves this soul in a dark prison, where all hope of seeing the sun—the light of the universe—ceases. There are those for whom the darkness of night is hateful, even though it dissipates quickly. Oh! What would those people do if darkness were the companion of their entire lives?

Certainly, there is no one who would not rather lose their hearing and smell than their sight; for to lose the eye would be the loss of all the sciences <note>In Leonardo's time, "science" referred to any organized body of knowledge or skill based on principles.</note> that have their end in words. <note>Leonardo likely means that while words can describe knowledge, the visual observation of nature is the source of all true understanding.</note>

The sun shines so that the beauty of the universe, which is seen on the surface of bodies, is not lost.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's "Paragone" (comparison), specifically the debate over the superiority of painting over poetry. It builds on the previous page's argument that sight is the primary sense for understanding the universe.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>

accidental (artificial) as well as natural, reflected in the human eye. (*LU. 24.*) <note>LU. 24 refers to the Codex Urbinas, the primary source for Leonardo's Treatise on Painting.</note>

361. — Painting serves a worthier sense than poetry, and renders the figures of nature's works with greater truth. It is more worthy to imitate nature through true factual similarities than to imitate, with words, the deeds and sayings of men. And if you, poet, wish to describe the work of nature through your simple profession, inventing various actions and the forms of diverse things, you will be overcome by the infinitely more powerful painter. If you turn toward the other sciences, they are all unrelated to poetry—such as astrology <note>In the Renaissance, astrology and astronomy were often treated as a single discipline involving the study of celestial bodies.</note>, rhetoric, theology, philosophy, geometry, and arithmetic, and others. You are then no longer a poet; you are utterly astonished and no longer know what the subject is. Do you not see that if you wish to approach nature, you must employ the means of the sciences, invented by those who have studied the effects of the aforementioned nature?

The painter, by himself, without the aid of any other science or foreign means, goes immediately to the imitation of the very works of nature. Thus, lovers turn toward the image <term>simulacre: a representation or likeness that carries the presence of the original</term> of the beloved object and speak with the painting that represents it; thus, peoples turn with fervent vows and seek out the image of their gods, and not to see the works of the poets who have depicted these same

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting is superior to poetry because it directly imitates nature's forms without needing external sciences, noting that people instinctively pray to painted icons rather than poems.</summary>
<keywords>Paragone, painting vs poetry, imitation of nature, visual arts, Renaissance science, religious icons, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's "Paragone" (comparison), specifically the argument that painting is superior to poetry because it creates a physical presence that can deceive even animals, whereas poetry relies on the weaker faculty of imagination.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

gods with words. Thus are animals deceived: I once saw a painting that deceived a dog by its resemblance to his master, and the animal made a great celebration for this picture. I have also seen dogs bark and try to bite painted dogs; and a monkey perform a thousand follies for a painted monkey; and also swallows fly and try to perch on the painted iron bars depicted on the windows of buildings. (*LU. 14.*) <note>LU. 14 refers to the Codex Urbinas. Leonardo uses these anecdotes to prove that painting captures the "truth" of nature so well that it bypasses human reason and affects animal instinct.</note>

363. — The imagination does not see as excellently as the eye, which receives the aspect or <term>similitude: the visual likeness or formal quality of an object</term> of objects and transmits them to the sensibility, and from the sensibility to the <term>common sense: in Renaissance psychology, the internal faculty that processes and judges sensory data</term> which judges; the imagination does not go beyond this same common sense, except when it delivers itself to the memory; and the imagination shuts down if the thing imagined is not of great excellence. In this case, poetry is found in the imaginative work of the poet, who invents the same things as the painter, and through these fictions wishes to equal the artist. He remains far behind, as has been demonstrated above. In this case of fiction, I have said that the respective proportion of painting and poetry was that of the body and the shadow it casts. The difference is even more pronounced, given that the shadow of this body, at least through the eye, reaches the common sense, but the image—the mere notion of this body—does not enter there; it is born in the dark eye (the brain). <note>Leonardo distinguishes between the "eye of the body" (physical sight) and the "dark eye" (the mind's eye or imagination), arguing that the former is more direct and powerful.</note>

<vocab>painting, dog, monkey, swallows, imagination, eye, sensibility, common sense, memory, poetry, poet, fictions, artist, body, shadow, brain</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting's power to deceive animals proves its superiority over poetry, as it engages the physical senses directly rather than relying on the weaker, internal faculty of imagination.</summary>

Oh, what a difference there is between imagining such light in the inner eye and seeing it in fact, emerging out of the darkness!

If you, poet, were to depict a bloody battle, a melee, through a dark and gloomy atmosphere, with the smoke of terrible and murderous machines, the rising dust swirling in the air, and the frantic flight of the wretched, trembling at the prospect of a horrible death. In this case, the painter will prevail over you, because your pen will be exhausted before you can describe even summarily what the painter represents immediately with his science. And your tongue will be parched by thirst and your body overcome by sleep and hunger, before you have depicted in words what the painter shows in an instant (1).

In this painting, nothing is missing but the soul of the things represented, and in every body is the integrity of the part, which can be demonstrated through a single aspect.

What long and painful efforts would be required of poetry to render all the movements of the actors in a combat, and the details of the limbs and the ornaments of the weapons, which painting indicates with as much brevity as truth. And to this demonstration nothing is lacking

Let us say that <term>poetry</term> is a science that operates only upon the blind, and <term>painting</term> does the same for the deaf. It [painting] remains more worthy than <term>poetry</term>, for it serves a better sense.

The true office of the <term>poet</term> is to imitate the words that people say to one another, and that corresponds only to the sense of hearing, from a natural point of view. How, poet, will you describe a beauty without the representation of a living thing, and yet awaken such desires in men? If you say: "I will describe hell and paradise and other delights and horrors"—the painter surpasses you because he finds things which, although mute, will express such delights and such horrors and will move your soul!

Painting moves the senses more quickly than poetry. If you say that with words you will make a people laugh or cry, I will answer you that it is not you who moves them, but the orator and his mouth!

A painter makes a picture and whoever sees it immediately yawns; and this takes place every time the eye fixes upon the painting, which was made for that purpose <note>Leonardo argues that painting can trigger involuntary physiological responses, like yawning, more effectively than text.</note>.

Who is he who would not rather lose hearing, smell, and touch than sight? 363. — Animals suffer a worse injury by losing their sight than their hearing, for several reasons: the first is that by means of sight they find their food, which is indispensable to all animals; the second is that through sight one perceives the beauty of created things, the supreme point of that which induces love. In this regard, the man born blind can receive nothing through the ear, for he has never had knowledge of what beauty is in any form. Hearing is limited to perceiving only the voice and human speech, wherein are found the names of all things and where there is a name for each. Without the knowledge of these names, one can live quite contentedly, as we see with those born deaf and with the mute, who amuse themselves by means of drawing—especially the mute. <note>Original: LU. 16. Leonardo argues that visual representation is a universal language that precedes and surpasses spoken or written labels.</note>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's "Paragone" (Comparison of the Arts), specifically the debate regarding the superiority of painting over poetry by analyzing how each art form interacts with the human senses and the intellect.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...will show you the location of rivers, the groves, the valleys, and the countryside where your happy days were spent, with more truth than the painter?

If you say: — painting is a poetry that is mute in itself, and which cannot make what it represents speak; do you not see that your book finds itself at a worse degree? For even though there may be a man who speaks for it, one sees nothing of the things of which he speaks expressed as well as by painting; and if the scenes are well ordered with their <term>mental characters</term> <note>Original: "caractères mentaux." This refers to the internal thoughts, emotions, and intentions of the figures, which Leonardo believed should be visible through their physical gestures.</note>, they will be understood as if they were speaking.

Painting is a poetry that is seen instead of felt, and poetry is a painting that is felt instead of seen. These are two kinds of poetry—I mean to say two kinds of painting—which have different modes of reaching the <term>intelligence</term> <note>Original: "l'intelligence." In Renaissance psychology, this refers to the faculty of understanding and processing sensory data.</note>. Therefore, if both kinds of painting, to reach the <term>common sense</term> <note>Original: "sens commun." A philosophical concept of a central faculty in the brain where all the five senses meet and are processed.</note>, pass through the most noble sense, which is the eye; and if both kinds of poetry must pass through the less noble sense of the ear, we shall give painting to the judgment of the man born deaf, and poetry shall be judged by the man born blind. And if the painting is represented with the movements appropriate to the <term>moral characters</term> <note>Original: "caractères moraux." Refers to the ethical disposition or "soul" of the subjects, expressed through their outward actions.</note> of the figures acting in a determined sense, there is no doubt that the man born deaf will understand the work and the intentions of the author; but the man born blind will never understand...

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting is superior to poetry because it communicates through the eye, the "noblest sense," and can convey complex human emotions and narratives even to those who cannot hear.</summary>

when many diverse voices sing together at the same time; and from this results a harmonic proportion, which so satisfies the sense of hearing that the listeners remain in such vivid admiration, as if half-fainted <note>Leonardo uses the term "pâmés" to describe a state of ecstatic or overwhelmed sensory pleasure.</note>.

Much more will the proportioned beauty of an angelic face do, in a painting; from its proportions results a <term>concerted harmony</term> <note>Original: "harmonie concertante." A metaphor comparing the arrangement of facial features to a musical ensemble where different parts work together to create a single effect.</note>, which speaks to the eye at the same time as music speaks to the ear. And if such harmony of beauty is shown to one who loves the person who served as the model, that person will remain in stupefied admiration and incomparable joy, superior to that of all the other senses.

From poetry—which applies itself to the evocation of a perfect beauty through the description of each of the parts of which the aforementioned harmony in painting is composed—there does not result that other grace which is felt in music; each voice, by itself, at different times which do not achieve any concert, as if we wished to show an image, side after side, always covering the one shown first, becoming forgetful of the preceding one and leaving no proportion of harmony; thus the eye does not seize it with its visual power at one and the same time.

366. — Painting manifests itself immediately; it shows itself exactly as its author generated it, giving such pleasure to the <term>superior sense</term> <note>Leonardo considers sight the "superior sense" because it perceives the world directly and instantly, whereas hearing is "inferior" as it relies on time and sequence.</note> that no thing created by nature equals it. In this case, the poet—who shows the same things to the <term>common sense</term> <note>Original: "sens commun." In Renaissance psychology, this was the internal faculty where all sensory data was integrated and judged.</note> by way of hearing, an inferior sense—gives no other pleasure to the ear than if one were hearing a story told.

Now, see what a difference there is between hearing a story told of a thing that gives pleasure to the eye over a long duration of time, or with that swiftness which we find in natural things. And even though the works of poets are read over a long interval of time, very often they are not understood. They must be accompanied by various commentaries, and truly, the commentators rarely understand the spirit of the poet; and it further happens that readers decipher only a small part of the work for lack of time. The work of the painter reveals itself suddenly and in its entirety to its beholders.
*(LU. 22.)*

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>Continuing the argument from page 189, Leonardo further develops the "Paragone" (Comparison of the Arts), specifically contrasting the spatial harmony of painting with the temporal limitations of poetry and music.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

proportionality of the parts that compose all things; and thus it satisfies the senses. But poetry reports the same fact in a manner less worthy than the eye, and carries the figuration of the named things into the <term>sensibility</term> <note>Original: "sensibilité." Refers to the faculty of feeling or perceiving sensory input.</note> more confusedly. This does not happen to the eye, the true <term>interpreter</term> <note>Original: "truchement." Literally a spokesperson or mediator.</note> between the object and the sensibility, which conveys with the greatest truth the real surfaces and figures that it has previously represented to itself. From this is born the proportion of harmony which, with a sweet concert, satisfies the sense, just as if we created the proportion of diverse voices for the sense of hearing—which is less worthy than that of the eye, because it is born and dies at once, the one as quickly as the other. 

This cannot happen with vision; if you represent a human beauty, composed according to the proportion of beautiful limbs, this beauty is not as fleeting and ephemeral as in music; it allows itself to be seen and considered at length and in a permanent way. It is not reborn as music is, in a great deal of resonance that fatigues, but it charms, and all the senses are seduced along with the sight; one would wish to possess it and be like it; all the senses rise up in rivalry with the eye. If the mouth is such that one would wish for it in reality, the ear takes pleasure in hearing of its beauties; the sense of touch would wish to penetrate it through all its pores; the nose itself inhales the air that the mouth seems to continue to breathe.

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>Continuing the "Paragone" argument from page 190, Leonardo concludes his comparison of the senses by arguing that painting preserves beauty against the ravages of time and offers a simultaneous harmony that poetry, being sequential, cannot achieve.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

The beauty of such harmony is destroyed in a few years by time, which does not reach this same beauty when imitated by the painter: and thus it is preserved for a long time; and the eye, attending to its duty, takes such pleasure from so much figured beauty that it would have no more from living beauty.

Touch alone is not satisfied—touch, which acts as the best brother <note>Leonardo personifies the senses as siblings; touch is the "brother" to sight because they both deal with physical form, though touch requires the physical presence that painting lacks.</note> at the same time, because it will have had its intention; reason does not prevent the consideration of divine beauty. In this case, painting largely compensates for the original, which the description of the poet could not do. The poet might wish to equal the painter, without admitting that his words, in order to enumerate the members of such beauty, would require the time to separate them one from another; and without speaking of omissions, he would isolate the proportions that he cannot name without great prolixity; and being powerless to enumerate them, he cannot present the harmonious personality made of these divine proportions. In the same span of time in which the conception of a painted beauty is enclosed, one cannot produce a described beauty. He sins against nature who wishes, instead of using the eye, to replace it with the ear. Let the office of music come, and do not seek to replace the <term>science of painting</term> <note>Original: "science de la peinture." Leonardo viewed painting not just as a craft, but as a rigorous intellectual discipline based on optics and geometry.</note>, the true initiator of the natural figures of all things.

What does the poet answer? He yields to these reasons.

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting immortalizes beauty against the decay of time and provides a simultaneous perception of harmony that poetry's sequential nature cannot replicate. He concludes that trying to describe visual beauty through the ear is a "sin against nature."</summary>
<keywords>Paragone, aesthetics, divine proportions, harmony, temporal vs spatial arts, science of painting, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>Continuing the "Paragone" or "Comparison of the Arts," Leonardo moves from discussing the physical senses to the intellectual domains of the poet versus the painter, arguing that poetry is merely a collection of other disciplines rather than a primary science of its own.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

...but he <note>Referring to the poet.</note> claims to surpass the painter because he makes men speak and reason with various fictions in which he evokes things that do not exist; he will incite men to take up arms, he will describe the heavens, the stars, nature, the arts, and everything. The reply to this is that none of these things of which he speaks belong to his own profession, but that if he wishes to speak and harangue, he must realize that in this he will be defeated by the orator. If he treats of astrology <note>In Leonardo’s era, "astrology" often encompassed what we now call astronomy.</note> or philosophy, he borrows his subject from the astrologers and philosophers. Poetry has no domain that is truly its own and has no more merit than that of a merchant who assembles goods manufactured by various craftsmen.

When the poet ceases to represent <term>figuration</term> <note>Original: "figurer." To represent or give form to something through description.</note> with words that which is a fact in nature, he does not equal the painter; abandoning this representation, he describes the ornate and persuasive speech of the character he wishes to make speak, and in doing so he becomes an orator, and no longer a poet or a painter.

If he speaks of the heavens, he becomes an astronomer; a philosopher and a theologian if he treats of the things of nature and of God. But, if he returns to the representation of objects and makes himself the rival of the painter, he will not satisfy the eye with words.

The divinity of the <term>science of painting</term> <note>Leonardo's term for the intellectual and mathematical foundation of art.</note> considers human works as divine—at least all those that are defined within a surface.

**Vocabulary:**
<vocab>poet, painter, orator, astrology, philosophy, theologian, representation (figuration), science of painting, merchant</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that poetry lacks a unique subject matter, acting only as a "merchant" of other disciplines like philosophy or oratory, whereas painting possesses a unique, divine power to represent physical reality.</summary>
<keywords>Paragone, Leonardo da Vinci, poetry vs. painting, philosophy, oratory, science of painting, representation</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s defense of painting as the supreme science and art, following previous arguments regarding its relationship to nature and the senses.</meta>

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

By the lines that define bodies, painting dictates to the sculptor the perfection of his statues. With its fundamental principle, drawing, it teaches the architect how to make his building pleasing to the eye; it instructs those who make various vases, goldsmiths, weavers, and even pulley-makers <note>The original French transcription questions "fabricants de poulie" (pulley-makers); Leonardo likely refers to mechanical designers or craftsmen of complex tools</note>. It discovers the characters that express the most diverse languages. To painting we owe arithmetic signs; it has taught the representation of geometry, and it also teaches perspective to astrologers <note>In Leonardo's time, "astrology" often encompassed what we now call astronomy</note> and other inventors of machines and engineers. (*R. 652 and 167.*)

368. — The poet says that his science consists of invention and measure, which simply form the body of poetry: the invention of subject matter and the measure of the verses, which then reveal all the sciences. The painter replies that he considers the same objectives within the science of painting, namely invention and measure: the invention of the subject matter he must form and the measure of the things represented, without which they would not be proportioned.

But painting does not need to clothe itself in these three sciences <note>Likely referring to the liberal arts of the Trivium: grammar, rhetoric, and logic</note>, whereas poetry largely clothes itself in painting—just as astrology is based on perspective, which is the principal part of the painter's science; I mean mathematical astrology <note>original: "l'astrologie mathématique"; here Leonardo refers to the geometric calculation of celestial positions</note>.

The poet says that he describes one thing to represent another full of a beautiful sen—

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting is the foundational science for all visual and technical disciplines, from architecture to mathematics, and asserts that painting’s use of proportion and perspective is superior to the descriptive nature of poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, painting vs poetry, perspective, mathematical astrology, geometry, proportion, drawing, mechanical arts</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Paragone" or comparison of the arts, specifically focusing on Leonardo’s argument that painting is superior to poetry because it appeals directly to the eye and evokes more immediate emotional and spiritual responses.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

tence: the painter claims to do as much at his will, and in this, he is also a poet. If the poet claims to raise men to love, which is the principal goal of the appearance of all living beings, the painter has the power to do the same. He shows the lover the very effigie <term>effigie: a likeness or representation of a person</term> of the beloved object, which often resembles them so closely that one kisses it and speaks to it: something that would not happen if the same beauty were described by the writer. And he so far surpasses the genius of the poet that he induces the viewer to love, to become impassioned for paintings that do not even represent any living woman.

If the poet speaks to the senses by way of the ear, the painter addresses the eye.

I ask for nothing more than a good painter depicting the fury of a battle: let the poet do the same, and let them be placed in public, side by side. We shall see if the spectators do not stop longer, which they will consider more, where their praise will go, and what will satisfy them most? Certainly, painting will appear more useful and more beautiful, and will please more. Inscribe the name of God somewhere and place His image in confrontation with it; you will see where the veneration goes!

If painting embraces, within itself, all the forms of nature, you <note>Addressing the poets</note> have nothing left but names, which are universal like the forms. If you have the effects of the demonstration, we have the demonstration of the effects.

Take a poet, who describes the beauty of a

You have placed painting among the <term>mechanical arts</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "mechanical arts" were seen as manual labor or trades, lower in status than the "liberal arts" like rhetoric or music.</note>; certainly, if painters had known how to praise their own work with commentaries, as you do, I doubt that you would have inflicted such a lowly category upon them.

Why do you call it mechanical? Is it because it is manual—because the hands execute what the imagination conceives? You poets, also, draw with the pen, manually, that which passes through your mind.

And if you call it mechanical because it is done for a price, who falls into this error more than you—if it can even be called an error? If you lecture for the schools, do you not go to whoever pays you best? Do you perform any work without some benefit? Although I do not say this to criticize that opinion, for every labor expects a wage, a poet might say: "I shall create a fiction that signifies great things." ## PARALLEL OF PAINTING AND MUSIC

371. — Music should be called nothing 
other than the sister of painting, subor-
dinated to hearing, which follows after the eye. It com-
poses its harmony through the conjunction of its 
proportional parts at the same time, 
constrained to be born and to die, in one or sev-
eral harmonic intervals; and these moments sur-
round the proportion of the members, for this har-
mony is composed just as if one were tracing a 
circumference line, analogous to the contour of the 
limbs which engenders human beauty.

Painting surpasses music and governs it, 
because it does not suffer an immediate cessation 
from the moment of its creation, like adventurous mu-
sic, and thus remains in its essence; and you see 
a living fact upon a single surface.

O marvelous science, you preserve as living 
the perishable beauties of mortals, and they 
last longer than the works of na-
ture, which are continually subject to the variations 
of time that leads them toward feeble old 
age. This science has the same proportion in 
the face of divine nature as its works (of

372. — A thing is worthy in proportion to the sense it addresses; painting, which satisfies the sight, is therefore more noble than music, which only satisfies the ear.

What constitutes the nobility of a thing is its eternity; music, which is consumed even as it is born, does not equal painting, which, once vitrified <note>Leonardo likely refers here to the durability of certain artistic processes, such as enamel or glazed ceramics, which remain unchanged over time.</note>, becomes eternal.

The science which contains within itself the greatest universality and variety is the most excellent. Therefore, painting reveals itself as the overseer <note>original: "prévôt"</note> of every operation, for it contains all existing forms and others that nature does not provide; it should be magnified and exalted above music, which only commands the voice.

Painting provides the image of the gods, before which divine worship is celebrated—adorned by the music performed there; it provides lovers with the likeness of the beloved; it perpetuates beauty which time and generative nature make fleeting; it preserves the faces of famous men. And if you say that music remains as all art does, we do the same with your letters <note>Leonardo is refuting the argument that musical notation makes music "permanent," noting that written words (letters) can similarly record anything, but do not possess the immediate life of a painting.</note>.

Why have you placed music among the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>In the Renaissance, the 'Liberal Arts' were the seven prestigious subjects of higher learning (like logic and music). Painting was often excluded and dismissed as a 'mechanical art' or manual labor.</note>: either remove it or include <term>painting</term>.

It is as if music were spoiled by those who do not understand it. If you say: — non-mechanical sciences are those of the mind — I will tell you that painting is a mental science — just as music and geometry consider the proportion of continuous quantities, and arithmetic considers discontinuous ones: painting encompasses all continuous quantities as well as the quality of the proportions of light and distance within its perspective of shadow. <note>Leonardo da Vinci, Libro di Pittura (LU) 30 and 31.</note>

373. — The musician claims that his science is equivalent to that of the painter, because the latter composes a body of several members and the observer contemplates all its grace within a determined harmonic time, which is the moment it is born and dies: and yet it amiably delights the soul residing within the body of its beholder.

But the painter responds and says that the body, composed of members, does not by itself give pleasure in harmonic times, in the space of which beauty is born and dies; it does so permanently, giving so much and with such excellence, that painting preserves like a rite <note>The word "rite" here suggests a sacred or ceremonial preservation of form.</note> that harmony of the proportion of members which nature, even by employing all its forces, could not maintain.

had prematurely destroyed the natural original; and the work of the painter has remained more worthy than that of nature, his mistress.

If you were to say, O musician, that painting is a mechanical art <note>In the Renaissance, "mechanical arts" were considered manual labor, while "liberal arts" were intellectual pursuits.</note> because it is performed with the use of the hand: what then? Music is performed with the mouth, yet not for the sake of the sense of taste, any more than the hand acts for the sake of the sense of touch.

Words are worth even less than deeds. 374. — In representing corporeal things, the painter and the poet differ as much as dismembered bodies differ from complete bodies. The poet, in the description of the beauty or the brutality of a body, will show it to you member by member and through successive glimpses, whereas the painter will make you see it whole and all at once. The poet cannot, with words, arrange the true shape of the members that form a whole, whereas the painter produces it with all the truth possible to nature. It happens to the poet as it does to the musician who sings alone a piece composed for four voices; first, he sings the first part, then the tenor, then the contralto, and finally the bass; the grace of the proportions does not result from this—

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting is an intellectual science rather than a manual craft, asserting that it captures the "whole" of a subject simultaneously, whereas poetry and music can only reveal parts sequentially.</summary>

To provide the likeness of a beautiful face, the poet shows it to you feature by feature; in doing so, you will not grasp the impression of its beauty, which consists of the divine proportion manifested by these members in their totality—which at that same moment manifest this divine harmony resulting from their union, so powerful that it overwhelms the viewer.

Music also creates, in its harmonic time, sweet melodies composed of its varied voices—of which the poet is deprived because of their harmonic division <note>Leonardo suggests that because poetry is read or heard one word at a time, it cannot achieve the "chord-like" layering of music or painting.</note>.

Although poetry enters through the sense of hearing to the seat of judgment, just as music does, the poet cannot describe the harmony of music; he lacks the power to say several things simultaneously, as does the harmonic proportion of painting, which is composed of various members within a single action.

The charm acts instantaneously for the detail, as it does for the whole.

Generally, as much for the understanding of the composition as for the specific understanding of the component parts that form the whole, the poet remains—with regard to the <term>figuration</term> <note>Figuration: The representation of physical forms and the human figure in visual art.</note> of bodily things—far behind the painter, just as the musician remains short of invisible things. 376. — It is told that on the anniversary of the birth of King Matthias <note>King Matthias Corvinus of Hungary (1443–1490), a famous patron of the arts and sciences.</note>, a poet produced a work in celebration of the day he was born for the good of the world; at the same time, a painter presented the monarch with a portrait of his mistress. > "O king, read, read, and you will experience something of greater substance than this mute painting."

The king, delivering a judgment in response to this reproach for looking at a mute thing, said: 
> "Be silent, O poet, you know not what you say. This painting serves a better sense than yours, which is for the blind. Sculpture is subordinate to certain lights, as I have said, while paint— <note>The text cuts off here as "pein-" (peinture), continuing on the next page.</note>

<header>SELECTED TEXTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

nature always carries with it its own shadows and lights; and this question of shadow and light is vital in sculpture.

The sculptor is aided by the nature of the relief which generates the <term>modeling</term> <note>The representation of three-dimensional forms through light and shadow</note> on its own; the painter, through accidental art, creates it in the places where nature would reasonably do so. The sculptor cannot diversify the various characters of color, whereas painting produces them all. The perspective of sculptors has no truth: that of painters reaches a distance of a hundred miles. <term>Aerial perspective</term> <note>The technique of representing distance by modifying colors to simulate the effect of the atmosphere</note> is missing from their works. (*Ashburnham Manuscript* I. 25, *recto* and *verso*.)

They cannot represent transparent bodies, nor luminous ones, nor reflected forms, nor <term>lucid bodies</term> <note>Objects that emit or reflect light clearly</note> such as mirrors and other brilliant objects, nor clouds, nor darkness; and many natural effects are impossible for them.

Sculpture resists time better; so be it: although painting done on copper covered with white enamel and executed with enamel colors, passed through fire and baked, is just as durable, and even surpasses sculpture. One might say that if one makes an error with this process, it is difficult to repair. It is a poor argument to try to prove that a process where retouching is impossible makes the work more worthy. I would certainly say that the invention of the master is more difficult to correct if he makes such an error than to correct

the work itself is thus spoiled. We know well
that this process is good and we shall not make an er-

Therefore this painting made on copper with the

If the bronze statue is eternal, paint-

Sculpture with little labor shows
that it does not have the miraculous character of
painting which makes the impalpable palpable, puts in
relief a flat surface and brings the distant near.
Painting is adorned with infinite speculations
unknown to sculpture.

378. — Sculpture is not a science, but a mechanical art <note>In the Renaissance, "mechanical arts" were considered manual trades, inferior to the "liberal arts" which required intellectual study.</note> that produces sweat and bodily fatigue in its practitioner; it requires of him only the simple measurements of the limbs and the nature of movements and poses, and its domain ends there. It shows the eye the object exactly as it is, without challenging the wonder of its observer in the way that painting does—which, upon a flat surface, by force of science, reveals the most vast landscapes with their distant horizons. (*LU. 35.*)

379. — Between painting and sculpture I find no other difference, except that the sculptor carries out his work with great physical fatigue, while the painter applies to his work a greater mental effort.

The proof that this is true is that the sculptor, to create his work, uses the strength of his arms to strike and shape the marble or other hard stone from which the figure will emerge, which is as if enclosed within it. This is an entirely mechanical labor that keeps him constantly in a sweat; he is covered in dust and debris, and his face becomes pasty and caked with marble dust like a baker's assistant <note>original: "mitron" — a young apprentice baker, typically covered in white flour.</note>. Furthermore, peppered with small stone chips, he appears covered in flakes of snow, and his filthy dwelling is filled with rubble and stone dust.

It is quite the opposite for the painter, according to what is said of famous artists. At his ease he is

380. — No comparison is possible between the genius, the artifice <note>In this context, "artifice" refers to the technical skill and ingenuity of the artist rather than deception.</note>, and the discourse of painting and those of sculpture, which does not concern itself with perspective—the latter being caused by the property of the material and not by artifice.

If the sculptor says that he cannot replace material once it has been removed as excessive, in the manner that a painter can, I will answer him that he who removes too much does not know his art well and is not a master.

If he had taken his measurements, he would not remove what ought to remain: that is merely a fault of the operator and not a defect of nature. Let us not speak of those who are not masters, but merely spoilers of marble.

Masters do not trust the judgment of the eye, for the eye is constantly deceived, as is proven if one tries to divide a line into two equal parts by a simple glance; often one is mistaken.

381. — Painting involves greater mental reasoning, higher ingenuity, and is more marvelous than sculpture: for necessity forces the mind of the painter to transmute the very mind of nature into his own mind, and to be the interpreter between nature and art, explaining with her the causes of her demonstrations, following her laws. 384. — One can truly say that those who call a painter a master are mistaken if that painter only knows how to do a head or a figure well. Certainly, there is nothing extraordinary in achieving a certain perfection if one constantly studies only a single thing.

But we who know that painting embraces and contains within itself everything produced by nature and everything conceived by human effort—and finally, everything the eye can grasp—it seems to us a poor master who only knows how to do a single figure well.

Do you not see how varied the actions of men are? How many diverse animals there are? Trees, plants, and flowers? What a variety of landscapes—flat or mountainous? And the fountains, the rivers, the islands, the public monuments, the houses, all the instruments for human use, and man's varied and adorned clothing and his arts?

All this belongs, by <term>similarity of operation</term> <note>The concept that the painter's creative process mirrors the generative power of Nature herself</note>, to the good painter; he deserves to be called such only if he makes use of all of these elements. (ASH. II. 25, v.) <note>ASH refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, now held in the Institut de France.</note>

390. — What true master would claim to be able to observe by himself all the forms and effects of nature? Such a person would seem to me a model of ignorance; for these forms and effects are of such diversity that our memory does not have the capacity necessary to retain them.

->**Studies of the Horse and Rider**<-

The **horse** <note>original: "cheval"</note> must be understood not merely as a beast of burden, but as a masterpiece of biological engineering. In these **sketches** <note>original: "esquisse"</note>, we observe the tension of the musculature during various maneuvers of **horsemanship** <note>original: "équitation"</note>.

The **rider** <note>original: "cavalier"</note> must maintain a center of gravity that complements the animal's own. These **anatomical studies** <note>original: "études anatomiques"</note> illustrate the precise alignment of the spine and the distribution of weight across the haunches.

### Observations on Motion

| Movement | Description of Form | Technical Note |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| The Trot | Symmetrical diagonal movement | Ensure the head remains steady |
| The Gallop | A three-beat gait with suspension | <insert>Watch the extension of the forelegs</insert> |
| The Levade | The horse stands on its hind legs | <term>Levade</term> <note>A classical dressage move where the horse raises its forehand from the ground at a 45-degree angle.</note> |

391. — We know well that errors appear to us more readily in the works of others than in our own, and while rebuking the small faults of another, you will remain ignorant of your own, which are great.

To ward off such blindness, first establish a sound perspective; then take notice of the measurements of man and other animals; observe as a good architect, as much as you are able, the form of buildings and other things which present an infinity of forms upon the earth. The more you possess sound knowledge, the more your work will be praiseworthy, and especially if you have followed and not rejected the image of nature. (*ASH. I. 28, r.*)

392. — Certainly, one must not reject the judgment of anyone while painting man; we know well that a man, even if he is not a painter, has a notion of the forms of another man, and will judge well if he is hunchbacked, or if his shoulder is high or low, or if he has a large mouth or a large nose and other defects.

393. — There is a certain kind of painter who, through their lack of study, complain about living without the beauty of gold and azure. <note>Azure refers to ultramarine, an extremely expensive pigment made from lapis lazuli; Leonardo is critiquing artists who blame their lack of talent on a lack of luxury materials.</note>

Their extreme folly alleges a lack of payment as a reason for not producing the good things they would do just as well as anyone else, if they were well paid. See, what a stupid breed! They see nothing good to be done in such blindness, saying: "This one is at a high price, and that one at a medium price, and this other at a low price," to show that they could work for the very best <insert>if they were paid accordingly</insert>. (*ASH. I. 25, r.*)

->*The use of the mirror.*<-

394. — When you wish to see if your painting as a whole conforms to the natural object, take a mirror and have it reflect the living model; compare this reflection to your work, and see clearly if the original conforms to the copy.

And above all, take the mirror for a mas- <note>The word is cut off; likely "maître" or "master." Leonardo famously called the mirror the "master of painters" because it provides a fresh, reversed perspective that reveals errors.</note>

<meta>This page concludes Leonardo’s thoughts on the mirror as a tool for artistic verification, completing the word "master" (maître) from the previous page, and moves into a botanical metaphor regarding the nourishment of artistic skill through the study of nature.</meta>

<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<page-num>219</page-num>

-ter, <note>Completing the word "master" (maître) from the previous page.</note> because on a flat mirror, objects project themselves in many ways similar to how they appear in a painting.

You see how a painting, executed on a flat surface, represents things that appear in relief <note>Relief refers to the three-dimensional quality of an object appearing to stand out from the background.</note>; the mirror on a flat surface does the same. A painting, like a mirror, is a single surface: it is impalpable, like a reflection that appears round and brilliant yet cannot be grasped with the hands, no more than a painting can. Both the mirror and the canvas show the likeness of things through their shadows and lights, and both offer the same flat surface.

And if you understand that the mirror—by means of the lines <note>original: "linéament" – the boundaries or contours created by the meeting of light and shadow.</note> of shadows and lights—shows you the object standing out; and if you know how to combine them well so that your painting possesses, through its colors, shadows and lights even more powerful than those in the mirror; then your painting will appear as a natural thing seen in a large mirror. <note>original: "ASH. I. 24, v." refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, folio 24 verso.</note>

395. — Every branch and every fruit is born from the bud of its leaf, which serves as a mother to provide it with the water of nature and the moisture of the dew that falls from the sky at night; often, it also protects it from the excessive heat of the sun's rays. Therefore, you, painter—who do not have such a rule to escape the criticism of connoisseurs—be fervent in copying every natural thing; do not disdain study, as those who are greedy for profit do. <note>original: "G. 33, r et v." refers to Leonardo's Manuscript G, folio 33 recto and verso.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that the mirror is the painter's ultimate guide because it shares the same two-dimensional nature as a canvas while accurately representing three-dimensional depth. He also uses the metaphor of a leaf protecting its fruit to urge artists to nurture their talent through the diligent study of nature rather than focusing on financial gain.</summary>

396. — Men and words are facts. And you, painter, by not knowing how to compose your figures, you resemble the orator who does not know how to order his words. *(ASH. I. 17, v.)* <note>Leonardo frequently compared painting to poetry and rhetoric to elevate the status of the visual arts.</note>

397. — The young artist must first learn <term>perspective</term> <note>The mathematical method of representing three-dimensional space on a flat surface</note>; then the measurements of all things; after that, from the hand of a good master <insert>from drawings</insert>, learn to draw the limbs. Then he moves to nature to verify the accuracy of what he has learned; he then looks, for a time, at the works of various masters, in order to become accustomed to the practice and the work. *(K. 110, v.)*

398. — I say that one must first study the limbs and their function. That done, one follows the movements according to the circumstances that happen to man; thirdly, one composes the stories. For this, one will study natural movements such as similar circumstances produce; and one will observe, through the streets, squares, and countryside, noting with a brief description the <term>lineaments</term> <note>The characteristic physical outlines or features of a person</note>. For example, for a head make an O and for an arm a straight and bent line, and likewise for the legs and the torso; then returning home, put these <term>sketches</term> into perfect form.

The detractor will say that to do this practically and in a sufficient manner, it is better that this study begin by...
