<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s instructions on artistic training, specifically addressing the debate between copying other masters versus studying directly from nature, and the necessity of keeping a sketchbook.</meta>

<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<page-num>221</page-num>

varied compositions, made on paper or on walls by various masters, and that by following them one acquires a rapid practice and an excellent habit.

To which it is replied that this method would be good if followed after good compositions and learned masters; but such masters are so rare, one finds so few of them, that it is safer to go to natural things than to those very works which have been poorly imitated from nature and thus acquire a bad habit; he who can go to the fountain does not go to the water-jar. <note>original: "qui peut aller à la fontaine, ne va pas à la cruche." A favorite proverb of Leonardo's, emphasizing that nature is the primary source of truth, while art that copies art is a secondary, often degraded, source.</note> (*C.A. 196, v.*)

399. — When you know <term>perspective</term> <note>The science of representing depth and volume on a flat surface</note> well and you have in your mind all the parts of the bodies of things, be an observer; and often, in the places where you go for recreation, study the positions and the actions of men—their way of speaking, of laughing, or of fighting; what attitudes they take, what gestures they make, the circumstances, the disputes, and the spectators. And note with brief signs all these features in your little notebook, which you must always carry with you. Let it be of tinted paper, not for erasing, but for changing the old for the new; and these are not things to be rubbed out, but preserved with great diligence, because there is such an infinity of forms and actions that the memory cannot retain them; and these sketches will serve you as your authors and masters. (*C. A. 27, v.*) <note>Leonardo refers to his sketches as "authors," suggesting they are the primary authorities or "texts" from which an artist should learn.</note>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, perspective, drawing, sketchbook, observation, Codex Atlanticus, study of nature</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that artists should prioritize studying nature over copying other masters and provides practical advice on using a pocket sketchbook to record human behavior and anatomy in real-time.</summary>

400. — It has been proven to me that it is of great use, when you find yourself at rest in bed, to go in spirit, representing to yourself the surface outlines of forms according to your most recent studies and other notable circumstances belonging to the domain of <term>subtle speculation</term> <note>Leonardo refers to "speculativa," the intellectual and theoretical side of painting that involves deep mental analysis of how light, shadow, and form work together.</note>.
It is a laudable and useful act, very well suited to confirming things in the memory. (*Ashburnham I, 26, recto*)

401. — I shall not fail to include, among these precepts, a new method of theory, although it may seem petty and almost ridiculous; yet it is very effective and useful for disposing the mind toward various inventions. It is this: if you look at certain walls stained with various spots and made of mixed stones, and you have to invent some setting, you will be able to see on that wall the resemblance of various landscapes, with their mountains, their rivers, their rocks, trees, plains, wide valleys, and hills in various aspects; you will be able to see there battles and lively movements of figures, and strange expressions of faces, and costumes, and a thousand other things which you can then reduce to good and complete form.

It happens with such stained and mixed walls as it does with the sound of bells, whose strokes make you think of every name and every word that you can imagine.

406. — The painter who translates by practice and judgment of the eye, without reasoning, is like a mirror, where the most opposing things are imitated without any cognition of their essence. <note>original: "C. A. 76, r." — refers to the Codex Atlanticus.</note>

407. — And you, painter, who desire to achieve the greatest mastery <term>practice: the technical execution and skill of an artist</term>, you must understand that if you do not base it upon the sound foundation of natural things, you will produce a mediocre work, of little honor and little duration. If you build it well, your work will bring you much honor and utility. <note>original: "LU. 750."</note>

408. — You must use these rules only to correct your figures. For every man, in his first composition, makes some error, and if he is unaware of it, he does not correct it. Therefore, to know your errors, critique your work; where you find errors, correct them, and let your warned mind not fall into them again. If you wish to apply the rules of composition from the very start, no, you will never reach the end and you will cast confusion into your work.

These rules ensure that you possess a free and sound judgment; sound judgment is born from sound understanding, and sound understanding derives from reason treated according to good rules, and good rules are the daughters of sound experience.

## HISTORICAL OVERVIEW OF PAINTING

409. — Painting declines and is lost from age to age when painters have no other authority than the painting that came before.
The painter will produce a work of little excellence if he takes the painting of others as his master; but if he draws inspiration from nature, he will bear good fruit. We see that since the time of the Romans, painters imitated one another, and thus from age to age, they continually brought about the decline of this art.
Later came Giotto: this Florentine, born among solitary mountains inhabited only by goats and similar beasts, sensing that the face of nature was akin to art, began to draw upon the rocks the poses of the goats he tended, and continued to depict all the animals he found in the countryside; in such a way that after much study, he surpassed not only the masters of his own time but those of many centuries past. <note>Giotto di Bondone (c. 1267–1337) is often credited with sparking the Italian Renaissance by moving away from stylized Byzantine art toward a more naturalistic style based on observation.</note>
Since Giotto, art fell once more, because everyone <insert>imitated the works already created</insert>...

<summary>Leonardo argues that artistic excellence is only achieved by studying nature directly, citing the decline of art after the Romans and its revival by Giotto as evidence. He warns that imitating other artists rather than nature leads to the inevitable decay of the craft.</summary>
<keywords>art history, Giotto di Bondone, nature vs. imitation, Renaissance theory, perspective, artistic decline, Codex Atlanticus</keywords>

imitate paintings already made; and so, from century to century, decadence continues until Tomaso, the Florentine nicknamed Masaccio <note>Tommaso di Ser Giovanni di Simone (1401–1428), a key figure of the early Italian Renaissance known for his realism</note>, who showed by his perfect work that those who take for their authority anyone other than nature, the mistress of masters, labor in vain.

This applies also to mathematics, which some study only in the works of authors and not in the works of nature; they are the nephews and not the sons of nature, who is the mother of all good authors.

I detest the extreme foolishness of those who blame the colors with which nature clothes herself, setting aside the authors who are the disciples of nature herself. (*C. A. 141, r.*) <note>Reference to the Codex Atlanticus, Leonardo's largest collection of drawings and writings</note>

410. — Even though the prosperity of the body does not spoil that of the mind, the <term>painter</term> <note>original: "peintre"; here referring to the artist as a philosopher of sight</note> or the <term>draftsman</term> <note>original: "dessinateur"; one who masters 'disegno' or design/drawing</note> must be solitary, especially when he is intent on observation and careful attention, so that everything presenting itself to his eyes may continually provide material for the memory to be well-circumspect.

If you are alone, you will belong entirely to yourself; if you are accompanied by even one person, you will belong to yourself only by half, or even less; and as the indiscretion of your company increases, the inconvenience will grow. If you should say: — "I will act in my own way; I will stay apart to better observe the forms of natural things," — I tell you that you can only do poorly, for you cannot prevent yourself from

411. — The mind of the painter must be like a mirror, which constantly transforms itself into the colors of the things it reflects, and which fills itself with as many images as there are objects placed before it.

419. — <term>Painting</term> first establishes its scientific and true principles: what is the <term>shadowy body</term>, what is <term>primary shadow</term><note>The shadow on the object itself.</note> and <term>derivative shadow</term><note>original: "ombre dérivative"; the cast shadow projected onto another surface.</note>, and what is light? All of this forms the <term>science of painting</term> which resides in the mind of those who contemplate it; from this, the operation is born which is truly worthy of the aforementioned contemplation or science. <note>original: "LU. 33." refers to the Libro di Pittura (Treatise on Painting).</note>

420. — I say: universal measurements must be observed in the length of figures, but not in their thickness: because, among the praiseworthy and marvelous things that appear in the work of nature, each operates within each species by itself; yet without the particularities of precision, they would resemble one another. 421. — The sovereign pleasure of masters consists in remaking the same themes of the same histories. They are neighbors to one another, and also beauty for the eyes is always the same. But these are never found repeated in nature, so that if all beauties of equal excellence were to become living again, there would be a greater number of beings than that which our century counts; and as in this century no one exactly resembles another, the same <term>disparity</term><note>The natural variation and difference between individuals.</note> would appear among these beauties. <note>original: "LU. 270." refers to the Codex Urbinas, section 270.</note>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's critique of painters who repeat themselves, specifically addressing the psychological phenomenon where an artist unconsciously reproduces their own physical likeness and temperament in their work.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

often filled with admiration because I have known none who, in all their figures, appear to have portrayed them after nature.

In them, one sees the attitudes and the manners of their author.

And, if he is quick to speak and lively in manner, his figures will have the same character. If the master is devout, then the characters will have twisted necks <note>A common artistic shorthand in the Renaissance for religious ecstasy or humility.</note>, and if the master is lazy, the figures will express laziness naturally; and if the master is well-proportioned, the figures will be so as well, and if he is foolish in his compositions, that is widely seen; those of the student reveal themselves as enemies of any conclusion and inattentive to their task, and look here and there as if they were dreaming; and each of the characters of the painting is one of the characters of the painter.

Having often considered the cause of such a defect, it appears to me to be this: the soul that rules and governs each body, if it is what forms our judgment, is rather our own judgment. Therefore, it has guided the entire figure of the man, as it decided he should be, with a long, short, or snub nose, just as it fixed the height of his body.

This judgment has such power that it leads the arm of the painter to reproduce himself to obey this soul; and this is the true way to represent man, and whoever does not do as it does, errs.

<summary>Leonardo explains the psychological tendency of artists to unconsciously project their own physical traits and personality—their "soul"—into the figures they paint. He argues that an artist's personal judgment acts as a blueprint that shapes their work in their own image unless they are vigilant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of the psychological and physical bond between the artist and their work. If the soul encounters someone who resembles the body that it has composed, it falls in love with them. Thus, many fall in love with and marry women who resemble themselves; and the children born of these marriages resemble their parents. (*LU. 108.*) <note>"LU" refers to the *Codex Urbinas*, the primary manuscript containing Leonardo's Treatise on Painting.</note>

423. — The painter must base his figures on the rules of a living body that possesses, in its entirety, commendable proportions. Furthermore, he must have himself measured to see in what parts his own physique deviates—whether much or little—from this ideal model. Having made this discovery, he must take care, with all his concentration, not to repeat these same physical shortcomings in the figures he creates.

Know that you must struggle against this vice <note>Leonardo uses "vice" here to mean a habitual artistic error or a natural instinctual bias.</note>, for such a failing is born alongside your own faculty of judgment. The soul, as the mistress of your body, is the source of your judgment; she naturally takes pleasure in works that resemble her and creates according to the very composition of the body she inhabits.

This is why there is no woman so plain-featured that she cannot find a lover, provided she is not monstrous. <note>In the Renaissance, "monstrous" referred to significant physical deformities or features that completely defied the laws of natural symmetry.</note>

Therefore, remember to keep the defects of your own person in mind, and take care not to transfer them to the figures you compose. (*LU. 109.*)

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s observations on the relationship between physical form and internal expression, moving from comparative anatomy to the psychological depiction of figures in art.</meta>

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

Terrestrial animals have a similarity of limbs, muscles, and bones; and if they vary, it is only in length and thickness, as will be demonstrated in the *Anatomy* <note>Leonardo's projected but never fully published treatise on human and animal dissection</note>. Next are the aquatic animals, which are of great variety; regarding these, I do not advise the painter to establish a fixed rule, because they are of infinite variety, just like insects. (*LU. 107.*)

425. — One may paint Fame <note>original: "la renommée"</note> covered entirely with tongues, winged and in the shape of a bird. (*ASH. II. 22, v.*)

426. — In painting, the depicted figures must be made in such a way that the observers can easily recognize, by their attitudes, the concept of their soul <note>The "motions of the mind" or internal thoughts expressed through external movement</note>. And if you have to represent a good man speaking, let his gestures be those that accompany virtuous words; and similarly, if you have to represent a brutish man, do it with abrupt movements, thrusting his arms toward the listener, with the head and chest protruding beyond the feet, agitated like the hands of the speaker: in the manner of a mute person who, seeing two people talking, although deprived of hearing, understood the subject of their dispute through no other interpreter than the actions and attitudes of the speakers.

I saw, in Florence, a man who became deaf by accident, who could not hear you even if you spoke loudly; yet he understood you if you spoke slowly without any sound of the voice, by the movement of the lips alone.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the anatomical similarities between land animals and provides instructions on how a painter should use physical gestures and "lip-reading" to convey a character's internal thoughts and emotions.</summary>
<keywords>Anatomy, painting theory, Fame, gestures, non-verbal communication, deafness, Florence, expression of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s instructions on how a painter must capture the internal thoughts and emotions of a subject through external physical movement, specifically focusing on the observation of the lips and the expressive power of the deaf and mute.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

Now, one might say to me: — he who speaks loudly moves his lips in the same way as he who speaks softly, and consequently, if they move their lips identically, they must be understood one like the other. To this, I let experience <note>Leonardo frequently champions "Esperienza" (direct observation) over theoretical dogma</note> give the verdict: have someone speak softly and then loudly, and observe the lips. (*C. A.* 139, *r.*)

427. — Apply your mind, while walking, to observing the faces of men and women in the evening when the weather is bad; what grace and sweetness are seen in those faces! (*ASH.* I. 21, *r.*)

428. — The good painter has two principal things to realize, namely: man and the concept of his mind <note>original: "le concept de son esprit"; Leonardo's famous "moti mentali" or motions of the mind</note>. The first is easy, the second difficult, because he must represent it through the gestures and the play of the limbs; and this must be learned from the mute, who do it better than any other sort of men. (*LU.* 180.)

429. — The figures of men have actions proper to their operation, so that in seeing them, you understand what they think and what they say. Those would be well-taught who imitated the movements of the mute, who speak with the movement of the hands and the eyes and the eyebrows and their whole person in their desire to express the concept of their soul. And do not mock me because I propose to you a preceptor without a tongue who

<vocab>painter, concept of the mind, the mute, gestures, tongueless preceptor, movement of the hands, faces, experience</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues that a painter must study the expressive gestures of the mute to learn how to depict a figure's internal thoughts and soul. He also notes how lighting conditions, such as twilight or bad weather, can reveal a particular grace in human faces.</summary>
<keywords>expression, gestures, motions of the mind, sign language, observation, painting technique, facial expressions, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the previous section where Leonardo discusses the "tongueless preceptor"—the mute person—as the ultimate model for a painter seeking to understand how physical gestures reveal the internal thoughts of the soul.</meta>

will know how to teach you this art that he does not know, because he will teach you with facts while others would only have words. This advice should not be despised, for the mute are the masters in the matter of movement; they understand from afar what someone is speaking about when that person accompanies his words with movements of the hands. (*LU.* 115.) <note>original: "LU." refers to the "Libro di Pittura," also known as the Codex Urbinas.</note>

430. — You shall make the figure in such an action that it is sufficient to demonstrate what the character has in his soul; otherwise, your work will not be praiseworthy. (*ASH.* I. 20, *r.*) <note>original: "ASH." refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts; "r." stands for "recto," the front side of a manuscript leaf.</note>

431. — A figure is not praiseworthy if it does not display the act that expresses its passion. This figure will be all the more praiseworthy as the action better expresses its passion. (*ASH.* I. 27, *r.*)

432. — Human movements are as varied as the accidents <term>accidents: in Renaissance art theory, these are the passing emotions or external events that affect the mind's state</term> that occur in their spirit; and each accident by itself acts more or less upon men, depending on whether they are of greater or lesser individuality and according to their age; for, in the same situation, a young person will not make the same movement as an old one. (*LU.* 373.)

433. — Thus must the ages of man be divided: infancy, childhood, adolescence, youth, old age, and decrepitude.

Old men must be depicted with slow and short movements, the leg bent at the knee when they are stopped, and their feet parallel to one another

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, movement, expression, passion, ages of man, gesture, the mute, painting</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo emphasizes that a painter's primary goal is to represent the "passions" and "accidents" of the soul through physical movement, using the expressive gestures of the mute as a guide. He also introduces the concept of tailoring movement to the specific age and character of the subject, from infancy to decrepitude.</summary>
<keywords>artistic expression, motions of the soul, physical gesture, human development, physiognomy, Renaissance painting theory, observation of the mute</keywords>

Women should be represented with demure attitudes, their legs held close together; their arms gathered, and the head somewhat low and tilted to one side.

Old women should be represented as lively and quick, with angry movements similar to those of the infernal Furies <note>In Greek and Roman mythology, the Furies were deities of vengeance known for their wild, terrifying appearance and relentless energy.</note>; these movements should manifest more in the arms and the head than in the legs.

Very small children should be shown with quick and sprawling attitudes when they play, and when at rest, upright with shy and fearful attitudes. (*ASH. I, 17, v.*) <note>original: "ASH." refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts; "v." stands for "verso," the back side of a manuscript leaf.</note>

# XIII

# BESTIARY AND BOOK OF BIRDS (1)

## BESTIARY

***Lion.***

439. — This animal, with its thundering cry, awakens its cubs on the third day after their birth, to teach them all that their sleeping senses are ignorant of; and the beasts that are in the forest flee.

One can liken the sons of virtue to lion cubs who, thanks to the cry of glory, awaken and rise in honorable studies, more and more; and all the villains flee at this cry, distancing themselves from the virtuous.

The lion covers its tracks so that its enemies remain ignorant of its path. It is fitting for captains to conceal the secrets of their souls so that

---

1. *The Flower of Virtue, which treats all human vices and how one must acquire virtue*, Venice, 1474, is the principal source from which Leonardo drew. One easily finds the Rome edition of 1740. — Furthermore, the master possessed the Pliny of Christoforo Landino <note>Pliny the Elder's *Natural History*</note> translated from the Latin language into Florentine, Venice, 1476.

### Felines.

440. — Lions, leopards, panthers, and tigers keep their claws in a sheath and only extend them to seize prey or an enemy.
When the lioness defends her young against the hands of hunters, so as not to be terrified by the sight of the spears, she lowers her eyes to the ground; and through her flight, her sons are not taken prisoner. <note>Leonardo suggests that by looking away from the weapons, the lioness maintains her courage to lead her cubs to safety.</note>
(H. 2, *recto*)

441. — The lion, that terrible animal, fears nothing so much as the noise of empty carts and the crowing of the cock; he also fears the sight <note>of the cock</note> and with a fearful expression looks at his mane and is much frightened when he has covered his face.
(H. 23, *recto*)

### Anger.

442. — It is said that when the bear goes to the hive to take the honey, the bees begin to sting him and they abandon the honey to run toward vengeance; and the whole swarm flies upon him and bites him, and takes revenge in such a way that his food is converted into rage; and lying on the ground, he flails his four limbs, without managing to defend himself.
(H. 6, *recto*)

### Panther.

443. — The panther has the form of a lioness but is higher on its legs, thinner and longer,

white with a sprinkling of black spots in a round shape; all animals delight in seeing her and would be constantly around her if not for the terribleness of her gaze. ### *Camel.*

444. — Bactrian camels <note>original: "Battriens"</note> have two humps, those of the Arabs one; they go quickly into combat and carry burdens very usefully.

This animal has a rule and measures that it observes; it does not rise if it is more heavily loaded than is appropriate, and if the journey is too long, it suddenly stops. The merchants are then forced to lighten its load. (*H. 23, verso*)

### *Tiger.*

445. — The tiger is born in Hyrcania <note>original: "Icarnie"; an ancient region southeast of the Caspian Sea famous in classical literature for its tigers</note>, similar to the panther by the various spots on its skin; it is of an astonishing velocity.

The hunter, when he finds its cubs, takes them, placing mirrors in the place from which he departs, and on a rapid horse he flees. The tiger, upon returning, finds the mirrors placed on the ground and, seeing itself there, it seems to see its cubs.

Scratching with its paw, it discovers the deception and, by scent, it follows the hunter. The latter

### *The Elephant.*

446. — Nature has endowed it with qualities that men rarely possess, namely: integrity <term>probity: the quality of having strong moral principles and honesty</term>, prudence, fairness, and religious observance.

At the new moon, they go to the river and, to purify themselves, they bathe solemnly as if they were greeting the planet <note>In the 15th century, the Moon was classified as a planet within the geocentric model of the universe</note>, then return to the forest.

When ill, they throw themselves on their backs and lie on the grass looking at the sky, as if they wished to offer themselves as a sacrifice.

The elephant buries its tusks when they fall out due to old age; it uses one of its teeth to dig around roots for food and keeps the other pointed for combat. When it is captured by hunters and overwhelmed by exhaustion, it breaks its tusks; as if by a treaty, it ransoms itself with them.

They are merciful and recognize danger. If they encounter a man who is alone and lost, they set him back on his path. If they find human footprints, they fear a betrayal; they stop and blow <note>original: "soufflent"; this likely refers to the elephant trumpeting or blowing air to alert the herd through scent and sound</note>, showing the others how to form into a troop, and then they depart cautiously. (*H. 19.*)

<meta>This page continues the descriptions of animal behaviors and natural history, likely part of a bestiary or a collection of selected texts on zoology.</meta>

# SELECTED TEXTS

---

**446 bis.** — They travel in troops and the eldest walks in front; and he who is second in age stays behind and brings up the rear. They are modest, mating only at night and in hiding.

They do not return from their lovemaking to the herd until after they have washed themselves in the river. They never fight with the female as other animals do. They are so merciful by nature that they do no harm, despite their strength. If they encounter flocks of sheep in the fold, they use their <term>trunk</term> <note>original: "trompe"</note>, which serves them as a hand, to set them aside so as not to trample them underfoot. Furthermore, they never cause harm unless they are provoked.

Once an <term>elephant</term> has fallen into a pit, the others fill it with branches, earth, and stones in a pyramidal shape at the bottom, so that he may easily remain free <note>This likely refers to creating a ramp or platform for the trapped animal to climb out</note>.

They fear the squeal of a pig and flee backwards, and they do not fail to strike their enemies with their feet.

Loving rivers, they always remain wandering in their vicinity, although because of their feet they cannot swim <note>The author incorrectly assumes elephants cannot swim due to their heavy build</note>. They swallow stones and gnaw on the trunks of trees, of which they are very fond.

They hold mice in hatred. Flies delight in their scent and land upon their backs.

<summary>This passage describes the social behaviors, physical traits, and perceived moral virtues of elephants, emphasizing their intelligence, modesty, and gentleness toward smaller creatures.</summary>
<keywords>elephant, trunk, animal behavior, zoology, mercy, herd dynamics, natural history</keywords>

and become encrusted in their skin, but they crush them within the folds of their hide. <note>This continues the previous page's discussion on elephants and their struggle with flies.</note>

When they cross rivers, they send their young across according to the warmth of the water, and they stand on the slope breaking the ordinary course of the water, so that the current does not sweep them away. (*H. 19, 20, r. and v.*) <note>Leonardo's notebooks are often cited by the manuscript letter and folio number; "r" and "v" stand for recto (front) and verso (back) of the page.</note>

### *Intemperance.*

447. — The <term>licorne</term> <note>An archaic term for the unicorn</note> or the unicorn, through its intemperance, does not know how to overcome the pleasure it takes in young maidens; betraying its ferocity and wildness, and casting aside all suspicion, it goes to the maiden and lies down in her lap, and in this manner the hunters capture it. (*H. 10, r.*)

### *Humility.*

447 *bis*. — The lamb shows the greatest experience of humility; it submits to every other animal, and when it is given as food to lions in a cage, it submits as if to its own mother, so that the lion has often been seen refusing to kill it. (*H. 11, r.*)

### *Abstinence.*

448. — The wild ass, when it goes to the stream to drink and finds the water murky, no matter how thirsty it may be, abstains from drinking and waits until the water becomes clear again. (*H. 11, r.*)

### *The Siren.*

448 *bis*. — The siren sings with such sweetness that she lulls the sailors to sleep; she then climbs onto the ship and kills them in their slumber. <note>original: "H. 8, r." refers to Manuscript H, folio 8, recto (the front of the page) in Leonardo's notebooks.</note>

### *Correction.*

448 *ter*. — When the wolf creeps toward a livestock stall and happens to misstep, he lets out a cry and bites his own foot to punish himself for his error. <note>original: "H. 7, r."</note>

### *Folly.*

449. — The wild bull detests the color red.
Hunters dress the stalk of a plant in red, and the bull charges toward it, striking it with great fury with his horns; it is then that the hunters kill him. <note>original: "H. 8, r."</note>

### *Temperance.*

450. — The camel is the most <term>lustful</term> <note>The original French "luxurieux" here implies intense sexual drive or lechery</note> animal there is; he will travel a thousand miles to reach a female camel, and yet he can live with his mother and sister without ever touching them, so great is his temperance. <note>original: "H. 10, r."</note>

*Falsehood.*

452. — The mole has very small eyes and dwells beneath the earth, and as long as it lives, it remains hidden. If it suddenly comes into the light, it dies, just like every lying thing. (*H. 9, r.*)

*Dolphin*

453. — Nature has given animals, besides the knowledge of their own advantage, that of the disadvantage of their enemies. The dolphin, when it casts off the edges of its fins <note>original: "tailles de ses nageoires"; likely referring to the sharp, serrated dorsal spines</note>, places itself on its back, and when it can reach the belly of the crocodile, then, in their combat, it thrusts itself beneath it, rips open the belly, and thus kills it.
The crocodile is terrible to those who flee, and very base to those who hunt it. (*H. 26, r.*)

*Hippopotamus.*

454. — When it feels sick, it seeks out a thorn or, failing that, a sharp fragment of a reed, and rubs a vein until it opens. Once the excess blood has flowed out, it plugs and closes the wound with mud. It resembles the horse: with a cloven hoof, a twisted tail, and the teeth of a

### *The Reindeer.*

455. — The reindeer is born in the Scandinavian islands;
it has the form of a large horse, except that the neck
and the ears are longer: it grazes on grass
standing upright, for its lips are so long

It has a leg made of a single piece <note>Leonardo repeats a common medieval myth that certain animals lacked joints in their legs.</note> and when it

through the shrub, and when it leans to sleep
it falls; the hunters, then, take it,
and there is no other way, for it is of an
incredible velocity in the race. (*H. 21, r.*)

### *Bison.*

456. — The bison is born in Paeonia <note>An ancient region in the Balkans, north of Macedonia.</note>, it has the mane

except that its horns are placed backward
and it cannot charge. It has no other safety than
in flight, where it throws its dung every
four hundred fathoms <note>original: "brasses"; a measurement of length based on the reach of the arms.</note> and if one touches this dung,
it burns like fire. (*H. 21, r.*)

### *The Doe.*

457. — The doe, feeling herself bitten by the spider called a <term>phalangium</term> <note>original: "falonge"; likely referring to the Phalangium harvestman or a similar spider, believed in medieval lore to be highly venomous to deer</note>, eats crayfish and frees herself from such venom. (*H. 22, r.*) <note>Reference to Leonardo's Manuscript H, folio 22 recto.</note>

### *The Weasel.*

The weasel, when it hunts rats, first eats <term>rue</term> <note>A bitter herb (Ruta graveolens) traditionally believed to provide protection against poisons and stings</note>. (*Id.*) <note>Latin "Idem," meaning the same source as above.</note>

### *The Wild Boar.*

The wild boar treats its illnesses by eating <term>ivy</term>. (*Id.*)

### *The Serpent.*

When the serpent wishes to renew its skin, it shelters in an old rock and, starting from the head, it sheds its skin in a day and a night. (*H. 6, r.*)

### *The Panther.*

The panther, even with its entrails hanging out, still fights against the dogs and the hunters. (*H. 20-21.*)

### *Peace.*

458. — It is said of the beaver that, when it is pursued, knowing it is for the medicinal virtue of its testicles, and being unable to flee, it stops; and to have peace with the hunters, with its sharp teeth, it cuts off its testicles and leaves them to its enemies. (*H. 6, r.*) <note>This is a widespread medieval allegory of the "Castor" or beaver, representing the wise man who sacrifices his worldly "organs" of sin to the devil (the hunter) to save his soul.</note>

<summary>Leonardo records several folkloric observations about animals using plants as medicine and the panther’s resilience, concluding with the famous moral allegory of the beaver's sacrifice.</summary>

<keywords>bestiary, doe, weasel, wild boar, serpent, panther, beaver, medicinal plants, animal legends, Manuscript H</keywords>

### *Fear.*

459. — The hare always trembles, and the leaves falling from the trees in autumn make him shudder and sometimes put him to flight. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 10 recto.</note>

### *Chastity.*

460. — The turtle-dove <note>The original French "tortue" (turtle) is likely a transcription error or archaic usage for "tourterelle" (turtle-dove), as the legend of refusing to perch on green branches is a classic trope of the widowed dove.</note> never deceives its mate; if one dies, the other observes a perpetual chastity, never again perching on a green branch and never again drinking from clear water. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 12 recto.</note>

### *Avarice.*

461. — The toad feeds upon earth and always remains thin, for want of satiating itself; and it is so fearful that it does not even eat its fill of this very earth. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 6 recto.</note>

462. — It flees the light of the sun, and if held there by force, it swells up so much that its head disappears below to escape the rays. So does the enemy of clear and shining virtue, which can only remain alive by strongly exalting the soul. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 17 recto.</note>

### *Ermine.*

463. — Moderation restrains all vices. The ermine, before dying, empties its body. The ermine eats only once a day, and rather than let itself be captured by the hunt—

<insert>pursuers</insert> wants to flee into its burrow so as not to stain its nobility. <note>original: "gentillesse." In this context, it refers to the ermine's legendary purity and its refusal to soil its white fur, preferring death to dirtiness. Refers to Manuscript H, folio 12 recto.</note>

<margin>*Catoblepas.*</margin>
464. — Native to Ethiopia, near Nigropont <note>original: "Négrepont." While Nigropont usually refers to Euboea in Greece, Leonardo or his sources likely confused geographical names or referred to a specific African region believed to be near the Nile's source.</note>, the catoblepas <note>A legendary creature from Pliny’s Natural History, often described as a bull-like animal with a heavy head.</note> is a medium-sized serpent, sluggish in all its limbs; it has a head so large that it carries it with difficulty and lets it hang toward the earth. Otherwise, it would be a plague to man, for whoever meets its eyes falls dead. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 24.</note>

<margin>*Basilisk.*</margin>
465. — From the province of Cyrenaica <note>original: "Arénaïque," likely a corruption of Cyrenaica in North Africa.</note>, no longer than twelve fingers, it bears on its head a white spot similar to a diadem. As its hissing drives away every serpent, by its resemblance of form <note>This likely refers to its unique upright posture compared to other snakes.</note> it does not move with sinuosity, but remains immobile. It is said of a basilisk killed by the spear-thrust of a horseman, that the venom running up the spear killed not the horseman, but the horse. It spoils the grain, and not only those it touches, but where it breathes, it withers the grass and breaks the stones. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 24.</note>

<margin>*Weasel.*</margin>
466. — The weasel, finding the hole of the basilisk by the smell of its scattered urine, kills it; the smell of this urine, at other times, kills the weasel itself. <note>Refers to Manuscript H, folio 7 recto.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the entry on the ermine's purity and describes the lethal attributes of the legendary catoblepas and basilisk, as well as the weasel's unique ability to hunt the latter. These accounts reflect the medieval and Renaissance tradition of the "bestiary," where animal traits—real or imagined—served as moral or natural wonders.</summary>

*Cruelty.*

467. — The basilisk is so cruel that when it cannot kill animals with its venomous eyes, it turns against the herbs and plants, and casting the effluvia of its gaze upon them, it makes them wither away. 
All snakes flee from it; the weasel, by means of <term>rue <note>A bitter-tasting herb (*Ruta graveolens*) long believed in folklore to provide protection against poison and the basilisk’s gaze.</note></term>, fights and kills it. (*H. 7, r.*) <note>Manuscript H, folio 7, recto.</note>

*Viper.*

468. — This creature has the peculiarity that, by opening its mouth and the tips of its small teeth, it kills the male; then, as they grow, the young tear open the belly and kill the mother. (*H. 14, r.*) <note>This reflects a common medieval and ancient belief about the viper, symbolizing a cycle of violence and poetic justice.</note>

*Boa.*

469. — This serpent, a very large animal, when it sees a bird in the sky, exhales a breath so foul that the bird falls into its mouth. 
Marcus Regulus, consul of the Roman army, was attacked along with his army by such an animal and nearly lost. A <term>siege engine <note>original: "machine murale." A heavy military machine like a catapult or ballista used for breaking down walls.</note></term> killed it; it was 125 feet long—that is to say, 64 and a half <term>fathoms <note>original: "brasses." An ancient unit of measurement based on the span of a man's outstretched arms.</note></term>—surpassing with its head all the plants of a forest. (*H. 21, r.*)
This great serpent coils itself around the legs of a cow and then remains there and nurses from it until the cow is dried up. One of this species died on the <term>Vatican Hill <note>A hill in Rome where, in Leonardo's time and earlier, many ancient remains and unusual natural history finds were reported.</note></term> during the time of the Emperor Claudius.

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI
255

He had a whole child inside his body, which he had swallowed. <note>Leonardo is likely referencing a legendary account of a giant serpent or dragon; source: Manuscript H, folio 21.</note>

*The Dragon.*

470. — This creature binds the leg of the elephant; they fall together and both die. In dying, the elephant takes his revenge. <note>Manuscript H, folio 14 verso.</note>

The dragon throws itself under the body of the elephant, ties its legs with its tail, and with its wings and paws it encircles the elephant's sides, slitting its throat with its teeth; the elephant falls on its back, and the dragon is crushed beneath it; thus, in death, the enemy is avenged. <note>Manuscript H, folio 24.</note>

470 *repeated*. — Dragons travel together and entwine themselves in the manner of <term>hydras</term> <note>A multi-headed water serpent from Greek mythology; Leonardo uses the term here to describe a collective, interlocking movement.</note>; with heads raised, they cross the marshes and swim to where they find better pasture, and although there are several of them, they appear to be only a single animal. <note>Manuscript H, folio 20 recto.</note>

*The Cerastes.* <note>A horned viper mentioned in classical natural histories like those of Pliny the Elder.</note>

471. — It has four small, movable horns; when it wishes to eat, it hides its entire body under the leaves and allows only its horns to show: and as they move, the birds mistake them for little wriggling worms; suddenly, if the birds swoop down to seize them, the cerastes uncoils itself in a circle and devours them. <note>Manuscript H, folio 24.</note>

### *Amphisbaena.* <note>original: "Amfesibène"; a mythical serpent with a head at each end.</note>

472. — It has two heads: one in the normal place, the other at the tail, as if it were not enough for the venom to lie in a single location. (*H. 24.*) <note>Leonardo is referencing Manuscript H, folio 24.</note>

### *Jaculus.* <note>original: "Iaculo"; a legendary "javelin-snake" said to hide in trees and launch itself at prey.</note>

473. — It stays upon the plants and launches itself like a dart, strikes the wild beast and kills it. (*H. 24.*)

### *The Asp.*

474. — There is no remedy for its bites unless the bitten part is cut away. This pestilential animal has such affection for its mate that it always accompanies it, and if by misfortune one of them dies, the other pursues the killer with incredible speed; it is so attentive and eager for vengeance that it overcomes every difficulty. It seeks to reach its enemy and traverses the vastest distances: it cannot be tired out.

It has deep-set eyes and large ears, and it guides itself more by hearing than by sight. (*H. 24. 2.*)

### *Ichneumon.* <note>Likely referring to the Egyptian mongoose, famous in antiquity for its ability to kill snakes and crocodiles.</note>

475. — This mortal enemy of the asp is born in Egypt, and when it sees an asp near it, it runs to the mud of the Nile, rolls in it, and then dries itself in the sun; and again it covers itself with mud and again it dries itself; once

after another, it makes for itself three or four coats like a suit of armor. Then it attacks the asp and valiantly throws itself into its mouth, piercing the stomach and the intestines, and finally kills it. (*H. 25.*) <note>Leonardo is referencing Manuscript H, folio 25.</note>

### *Crocodile.*

476. — This son of the Nile has four feet; formidable on water as on land, he is the only animal that has no tongue and that bites by moving its upper jaw. It reaches 40 feet in length, is clawed, and is armed with a hide <note>original: "cuir"; literally leather, referring here to the crocodile's tough, armor-like skin.</note> ready for any blow; it stays on land during the day and spends the night in the water. It feeds on fish, falling asleep on the banks of the Nile with its mouth open. A bird called the trochilus <note>original: "trochilo"; traditionally identified as the Egyptian Plover, which was believed to clean the teeth of crocodiles.</note>, very small in size, darts into its mouth and between its teeth to peck at the food that remains there; by pecking with exquisite pleasure, it invites the crocodile to open its maw wide and thus fall asleep. (*H. 25.*)

As was seen with the ichneumon, the trochilus hurls itself into the mouth, perforates the stomach and the belly, and finally kills its adversary. (*H. 17, r.*) <note>Leonardo appears to conflate the symbiotic behavior of the bird with the aggressive legendary behavior of the ichneumon.</note>

### *Hypocrisy.*

477. — The crocodile attacks man and kills him suddenly. After he is dead, with a mournful voice and many tears, it weeps for him, and once its lamentation is finished, it cruelly devours him. <note>This is the classic medieval bestiary origin for the phrase "crocodile tears," symbolizing a hypocrite who feigns sorrow for those they have harmed.</note>

# SELECTED TEXTS

Thus acts the hypocrite who, for the same reason, fills his eyes with tears while possessing the heart of a tiger, and rejoices inwardly at the misfortune of others while wearing a face full of pity. <note>Leonardo often used animal metaphors to describe human vices; here the tiger represents hidden cruelty.</note> (H. 17, r.)

<margin>*Virtue.*</margin>
478. — The salamander in the fire perfects its shell. Like virtue, it has nothing passive about it and takes no concern for any nourishment other than fire, where it renews its skin. <note>In medieval lore, the salamander was believed to live in and consume fire, symbolizing purification and the strength of virtue.</note> (H. 12, r.)

<margin>*Chameleon.*</margin>
479. — The chameleon lives on air and wins over all the birds; to be more secure, it flies above the clouds into a zone so subtle <note>meaning thin or rarefied air</note> that the birds following it cannot sustain themselves there.
To this height goes only he to whom heaven has granted permission, just as the chameleon flies.
The chameleon always takes the color of the object where it rests. Sometimes it blends with the foliage, and thus the elephants devour it. <note>This reflects the ancient belief that elephants accidentally ate small creatures while consuming leaves.</note> (H. 27, r.)

<margin>*Caterpillar.*</margin>
479 *bis*. — The caterpillar, thanks to its skillful way of weaving its new dwelling with admirable artifice and subtle labor, later emerges with beautiful colored wings by which it ascends toward heaven. (H. 17, r.)

### *The Spider.*

480. — The spider gives birth to the ingenious and masterfully crafted web which, for its benefit, secures the prey it has captured. (*H. 17, r.*)

### *The Tarantula.*

481. — The bite of the tarantula keeps a man in the same state of mind he was in at the moment he was bitten. <note>This refers to the historical phenomenon of "tarantism," where victims were believed to fall into a trance or uncontrollable dancing based on their initial reaction to the bite.</note> (*H. 18, r.*)

### *Justice.*

482. — One can compare the virtue of justice to the king of the bees <note>original: "roi des abeilles"; Leonardo, following ancient tradition, refers to the queen bee as a king.</note> who orders and arranges everything with reason; some are charged with going to the flowers, others with working, others still with fighting the wasps, others with removing refuse, and others with forming the king's procession. When he is old and wingless, they carry him, and if any bee fails in its duty, it is punished without mercy. (*H. 8, r.*)

### *The Cicada.*

483. — Its song silences the owl; it dies in oil and is reborn in vinegar; it sings during the burning heat of the day. <note>These paradoxical traits are drawn from classical natural histories, where vinegar was thought to have restorative properties for certain insects.</note> (*H. 14, r.*)

## TREATISE ON BIRDS <note>original: "Volucraire"; a term for a collection of lore regarding birds, derived from the Latin "volucris" (winged creature).</note>

->*Love of Virtue.*<-

488. — The lark is a bird of which it is said that if it is brought near a sick person, and if the invalid is to die, the bird flies to the side, stays opposite, and never looks at him; but if the patient is to recover, the bird never takes its eyes off him, so as to draw away all the illness. <note>This legend is traditionally associated with the "Caladrius," a mythical white bird; here Leonardo attributes the miraculous healing behavior to the lark.</note>

# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

Thus, the love of virtue does not consider anything vile or sad, and remains always with what is honest and good; and always, in its noble heart, it keeps the example of the birds in the green forests, perching upon the flowering branches.

This love appears better in adversity than in prosperity, just as light shines all the more when it is found in a dark place. <note>Reference to Manuscript H, folio 5, recto.</note>

### *Joy.*

489. — This is represented by the cock <note>The rooster</note>, who takes delight in the smallest thing and sings with varied and joyful movements. <note>Manuscript H, folio 11.</note>

### *Sadness.*

490. — This is likened to the raven who, when he sees his children are white <note>A common medieval belief that young ravens were born white and only later grew black feathers</note>, gives himself over to great sorrow and abandons himself to desolate cries, and is only appeased if he sees some few black feathers. <note>Manuscript H, folio 11, recto.</note>

### *Magnanimity.*

491. — The falcon, by its haughty and disdainful flight, wishes to prevail over all other birds of prey and desires to be the only one: it has been seen at times to attack the eagle, the king of birds. <note>Manuscript H, folio 11, recto.</note>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci uses a moralized bestiary to illustrate human emotions and virtues, comparing joy to a rooster, sadness to a raven, and magnanimity to a falcon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s moralized bestiary from Manuscript H, where he draws allegorical lessons from the perceived behaviors of birds and animals to illustrate human virtues and vices.</meta>

<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

---

### *The Falcon.*

492. — The falcon takes only large birds and would prefer to die rather than eat flesh that has a foul smell. <note>Manuscript H, folio 17, verso. This reflects the Renaissance ideal of noble refinement and the refusal of "corrupt" influences.</note>

### *Envy.*

493. — One reads of the kite <note>A medium-sized bird of prey</note> that, when it sees its young in the nest becoming too fat, it strikes them with its beak and keeps them without food. <note>Manuscript H, folio 52, recto. Leonardo uses this to symbolize how envy begrudges the prosperity of even those closest to it.</note>

493 *bis*. — The vulture is so driven by its maw <note>original: "la gueule," referring to its gluttonous appetite</note> that it will travel a thousand miles to eat of carrion: and for this reason, it follows armies. <note>Manuscript H, folio 11, recto.</note>

### *The Eagle.*

494. — When the eagle is old, it flies so high that it burns its feathers, and nature allows it to find its youth again by falling into shallow water. <note>A reference to the medieval myth of the "Phoenix-like" renewal of the eagle, often used as a symbol of spiritual rebirth.</note>

If its young cannot endure the sight of the sun, it does not feed them. No bird that wishes to live approaches its nest; all animals fear it, but it does not harm them and leaves them something of its prey. <note>Manuscript H, folio 12, recto. This depicts the eagle as a "just" and "magnanimous" ruler.</note>

### *The Ibis.*

495. — The ibis resembles the stork; and when it feels sick, it fills its throat with water and with its beak gives itself an enema <note>original: "clistère," a common medical procedure of the time</note>. <note>Manuscript H, folio 26, recto.</note>

### *The Raven.*

496. — The raven, when it has killed the chameleon, purges itself with laurel. <note>Manuscript H, folio 17, recto. In medieval lore, laurel was often seen as an antidote to the perceived toxicity of certain reptiles.</note>

<summary>Leonardo continues his allegorical bestiary, describing the noble habits of the eagle and falcon alongside the envious nature of the kite and the self-medicinal instincts of the ibis and raven.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, bestiary, allegory, falcon, envy, vulture, eagle, ibis, raven, Manuscript H, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s moralized bestiary from Manuscript H. Having previously discussed the eagle and the ibis, Leonardo here focuses on the symbolic behaviors of doves, ostriches, swans, and storks to illustrate themes of family loyalty and natural wonders.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### *Ingratitude.*

497. — Doves are prone to ingratitude. As soon as they are old enough to no longer need to be fed, they begin to fight with their father; this struggle does not end until his death, and as for the mother, they take her as their mate. <note>Manuscript H, folio 6, verso. Leonardo often recorded these observations to serve as allegories for human behavior, here representing the ultimate betrayal of parental care.</note>

### *The Ostrich.*

498. — It converts iron into food and hatches its egg with its gaze. <note>A common medieval and Renaissance belief was that ostriches could digest metal and that the heat of their stare was sufficient to incubate their eggs.</note> For the army, it is the true food of a captain. <note>Manuscript H, folio 13, recto. This likely refers to the "sustenance" of prestige, as ostrich plumes were a primary decoration for high-ranking military helmets.</note>

### *Swan.*

499. — The swan is white without any blemish, and it sings sweetly before dying: this song brings its life to an end. <note>Ibid. The "Swan Song" is one of the most enduring myths in natural history, symbolizing a beautiful conclusion to a virtuous life.</note>

### *Stork.*

500. — By drinking salt water, it heals itself; if it finds its mate in fault <note>referring to infidelity</note>, it abandons her; and when it is old, its sons brood over it and feed it until its death. <note>Ibid. The stork was a traditional symbol of "filial piety" (loyalty to parents) in Renaissance emblems, directly contrasting with the "ingratitude" of the doves mentioned above.</note>

### *Lumerpa.*

501. — It is born in Upper Asia and shines so brightly that it removes the shadow around it; even in dying, it does not lose this brilliance, and its feathers never fall out; however, a feather that is plucked no longer glows. <note>Ibid. The "Lumerpa" is a legendary bird, likely derived from accounts of the "Hercynian birds" mentioned by Pliny the Elder, whose feathers were said to glow in the dark to light the way through dense forests.</note>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, Bestiary, Ingratitude, The Ostrich, Swan, Stork, Lumerpa, Upper Asia</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo records a series of animal allegories focusing on themes of loyalty, betrayal, and mythical biological properties. These entries serve as moral lessons, contrasting the filial piety of the stork with the aggressive ingratitude of the dove.</summary>
<keywords>bestiary, Leonardo da Vinci, moral allegory, folklore, natural history, symbolism, Manuscript H</keywords>

<margin>*Pelican.*</margin>
502. — It bears great love for its young, and finding them dead in the serpent's nest, it strikes itself to the heart and ends its life by bathing them in its blood. <note>original: "H. 12, r." refers to the manuscript source. The pelican was a common medieval symbol for Christ's sacrifice.</note>

<margin>*Partridge.*</margin>
502 *bis.* — This one transforms from female to male and denies its original sex, and out of envy steals the egg of another; but the children return to the true mother.

<margin>*Swallow.*</margin>
503. — The swallow, with the celandine stone <note>original: "pierre célidoine." A legendary gemstone or herb believed to be found in the bellies of swallows and used to heal eyes.</note>, restores sight to its young born blind. (*H. 48, r.*)

<margin>*Lizard.*</margin>
504. — When the lizard <note>original: "luserta," likely from the Latin lacerta.</note> fights with the serpent, it eats the sow-thistle <note>original: "cicerbite," a plant believed to have medicinal or protective properties.</note> and frees itself. (*H. 26, r.*)

<margin>*Liberality.*</margin>
505. — It is said that the eagle is never so hungry that it does not leave some part of its prey to the birds nearby, who, unable to feed themselves, follow the eagle in order to eat its remains. (*H. 7, r.*)

<margin>*Crane.*</margin>
506. — The crane, fearing that its king might perish for lack of a good guard, stands near him at night with a stone in its claw. <note>In folklore, the crane holds a stone so that if it falls asleep, the stone will drop and wake it up.</note>

509. — Gratitude is a virtue said

make them a nest, brood over them, feed them, and

and with certain herbs they restore their life, and
finally return them to prosperity. (*H. 6, r.*)

510. — The bat, in its unbridled
lust, observes no universal
mode of lust; and male with male, female
with female, as the case may be, together
they copulate. (*H. 12, r.*)

### *Vanity.*

**511.** — Vice is seen in the peacock, which, more than any other animal, constantly contemplates itself in the beauty of its tail, which it spreads out in the shape of a wheel, and with its cry which draws the attention of other animals. And this vice is the most difficult to conquer. (*H. 10, r.*)

### *Constancy.*

**512.** — The phoenix represents constancy; it awaits its renewal from nature; it endures the burning flames that consume it, in order to be born again. (*H. 10, r.*)

### *Inconstancy.*

**513.** — The swallow represents inconstancy, as it is always in motion and cannot endure the slightest scarcity. (*H. 10, r.*)

### *Fidelity.*

**514.** — Cranes are so faithful and loyal to their king that at night, while he sleeps, some go around the meadow to watch from afar, while others stay close to him; they each hold a stone in their claw, so that if sleep overcomes them, the stone will make a noise upon falling and wake them; and others surround the sleeping king, keeping watch all night, so that he lacks for nothing. <note>This legend of the "vigilant crane" holding a stone was a popular medieval trope for alertness and duty.</note> (*H. 9, r.*)

# XV

## DESCRIPTIONS. — LANDSCAPES

### REPRESENTATION OF THE DELUGE (1)

**515.** — The air shall be darkened by the heavy rain falling obliquely, driven by the cross-winds and creating waves in the atmosphere, much like one sees with dust, with the distinction that this flood will be streaked by the lines formed by the falling drops of water. But its color will be derived from the fire engendered by the lightning, cleaving and tearing through the clouds; the flames of which will strike and reveal the depths of the flooded valleys, where the openings show the tops of bent plants within their shelters. Furthermore, you might have seen, upon the peaks of many mountains, the most diverse animals huddled together by fear and reduced to familiar contact in the company of men fleeing with their wives and children.

### SELECTED TEXTS

Oh! what despair, and how many panicked souls hurl themselves from the tops of the rocks! One can see the branches of a great oak, laden with men, carried through the air by the impetuous fury of the wind.

Every boat there is—some are completely overturned, others smashed to pieces upon the people who struggle for their lives with pained gestures and movements, feeling the threat of death. Others, in their desperation, take their own lives, despairing of being able to endure such anguish; some throw themselves from the rocks, others strangle themselves with their own hands, others take their children and quickly cast them onto the embankment <note>original: "remblai" — likely referring to the last remaining mounds of earth or piles of debris</note>, others strike themselves with their weapons and kill themselves, and others fall to their knees, commending themselves to God.

Oh, how many mothers weep for their drowned sons whom they hold upon their knees, raising their open arms toward heaven and, with a howling voice, cursing the divine wrath. Others, with hands joined and clenched, bite themselves with cruel teeth as if they were devouring themselves, praying with supplication, crushed by an immense and unbearable sorrow.

One sees herds of animals—horses, oxen, goats—already surrounded by water and left isolated on the high peaks of the mountains; they huddle together, and those in the center are pushed upward.

And the ruins of certain mountains fall into the depths of the valleys and form an obstacle to the swollen water of the river; and this obstacle is soon overcome by the water <note>In Leonardo’s notebooks, he often describes how water builds up behind a landslide until the pressure causes a violent breach</note>, which rushes forth violently and goes on to destroy and strike the walls of the city and the homes in the valley.

And the circular waves <note>Leonardo was deeply fascinated by "circular waves" (waves of interference), observing how they move through one another without losing their form</note> fleeing the point of impact travel with violence across the path of other circular waves moving in the opposite direction; after the collision, they leap into the air without breaking away from their base.

they acquire weight and an impetuous movement; then, penetrating the struck water which opens and strikes furiously at the <term>percussion</term><note>The forceful impact of one body against another; here, the water hitting the bottom.</note> of the bottom, from where it is then pushed back, it rebounds to the surface of the abyss, accompanied by the air that it submerged along with it. It emerges again foaming, mixed with wood and other things lighter than water, around which, according to its principle, it increases its circuit and therefore acquires movement; this makes it lower when it takes a wider base, though this is barely perceptible in its dissipation. The wave, repelled by various objects, then leaps up upon the approach of another wave, observing the increase of the same <term>curvity</term><note>The degree of curvature or the arched shape of the wave's path.</note> that it had acquired according to the observation of the movement begun. that which moves furthest from the opposite bank will be the heaviest or of the greatest volume.

The eddies of water in its various parts are faster the closer they are to its center. The crest of the sea wave descends first to its base, beating and crashing upon the roundness of its surface: and this <term>conflagration</term><note>Leonardo uses "conflagration" metaphorically here to describe the violent, energetic collision of water particles, similar to a fire's intensity.</note>, crushed into small particles of falling water, changes into a thick cloud and mingles with the course of the winds in the manner of a disordered smoke; a cloudy revolution rises in the air and is converted into a mist. But the rain, which descends through the air in its combative action and is struck by the course of the wind, becomes thin or dense according to the rarity or density of the winds; and thus there is generated in the air a flood of transparency, made of the falling rain that is close to the eye that beholds it.

The sea wave that is struck by the <term>obliquity</term><note>The slanted or sloping nature of the terrain.</note> of the mountains which combine with it, will be foaming and rapid against the backs of the hills, and in its turning will meet with the arrival of the second flow of water; and then the great noise returning, with a great flood, will go back to the sea from whence it came. A great multitude of human peoples and diverse animals saw themselves perish by this rising of the deluge toward the peaks of the mountains neighboring the aforementioned water. (*R. 327.*) <note>Reference to Jean Paul Richter's "The Literary Works of Leonardo da Vinci," entry 327.</note>

### HOW TO REPRESENT A BATTLE

516. — You shall first depict the smoke of the artillery mingled in the air with the dust raised by the movement of the horses and the combatants. You shall treat this mixture in this way: the dust, which is a terrestrial and <term>ponderable</term><note>original: "pondérable"; meaning having physical weight or being heavy.</note> thing, although by its lightness it rises easily and mingles with the air, does not readily fall back down; its highest elevation is reached by its lightest part, that which is least visible and almost blends with the color of the air. The smoke that mingles with the air becomes laden with dust, and the higher it rises, the more it appears as a dark cloud; the smoke reaches the top more quickly than the dust.

The smoke will take on a slightly <term>azure</term><note>A sky-blue or light blue color.</note> hue, and the dust will take the color of the earth itself. From the side where the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear brighter than on the opposite side. The combatants will be less visible, and one will see less distinction between their lights and shadows, the more they are enveloped in this turbulent air.

You shall make the clouds, the people, the air, and the musketeers glow red together with everything that surrounds them; and this redness diminishes as it moves away from its cause. The figures that are between you and <insert>the light</insert> <note>Leonardo implies the light source or the fire of the weaponry here.</note>

<vocab>battle, artillery, dust, smoke, light, azure, to glow red, combatants, ponderable</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci provides technical artistic instructions on depicting a battle, focusing on the physics of how smoke and dust interact with light and color. He emphasizes the "azure" tint of smoke versus the earthy tone of dust and how these elements obscure the combatants.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, painting techniques, battle scenes, atmospheric perspective, smoke and dust, optics, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

the light, being distant, will appear dark against a bright field, and their legs will be all the less visible as they approach closer to the ground, because at the bottom the dust is thicker and more dense.

If you depict horses running away from the fray, make for them little clouds of dust, distant from one another by the interval of a horse's leap; and let this little cloud be seen less the further it is from the horse, and at that point let it be higher, scattered, and small. Closer [to the horse], let it be more evident, small, and thicker.

The air shall be full of arrows of various kinds, some rising, some descending, and some traveling in a flat line; and let the shots from the <term>guns</term><note>original: "fusils"; referring to early firearms or muskets used in the late 15th/early 16th century.</note> be accompanied by some smoke aimed straight toward their target.

And the figures in the foreground you shall make dusty, including the hair, the eyelashes, and other smooth parts suited to holding dust.

You shall depict the victors running with manes and other light ornaments fluttering in the wind; with their brows lowered and thrusting forward the opposite limbs—that is to say: such a man will send forward his right foot, while his left arm falls back <term>fatigued</term><note>original: "fati_gué"; the underscore in the OCR represents a line-break hyphen.</note>. ## ON THE MANNER OF REPRESENTING A NIGHT

**518. —** That thing which is entirely deprived of light is all darkness. Night being in such a state, if you wish to depict a story therein, you shall place a great fire. That which is closest to the fire is tinted most deeply with its color, because the nearer a thing is to an object, the more it participates in its nature. And making the fire tend toward a red color, you shall make all things illuminated by it of that glowing red color, and those situated furthest from the fire will participate more in the black color of the night.

The figures that are between you and the fire will appear dark against the obscurity of the night and not illuminated by the fire; those located at the sides will be half dark and half glowing red; and those that can be seen <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with those figures facing the fire directly.</note>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, painting, representation of night, chiaroscuro <term>chiaroscuro: the treatment of light and shade in drawing and painting</term>, light, darkness, fire, glowing color, artistic technique, Ashburnham MS</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his description of a violent storm and begins a technical lesson on "chiaroscuro," explaining how to use a single fire source to realistically illuminate a night scene.</summary>
<keywords>shipwreck, storm, night painting, firelight, color theory, light and shadow, Leonardo da Vinci, artistic instructions</keywords>

## LANDSCAPES

->*An effect of clouds over Lake Maggiore.*<-

519. — I have already observed similar condensations of air; and above Milan, toward Lake Maggiore, I saw a cloud in the shape of a very large mountain full of hollow rocks: for the rays of the sun, which was already at the horizon, tended toward red and tinted the cloud with their colors. And this cloud attracted to itself all the other small clouds that followed it; as for the great cloud, it did not move from its place; even on its summit, the light of the sun remained until an hour and a half into the night, so immense was this cloud; and toward two hours into the night, there arose a great wind which was an astonishing and unheard-of thing. <note>original: "R. 1021." refers to Richter's compilation of Leonardo's notebooks, entry 1021.</note>

<vocab>LANDSCAPES, cloud, Lake Maggiore, Milan, light, atmosphere, wind</vocab>

## *On the varied colorations of the sea.*

520. — The undulating sea does not have the same color everywhere: for one who sees it from the mainland, it is of a dark color, and all the darker the closer it is to the horizon; and you see certain brightnesses or glimmers that move slowly in the manner of little white sheep <note>The French "petites brebis blanches" (little white sheep) is a translation of Leonardo’s "pecorelle," a term still used in several languages to describe the whitecaps of waves or "mackerel sky" clouds.</note> in the flocks; he who looks while being out at sea sees it as azure blue! This comes, regarding the darkness of the sea seen from the land, from the fact that you see the water reflecting the darkness of the earth; and for the azure effect seen out at sea, you see in the water the azure of the sky which it reflects. <note>original: "LU. 327." refers to the Codex Urbinas, the primary source for Leonardo's Treatise on Painting.</note>

## *The Island of Cyprus.*

521. — At the southern shores of Cilicia <note>Cilicia is an ancient region on the south coast of Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey).</note> is seen, to the south, the beautiful island of Cyprus, which was the kingdom of the goddess Venus. Many, drawn by its beauty, had their ships and rigging shattered among the rocks surrounded by swirling water. The beauty of the gentle hill invites wandering ships to refresh themselves among its flowery greenery, for deceptive winds fill the island and the sea that bathes it with sweet aromas.

Oh! how many ships have already been submerged! Oh! how many vessels shattered on these rocks! There, one could see countless ships lying helpless and half-covered by the

->*An ascent of Monte Rosa.*<-

524. — I say that the azure blue displayed by the air is not its own proper color, but is caused by warm moisture evaporated into tiny and imperceptible atoms; and this warm moisture then receives the percussion <note>The striking or reflection of light</note> of the solar rays, which makes it luminous against the obscurity of the immense darkness of the region of fire <note>In the Aristotelian cosmology used by Leonardo, the "region of fire" was the highest elemental sphere, located above the air and appearing black due to its distance and lack of moisture</note> which acts as a cover above it.

One will see this, as I have seen it, if one goes upon Monte Rosa, a peak of the Alps which separates France from Italy. This mountain at its base gives birth to four rivers which water all of Europe in four contrary directions; and no mountain has its base at such a height.

It rises to such a height that it sur-

->*Vegetation on a hill.*<-

225. — These grasses and plants will be of a color all the paler as the soil that nourishes them is leaner and deprived of moisture; and the soil is poorer and leaner upon the rocks that form the mountain. ## ALLEGORIES

*The Enraged One.*

526. — For the figure of the enraged man, you will have him holding a man by the hair, whose hat has fallen to the ground, with another person kneeling by his side; the figure shall raise the fist of his right arm into the air. This figure shall have his hair standing on end, his eyebrows low and thick, his teeth clenched, and the two corners of his mouth arched downward; his neck should be thick and leaning toward the enemy, and covered in wrinkles. (*ASH. I. 29, r.*) <note>Leonardo emphasizes the physical tension of the "fat neck" and clenched teeth to convey the internal pressure of rage. "ASH" refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts.</note>

*The Desperate One.*

527. — To the desperate man, you will give a knife, and with his hands he will have torn his clothes; one of his hands shall be at work pulling open his wound; and depict him with feet apart and legs slightly bent, his entire body turned toward the earth with his hair torn and scattered. (*ASH. I. 29, r.*)

528. — Envy attacks with a sharp infamy; that is to say, she drives away virtue, which she terrifies.

<vocab>allegories, the enraged one, the desperate one, envy, Leonardo da Vinci, treatise on painting</vocab>

<summary>This page provides Leonardo da Vinci's precise artistic instructions for depicting the physical manifestations of anger and despair, as well as a brief aphorism on the nature of envy.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, anger, despair, envy, physiognomy, painting techniques, Renaissance art, human emotion</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of artistic instructions for depicting human conditions and allegories. Following the descriptions of anger and despair on page 287, Leonardo now provides a highly detailed iconographic program for the personification of Envy.</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

She is represented looking toward the heavens, for if she were able, she would employ her strength against God Himself. Render her with a mask over a face of beautiful appearance. <note>The mask symbolizes the deceptive nature of Envy, which hides its malice behind a pleasant exterior.</note> Depict her wounded at the sight of palms and olive branches; depict her wounded in the ear by laurels and myrtles, which signify that victory and truth offend her. <note>Palms, olives, laurels, and myrtles are traditional symbols of peace, victory, and honor—achievements that the envious person cannot bear to witness or hear about.</note> Have smoke rising from her to signify her malicious speech. Make her thin and withered, because she is in continual agony; depict her body being gnawed by a serpent. Give her a quiver and long arrows, for that is how she attacks. Dress her in a leopard skin, because this animal kills the lion through cunning. Place in her hand a vase full of flowers, and among them scorpions, toads, and other venomous creatures. Have her riding upon Death, because envy does not die but only withers away; make her bridle heavy with various weapons, the instruments of death. As soon as <term>virtue</term><note>In the Renaissance context of *virtù*, this refers to excellence, talent, and moral strength.</note> is born, it awakens envy against itself, and there is no more a body without a shadow than there is virtue without envy. (*ASH. II. 22, v.*) <note>original: "ASH. II. 22, v."; refers to the Ashburnham Manuscripts, part of Leonardo's notebooks now held at the Institut de France.</note>

<vocab>Envy, virtue, death, leopard, serpent, iconography, allegory, Cesare Ripa</vocab>

<summary>This page contains Leonardo da Vinci’s detailed instructions for the allegorical representation of Envy, using specific symbols like the leopard skin and serpent to illustrate its deceptive and self-consuming nature. He concludes with a philosophical observation on the inseparable relationship between excellence and the envy it inevitably provokes.</summary>
