# XVI
## FABLES

### THE FLAME AND THE CANDLE.

529. — A flame had already lasted for a month in a glassmaker's furnace <note>Glassmaking furnaces required constant, intense heat, making a month-long flame a symbol of stability and longevity.</note>, when it saw near it a candle placed upon a beautiful and shining candlestick. With a great desire, the flame strove to reach it. Abandoning its natural course and leaving the others, it chose a piece of firewood <gloss>tison</gloss> where it increased its size, escaped through a small fissure, and threw itself upon the neighboring candle. With greed and gluttony, it devoured the candle until it was entirely consumed.

Wanting then to ensure the prolongation of its life, the flame attempted to return to the furnace from which it had departed; but it died and was extinguished along with the candle. Finally, with moans and crackling <note>The "plaintes et pétillements" represent the physical sound of a dying fire personified as cries of regret.</note>, it dissolved into a detestable smoke, while all its sisters, the other <note>original: "aurtes", a typographical error for "autres"</note> flames, shone with long life and beauty. (C. A. 67, r. <note>This refers to the Codex Atlanticus, a bound set of drawings and writings by Leonardo da Vinci.</note>)

<term>fable</term> (a short story conveying a moral), <term>flame</term>, <term>candle</term>, <term>glassmaker</term>, <term>candlestick</term>, <term>firebrand</term>, <term>shone</term>

<summary>A moral fable describing a furnace flame that leaves its stable source out of greed to consume a candle, resulting in its own untimely extinction. It serves as a warning against abandoning security for short-lived, selfish gains.</summary>
<keywords>fable, allegory, greed, glassmaking, Leonardo da Vinci, Codex Atlanticus, morality</keywords>

### FALSE SPLENDOR.

530. — The giddy and wandering butterfly was not content with being able to fly comfortably through the air; it came toward the attractive flame of the candle, resolved to fly into it; and its joyful movement was followed by a sudden pain. In that light, its delicate wings were consumed, and the wretched insect fell, completely burnt, to the foot of the candlestick.
After many complaints and lamentations, it wiped away the tears bathing its eyes and, lifting its face, said: — O deceptive light, how many others like me must you have already abominably deceived! Oh! I wished to see the light; should I not have discerned the sun from the false glow of the filthy tallow? <note>Tallow (suif) was a common, often foul-smelling candle fat made from animal lipids, contrasted here with the pure light of the sun.</note> (*C. A.* 67, *r.*) <note>Codex Atlanticus, folio 67 recto.</note>

### THE FLAME AND THE POT.

531. — A little fire, which had hidden itself within a small coal beneath the warm ash, judged itself with spite to be in a miserable situation.
Then the kitchen maid, to prepare the usual meal, arrives and places wood in the hearth; she resuscitates a small flame with the bellows, and upon the pieces of wood she has brought, she places the pot <gloss>marmite</gloss> without worrying if it is properly balanced.
Then, revived, the fire catches the dry wood and begins to rise.

<term>tallow</term> (rendered animal fat used for cheap candles), <term>bellows</term> (a device used to blow air into a fire to make it burn more fiercely), <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> (the largest collection of Leonardo da Vinci's papers)

<summary>Two fables illustrating the dangers of misplaced desire: a butterfly is destroyed by the false light of a tallow candle, and a small ember is revived by a kitchen maid only to be burdened by a heavy cooking pot.</summary>
<keywords>fable, allegory, butterfly, candle, tallow, hearth, Leonardo da Vinci, vanity, deception</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page (531. — The Flame and the Pot), detailing Leonardo’s anthropomorphic description of fire as it consumes wood in a kitchen hearth.</meta>

<page-num>291</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

Chasing the air from the gaps in the wood, which it fills with strength and a joyful passage.

It begins to blow through the gaps in the wood as if they were windows made for that very purpose, and it pushes out long, glowing flames, suddenly illuminating the darkness of the closed kitchen.

Joyfully, the growing flame plays with the surrounding air and, as if singing with a soft murmur, makes a pleasant sound.

The fire, delighted by the dry wood found in the oven—by which it had been awakened—begins to frolic in its little flickers; from moment to moment, it pulls itself through the gaps it finds in the wood.

Running between the logs, joyful and passing through gaily, it begins to blow and appears from time to time in the upper openings of the wood, like favorable windows.

Now it appears above the wood, grown larger and quite lively, and begins to lift its spirit—until then soft and quiet—into swelling and unbearable pride, acting as if it believed it could draw the entire magnificent element <note>In Leonardo’s time, fire was considered one of the four fundamental elements of the universe.</note> upon that small amount of wood. And it begins to blow and throw sparks in a crackling manner all around the hearth; already the flame, having become large, was dividing under the pressure of the air when, rising higher, it traveled along the pot from the bottom to the upper rim... (*C. A. 116, v.*) <note>Codex Atlanticus, folio 116 verso.</note>

<summary>This passage provides a vivid, personified description of a fire growing in a kitchen hearth, transitioning from a gentle flicker to an arrogant, roaring flame. It illustrates Leonardo's keen observation of physical phenomena combined with his habit of giving human emotions to natural elements.</summary>
<keywords>fire, combustion, personification, hearth, kitchen, Codex Atlanticus, elements</keywords>

### HE WHO HUMBLES HIMSELF SHALL BE EXALTED.

532. — A small patch of snow lay upon the summit of a rock forming the highest peak of a very lofty mountain; and, gathering its thoughts, it began to compare itself to the mountain, saying to itself:

> “I must not judge myself to be haughty and proud, being but a tiny bit of snow placed in this high place, nor should I permit such a vast quantity of snow as I see below me to be situated so far beneath my level. My small quantity does not deserve this altitude—an altitude which can well be proven, by the witness of my small shape, to be the same that the sun dealt to my companions yesterday, who in a short time were melted by its rays. This happened to them because they were placed higher than was necessary. I wish to flee the wrath of the sun, to lower myself, and to find a place suitable to my smallness.”

It threw itself down and began to descend, rolling from the high rock onto the other snow. The more it sought a lower place, the more its bulk increased, in such a way that at the end of its course it rested upon a hill that was scarcely less large than the one supporting it. This was the last snow in that place that the sun melted. This teaches that those who humble themselves shall be exalted <note>original: "Qui s'humilie sera exalté," a reference to the Gospel of Luke 14:11</note>. (Codex Atlanticus 66, *verso*)

533. — The more a snowball rolls as it descends the mountain of snow, the more its volume increases. (Codex Atlanticus 67, *verso*)

<term>Codex Atlanticus<note>The largest collection of Leonardo da Vinci's drawings and writings, covering subjects from weaponry to botany.</note></term>

<summary>This page features a moral fable by Leonardo da Vinci about a small patch of snow that humbles itself by rolling down a mountain, only to grow in size and longevity. It serves as an allegory for the Christian virtue of humility leading to true greatness.</summary>

### THE RAZOR.

**534.** — Emerging one day from the handle that served as its sheath, the razor, placed in the sun, saw the star reflected upon its surface; from this it took great pride, and revolted in its thoughts, beginning to say to itself: “I shall never return to that shop from which I have just come. Certainly not: may it not please God that such splendid beauty fall into such vileness of soul! What a place is that, which leads me to shave the lathered beards of wretched peasants and perform a mechanical task. Am I made for such an exercise? Certainly not. I wish to hide myself in some secret place and there pass my life in perfect repose.” And so, it remained hidden for several months, and one fine day returned to the air and drew itself out of its sheath, and it saw itself looking like a rusty saw, and its surface no longer reflected the splendid sun. With vain repentance, it deplored its irreparable <term>loss<note>original: "dam," an archaic term for damage or ruin, often used in a moral or legal sense.</note></term>, saying to itself: “Oh! How much better it was to exercise my fine edge, now lost, at the barber's? Where is the brilliance of my surface? The implacable and brutal rust has devoured it.”

The same happens to those minds which abandon exercise to give themselves over to inertia; like this razor, they lose the edge of their subtlety, and the rust of ignorance deforms them.
(*C. A. 172, v.*) <note>Reference to the Codex Atlanticus, folio 172 verso.</note>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci uses the allegory of a razor that ruins itself through idleness to warn that the human mind loses its sharpness and brilliance when it abandons active work for sloth.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, allegory, sloth, rust, ignorance, Codex Atlanticus, moral fable, work ethic</keywords>

AGITATION.

**535.** — The torrent brought so much earth and stone into its bed that it was forced to change its course. (*R. 1314.*) <note>Reference to the Jean Paul Richter edition of Leonardo's notebooks, entry 1314.</note>

PAPER AND INK.

**536.** — Seeing itself all <term>blackened<note>original: *mâchuré*, an archaic term for being smeared, soiled, or poorly printed.</note></term> by the thick darkness of the ink, the paper laments; but the ink shows it that the words traced upon it will be the reason for its preservation. (*R. 1322.*) <note>This allegory suggests that while "ink" (hardship or labor) may seem to defile, it is the medium through which thoughts become immortal.</note>

THE STONE.

**537.** — A stone of fine size found itself covered by water; it was situated at a certain elevated spot where it bordered a charming grove, above a rocky path, among herbs and flowers of varied colors. It saw the great quantity of stones that were placed on the path. The desire came to it to let itself fall, saying to itself: “What am I doing among these herbs? I want to be in the company of my sisters.” And it lets itself fall and comes rolling into the desired company. At the end of a short time, the wheels of carriages, the iron-shod feet of horses, and those of passers-by left it no rest; it was a contest of who would push it, strike it, and sometimes it lost pieces. When it was covered with mud and the <term>droppings<note>original: *fiente*, animal excrement.</note></term> of animals, it looked

### WATER.

**538.** — Finding itself in the magnificent sea, its own element, the water came to desire to rise above the air. Aided by <term>elemental fire<note>In the science of Leonardo's time, "elemental fire" was believed to be a sphere of heat above the air that caused evaporation.</note></term>, it rose as a subtle vapor and appeared as subtle as the air. As it rose, it reached a thinner and colder zone where the fire abandoned it; the small, restricted droplets joined together and became heavy, and in falling, the proud water sank. It fell from the sky: it was welcomed by the parched earth, where, being absorbed for a long time, it did penance for its sin. (**S. K. M. III, 98, v.**) <note>Reference to the Forster Codices (III, 98 verso) held at the South Kensington Museum, now the Victoria and Albert Museum. This is a moral allegory on the "sin" of pride and the natural cycle of evaporation and rain.</note>

See how my sweet children are preserved, first

### BAD COMPANY.

**540.** — The vine, grown old upon the old tree, fell at the same time as it, and was dragged by its sad companion into the same ruin. (**R. 1314.**) <note>Reference to Jean Paul Richter’s 1883 compilation of Leonardo's notes. This is a classic "memento mori" or a warning about the risks of dependency.</note>

**541.** — The willow which, by its long foliage, wishes to grow until it surpasses all other vegetation, was nonetheless crippled for having kept company with the vine, which is pruned back every year. (**R. 1314.**) <note>In traditional agriculture, vines were often "married" to trees for support. Leonardo observes that the heavy pruning required for the vine eventually damages the growth of the supporting tree.</note>

**543.** — The net that was used to catch the fish was carried away and torn by their fury. (**R. 1314.**) <note>Refers to Jean Paul Richter’s 1883 compilation. A brief observation on how the collective strength of the weak (the fish) can overcome the instrument of their capture.</note>

**544.** — Like the branch of the <term>walnut tree<note>original: *noyer*.</note></term>—which alone is struck and beaten once it has brought its fruit to maturity—if one observes how, by means of the conclusion of their famous work, they are struck with envy in various ways. (**H. 98, r.**) <note>Manuscript H, folio 98 recto. Leonardo identifies with the walnut tree: just as people throw sticks at the tree to get its nuts, the successful person is "beaten" by the envy of others once they have produced something valuable.</note>

God had granted it—being so high and enriched with such a beautiful bell of such a fine sound—to help it, since it had been unable to fall beneath the green branches of its old father and into the fat earth covered by the falling leaves; it begged that he might not abandon it.

After finding itself in the beak of the proud crow, changing its state of existence, it desired to finish its life in a little hole. Moved and compassionate at these words, the wall was compelled to keep it in the place where it had fallen. In a short time, the walnut tree began to sprout and to slide its roots between the fissures of the stones and to stretch its branches out of its hole; its branches soon rose above the building, and the knotted roots, having grown thick, began to force open the wall and to drive the ancient stones from their place. Then the wall, too late and in vain, understands the reason for its misfortune, and in a short time, it sees the ruin of the greater part of its members.

557. — Seeing the usefulness of the vine, a peasant makes many props <note>original: "tuteurs"; stakes or supports used to hold up climbing plants</note> to support it; but once the fruit is gathered, he removes the poles and lets it fall, and makes a fire with the props.
(*C. A. 67, r.*)

### THE WILLOW AND THE PUMPKIN.

558. — The willow considered itself miserable because it could not enjoy the pleasure of seeing its slender branches reach their desired height; it called heaven to witness that, because of the vine and other neighboring plants, it was constantly crippled, disturbed, and spoiled. Gathering all its wits and giving sharp course to its long-pensive imagination, it searched for what plant it could ally itself with that would not have need of its bonds <note>The willow is traditionally used as a source of flexible ties or "withes" to bind other plants to stakes.</note>; and as it was in these meditations, the course of its thoughts led it to the pumpkin. In the joy of having found the desired company, it shook its branches, for the pumpkin is less suited to binding others than to being bound itself. Having deliberated, it raised its branches toward the sky, waiting for some obliging bird to help it realize its desire.

> Soon it perceived a magpie: "Gentle bird, I pray you—in the name of the refuge you found one morning in recent days within my branches, when the hungry, cruel, and rapacious hawk wished to devour you; and by the rest you enjoyed upon me when you felt the need to close your wings; and for the pleasures you tasted under my leaves with your mate during the season of love—I pray you, therefore, to go find the pumpkin and ask it for some of its seeds, and tell it that I shall treat them as if they were generated by my own body; use every means

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci presents a fable where a willow tree, tired of being used to bind other plants, seeks to grow a pumpkin instead. The willow appeals to a magpie's gratitude to help facilitate this ill-fated alliance.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, fable, willow, pumpkin, magpie, allegory, nature, gratitude, Codex Atlanticus</keywords>

of persuasion, and I have no need to lecture you, who are a master in the art of speaking. If you do this, I shall receive your nest at the birth of my branches, with all your family, without payment of any kind."

Then the magpie, having made a pact with the willow and settled other points—more than a serpent or a weasel <note>The original French "plus que serpent et fouine" suggests a cunning or complex negotiation</note> if she did not accept—raised her tail and lowered her head, and leaving the branches, gave her weight back to her wings.

And beating the air here and there inquisitively and steering with the rudder of her tail, she reached the pumpkin and, with a fine greeting and good words, requested the desired seed. Having returned to the willow, who received her with joy, she stirred the earth a little at the foot of the tree with her beak and planted the seed around him. This seed soon grew, enveloped all the branches with its shoots, and with its large leaves hid the beauty of the sky and the sun. And not stopping there, the pumpkin, by the increase of its weight, began to pull the tips of the tender branches toward the earth, torturing and distending them. Then the willow shook and agitated itself to make the pumpkin let go, but the days passed in vain efforts, for the binding was so strong that it was impossible to break it. Seeing the wind pass by, the willow asked it to blow very hard. Then the trunk of the old willow split in two down to its roots...

### THE DOG AND THE FLEA.

559. — A dog was sleeping on the skin of a kid <note>a young goat</note>. One of his fleas, smelling the scent of the oily wool, judged that this must be a better place to live, being safe from the teeth and claws of the dog; and without another thought, it abandoned the dog.

Having entered deep into the thick wool, it began with great effort to try and reach the root of the hairs.

After much sweating, it found the skin dry because the hairs had been so tightly pressed together that they touched, and there was no place where the flea could bite into the skin. After much painful labor, it wished to return to its dog; but he had gone. After long suffering and bitter regrets, it was forced to die of hunger. (C.A. 119, r.)

### THE MOUSE, THE WEASEL AND THE CAT.

561. — Sitting in front of the small dwelling of a mouse, the weasel, with continual vigilance, awaited its death. Through a small vent-hole, the mouse watched its great peril. In the meantime, a cat arrives, throws itself upon the weasel and immediately devours it. Then the rat <note>Leonardo often uses "mouse" and "rat" interchangeably in these fables</note> makes a sacrifice to <term>Jupiter</term><note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology; Leonardo uses classical references to give his fables a timeless, Aesop-like quality</note> of some of its nut-kernels and thanks the divinity with effusion and leaves its retreat to enjoy its liberty. Suddenly, its liberty is snatched away along with its existence by the ferocious claws and the teeth of the cat. (*C. A. 67, r.*)

### THE OYSTER, THE RAT AND THE CAT.

564. — The oyster, mixed with other fish in the fisherman's hand, wished to return to the sea and begged the rat to lead it there; the rat, intending to eat it, makes it open, and then the oyster clamps down on its head and closes its shell; the cat arrives and kills the rat. (*H. 51, v.*) <note>Refers to Manuscript H, held at the Institut de France.</note>

### THE FALCON AND THE DUCK.

565. — The falcon, unable to patiently endure the hiding place used by the duck as it flees and dives under the water, tries to follow it; it wets its feathers and remains stuck in the water, while the duck rises into the air and laughs at the falcon who drowns. (*H. 44, v.*)

### THE OYSTER AND THE CRAYFISH.

566. — At the full moon, the oyster opens itself completely, and when the crayfish <note>While "écrevisse" usually means a freshwater crayfish, in this maritime context Leonardo likely refers to a crab</note> sees it, it watches from some rock: and the oyster, which can no longer close itself, becomes the food of the crayfish. So it is with anyone who opens their mouth and tells their secret and...

### THE THRUSHES AND THE OWL.

567. — The thrushes rejoiced when they saw the man catch the owl <term>civette (little owl)</term> and take away its liberty, tying its feet with strong bonds. This owl was afterwards the cause, through the use of birdlime <note>A sticky substance spread on branches to trap birds.</note>, of the thrushes losing not just their liberty, but their very lives. This is said for those who rejoice to see their friends lose their freedom and all assistance, remaining bound in the power of their enemies, and [consequently] lose their own liberty and sometimes their lives. (C. A. 67, v.)

### THE SPIDER.

568. — The spider, wishing to catch the fly in its deceptive nets, was cruelly killed by the hornet. (C. A. 67, r.)

### THE CRAYFISH.

### THE PLANTS AND THE PEAR TREE.

571. — Seeing the pear tree being pruned, the laurel and the myrtle cried out in a loud voice: — O pear tree, where are you going? Where is your pride now, which you held when you bore your ripe fruit? Now you will no longer provide shade with your thick foliage. It replied: — I go with the gardener who prunes me and carries me to the workshop of the finest sculptor, who by his art will make me take the form of the god Jupiter. I shall be dedicated in the temple and adored by men as an equal to God. And you, you are prepared to remain crippled and deprived of your branches, which men will arrange around me to honor me. *(C. A. 76, r.)*

### THE DONKEY ON THE ICE.

572. — A donkey had fallen asleep on the ice of a deep lake; his warmth melted the ice, and the donkey fell under the water and was drowned. *(C. A. 67, v.)*

### LEGEND OF WINE AND MOHAMMED.

574. — Finding itself in a rich golden cup on the table of Mohammed, the wine, that divine liquor of the grape, revels in the glory of such great honor; then, troubled by a contrary thought, it says to itself: — "What am I doing? Why do I take such pride in myself? Am I not on the verge of dying and leaving the golden habitation of this cup to enter the coarse and fetid cavern of the human body, and to change my sweet and fragrant liquor into filthy and wretched urine? And as if such an evil were not enough, I must still dwell for a long time in those foul hideouts with the other fetid and corrupt matter expelled from the human belly." It cried out to heaven, demanding vengeance for such a downfall and asking how there might be an end to such a disgrace, seeing as the lands producing the finest and best grapes of the other world <note>Leonardo likely refers here to the "Orient" or the Levant, then considered the "other world" to a European audience.</note> are nonetheless reduced to wine. Then Jupiter <note>Leonardo frequently uses the names of Roman gods like Jupiter to represent a supreme divine power or "Nature" in his fables.</note> caused the wine drunk by Mohammed to stir his soul in a way contrary to his reason, making him mad and inspiring such errors in him that he made a law forbidding the people of Asia to drink wine. Since then, the vines have been left with their fruit. *(C. A. 76, r.)*

# XVII

## JESTS <note>original: "FACÉTIES"; these are riddles or "prophecies" where Leonardo describes common objects or events in apocalyptic or paradoxical terms to surprise the listener.</note> (1)

576. — Certain ones who, dressed in white garments, will walk with arrogant movements, threatening with metal and fire: which shall do them no harm (*censers* <note>vessels for burning incense used in religious rituals</note>). (*R. 1310.*)

577. — Many of those, in order to practice their art, will dress themselves very richly: and this appears to be done according to the custom of women's aprons (*priestly vestments*). (*C. A. 362, v.*)

578.— Simple peoples will carry a great quantity of light to illuminate the journey of all those who have entirely lost the faculty of sight (*funerals*). (*C. A. 362, v.*)

579.— I see Christ sold and crucified once again, and once again I also see his saints martyred (*devotional images* <note>Leonardo is likely critiquing the trade and sale of religious art and icons</note>). (*I. 65, v.*)

580. — Those who have been dead for a thousand years will provide for the expenses of many of the living (*saints* <note>referring to the income generated by shrines, relics, and the veneration of long-dead holy figures</note>). (*I. 65, v.*)

---

1. The most important groups of these jests bear the title of *Prophecies of rational and irrational animals* <note>original: "Profezie degli animali razionali et irrazionali"</note>. — *Of philosophical matters* <note>original: "Delle cose philosophice"</note>.

631. — One will see forms and figures of men and animals that will follow these men and animals wherever they may be; and these forms will make the same movement as the man, but it will be a marvelous thing to see the variety of size these forms will take <note>original: "l'ombre humaine" (the human shadow).</note>. (*C. A. 362, r.*)

632. — Very great figures in human form will appear, and the closer they are to you, the more they will diminish their immense size <note>original: "ombres" (shadows). This refers to the optical effect where a shadow cast on a distant wall appears larger than one cast nearby.</note>. (*K. 50, v.*)

### FROM LEONARDO DA VINCI

633. — One will see human generations who will not understand one another when speaking, like a German and a Turk. (*I. 64, r.*)

634. — Bodies without souls will move by themselves and carry with them innumerable generations of the dead, taking riches from the living surrounding them <note>original: "bois mort" (dead wood). Leonardo refers here to wooden chests or ships used for commerce, which are "dead" matter moving "living" wealth.</note>. (*Id.*)

635. — One will see the dead carrying the living — carriages and ships. (*I. 66, r.*)

636. — One will find, in walnut and other trees, great treasures that are hidden and well guarded there <note>A riddle referring to the edible nuts protected by hard shells.</note>. (*C. A. 362.*)

637. — One will see the trees of the great forests of the Taurus and Sinai, of the Apennine and the Atlas mountains, cleaving the air from East to West, and from the North to the South, and thus transporting many men.

Oh! how many vows, how many deaths, how many separations of friends and relatives; and how many of them will not see their province or their homeland again, and who will die without burial, leaving their bones scattered in various places of the world <note>original: "navires" (ships). In this evocative passage, Leonardo describes the timber used to build the hulls and masts of ships that carry explorers and merchants across the globe.</note>. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

638. — Why the Hungarians hold the double cross <note>A reference to the "patriarchal cross," a heraldic symbol used on the coat of arms of Hungary.</note>. (*H. 3. 7, v.*)

639. — The ferocious horns of powerful bulls will defend the nocturnal light from the impetuosity of the wind <note>original: "lanterne" (lantern). Before the widespread use of glass, thin, translucent shavings of animal horn were used to shield the flame of a lantern from the wind.</note>. (*F. 64, v.*)

640. — Oxen, with their horns, will prevent the fire from dying (*lantern* <note>original: "lanterne"; refers to lanterns made of translucent shaved horn which protected the candle flame from the wind</note>). (*C. A. 362.*)

641. — Within the horn of animals, one will see sharp irons with which the lives of many of their own kind are taken (*bow* <note>original: "arc"; refers to composite bows made of horn and the metal-tipped arrows they fire</note>). (*C. A. 362.*)

642. — Many, because of the bovine horn, will die a painful death (*bow*). (*C. A. 362.*)

643. — Flying animals will support man with their own feathers (*mattress* <note>original: "matelas"; referring to down-filled bedding</note>). (*C. A. 362.*)

644. — They will be revered and honored, and with reverence and love their precepts will be accepted, for a thing that was first bound, cast to the ground, and martyred by many and various beatings (*flax* <note>original: "lin"; describes the violent process of threshing and retting flax to create linen for clothing or sacred cloths</note>). (*C. A. 362.*)

645. — The forest engenders sons who will become the cause of its death (*the axe handle*). (*Id.*)

646. — The movement of the dead will cause many of the living to flee with pain, lamentation, and cries. (*Id.* <note>This likely refers to the skins of dead animals used for drums or bellows</note>)

647. — Many of the dead will stir with fury and will catch and bind the living, serving their enemies almost until their death and destruction (*snares and traps* <note>original: "lacs et trappes"; traps were often made from the sinew or hides of dead animals</note>). (*Id.*)

648. — They live together like nations and are drowned to take their honey: many great peoples will be drowned in their own homes (*bees*). (*R. 1329.*)

He who gives light to divine worship shall be drowned (*the bee* <note>original: "abeille"; refers to bees being killed or "drowned" in smoke/water to harvest the wax used for church candles</note>). (*R. 1297*)

651. — Men shall emerge from their graves changed into birds and shall assault other men, taking their food from their very hands or from their tables <gloss>the flies</gloss>. (*C. A. 143, r.*)

652. — Oxen shall be in large part the cause of the city's ruin, as well as horses and buffaloes <gloss>as artillery teams</gloss> <note>Leonardo refers to the heavy animals used to pull cannons and siege engines during warfare</note>. (*R. 1297.*)

653. — Certain things, upon ceasing to be pressed, shall increase in volume, and if a weight is placed upon them, they shall immediately collapse <gloss>cushions</gloss>. (*C. A. 36, r.*)

654. — Many shall be occupied in a task where the more they pull the thing downward, the more it shall rise, in contrary motion <gloss>rope with two buckets</gloss> <note>A reference to a pulley system in a well</note>. (*C. A. 362, r.*)

655. — There shall be many working to take away from that thing which grows as much as one removes from it <gloss>a pit</gloss> <note>The more earth you remove, the larger the hole becomes</note>. (*C. A. 362.*)

657. — Many shall perish with their heads shattered, and their eyes shall almost start out of their heads, because of fearful animals killed in the darkness <gloss>birds caught with birdlime</gloss> <note>Birdlime is a sticky substance used to trap birds; the "fearful animals" likely refers to the birds themselves or the decoys used in the hunt</note>. 658. — One shall see, at a great height in the air, very large serpents fighting with birds <gloss>the stork</gloss> <note>Storks were known for hunting snakes, often carrying them high into the air</note>. (*C. A. 127, v.*)

659. — How many there are who, after their death, shall rot within their own bodies, spreading around them a fetid pus <gloss>discarded shells</gloss> <note>Likely referring to mollusks or snails left to decay in their shells</note>. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

660. — How many shall be forbidden from being born? <gloss>eggs</gloss> <note>original: "œufs"</note>. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

661. — One shall see fathers and mothers give much more assistance to their step-children than to their true sons <gloss>grafting</gloss> <note>original: "greffe"; in gardening, the "step-child" or scion is grafted onto the host plant to receive its nutrients</note>. (*R. 1310.*)

662. — The trees and shrubs of the great forests shall be converted into ashes. (*A. 362, v.*)

663. — Many sons, by cruel beating, shall be snatched from the very arms of their mothers, thrown to the ground, and then torn apart <gloss>fruits</gloss> <note>original: "fruits"; refers to the harvesting of fruit by shaking or striking the branches</note>. (*C. A. 143, v.*)

664. — Those who have done the best shall be the most beaten, and their sons snatched away and discarded—that is to say, stripped, and their bones broken and crushed <gloss>walnuts</gloss> <note>original: "noix"; refers to the process of harvesting and cracking nuts</note>. (*I. 65, v.*)

665. — That which gives nourishment to light shall descend with fury toward the earth <gloss>olive</gloss> <note>original: "olive"; the olive falls to the ground to be pressed into oil for lamps</note>. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

666. — Aquatic animals shall die in boiling water. (*C. A. 362.*)

667. — There shall be great winds that will make Eastern things Western; and those of the South, largely mingled by the course of the winds, shall follow them to distant lands. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

668. — One shall see very large lifeless bodies carrying a multitude of men with speed to the destruction of their lives. (*C. A. 362, v.*) <note>This likely refers to ships during a storm or naval battle</note>

669. — The mud shall be such that men will walk upon the trees of their country <gloss>sandals</gloss> <note>original: "sandales"; a riddle explaining that wooden-soled shoes or clogs allow a person to walk above the mire</note>. (*C. A. 362, v.*)

### *A Fantastic Giant (1).*

**702.** — His black face, at first sight, is very horrible and terrifying to look upon, especially the deep-set and red eyes, placed under sparse and dark eyebrows—eyes capable of making the weather darken and the earth tremble.

We believe there is no man so proud! who, upon encountering these flaming eyes, would not willingly wish for wings to flee; for the infernal Lucifer shows an angelic face in comparison to this one. The bristly nose with wide nostrils from which many long hairs emerge, under which is seen the menacing mouth with thick lips, the ends of which resemble those of cats by their whiskers, over yellow teeth.

And even more horrific (be patient), his anger turns to fury; he begins with his feet which he agitates, with his powerful legs he enters the crowd and with kicks he throws men into the air; and they fall back onto other men, as if it happened that they were a thick hail. And many died from

<note>original vocabulary: giant, Lucifer, Morgante Maggiore, Pulci, physical description, portrait, itch</note>

Certainly, on this occasion, the human species has reason to envy every other species of animal; for if the eagle conquers other birds by its power, those that retain the speed of flight, such as the swallow, escape the plunder of the blackbird through their agility; dolphins, by the liveliness of their flight <note>Leonardo often uses the word "flight" (volo) to describe the swift movement of aquatic animals through water.</note>, escape the hunt of the whale and the great narwhals; but we, miserable ones! Here no flight is of any use, for this beast, with its slow step, far exceeds the speed of any courser. I know not what to say nor what to do; and it seems to me nonetheless fatal to fall headlong into the great maw and to remain, in a confused death, buried within the great belly. (C. A. 96, v.) <note>Reference to the Codex Atlanticus, folio 96 verso.</note>

->*Letter to Dear Benedetto de Pertarli.*<- <note>original: "Caro Benedetto de Pertarli." This refers to Benedetto Dei, a Florentine merchant and traveler known for his own exaggerated tales.</note>

703. — He has fallen, the proud giant, upon the muddy and blood-stained earth; less is the fall of a mountain, shaking the countryside, jolting the earth with tremors and terrifying the infernal Pluto <note>The Roman god of the underworld, used here to emphasize the subterranean impact of the giant's fall.</note>.

And, by the great blow, he remains upon the level ground completely stunned; suddenly the people, believing him dead by some lightning bolt, run toward him—the great crowd returns.

In the manner of ants that run furiously, scurrying over the entire body of the prostrate giant, so these people traverse his enormous limbs, lacerating them with crowded wounds.

Then the giant, having regained consciousness and feeling himself as if covered by a multitude, suddenly feels the stinging of their bites—and lets out a bellow that sounded like terrifying thunder. # XVIII

## ANECDOTES

->*Of a Friar and a merchant.*<-

704. — The Friars Minor <note>original: "frères mineurs." These are the Franciscans, a religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi, known for their vows of poverty.</note> are accustomed, at certain times during their Lent, to not eat meat within their convent; but while traveling, as they live on alms <note>Charitable donations of food or money.</note>, they have permission to eat whatever is placed before them.

Therefore, on one of these journeys, a pair of friars happened upon an inn in the company of a certain merchant. Being at the same table, there was brought—given the poverty of the inn—nothing other than a single roasted chicken.

Seeing that this was very little for himself, the merchant turned to the friars and said to them: — If I remember correctly, on such a day as this, you do not eat any kind of meat in your convents.

At these words, the friars were constrained by their rule <note>The "Regula," or the set of laws governing their religious life.</note> to admit without any evasion that this was the truth; then the merchant satisfied his own desire; and thus he ate the chicken, and the friars put on the best face they could.

<gloss>Friars Minor, Lent, convent, alms, merchant, roasted chicken, rule</gloss>

<summary>This page recounts a humorous anecdote where a merchant uses the religious dietary restrictions of two Franciscan friars to trick them out of a meal, leaving him to eat the only available chicken himself.</summary>

<keywords>Friars Minor, Franciscans, Lent, merchant, anecdote, dietary laws, roasted chicken, wit</keywords>

--- Page 346 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 335, this text completes the humorous anecdote about the merchant and the Franciscan friars before beginning a new "facetia" (joke) involving a painter and a priest.</meta>

<page-num>336</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

Afterward, following such a dinner, the dining companions set off all three together. At one point in the journey, they encountered a river of considerable width and depth; all three were on foot, the brothers due to their vow of poverty and the merchant due to his greed. For the convenience of the group, it was necessary for one of the brothers to remove his shoes and carry the merchant on his shoulders; thus the brother gave his sandals to be held and shouldered the man.

It happened that while the brother was in the middle of the river, he suddenly remembered his rule and, stopping in the manner of <term>Saint Christopher</term> <note>Saint Christopher is traditionally depicted as a giant who carried the Christ Child across a dangerous river; he is the patron saint of travelers.</note>, and turning his head toward the one he was carrying, said to him:
— Tell me a bit, do you have any money on you?
— Of course, replied the other, how could you believe that I, a merchant, would travel without money? — Alas! <note>original: "Ohime!" an Italian exclamation of dismay often found in Leonardo’s original notes.</note> said the brother, our rule forbids us from being able to carry money on our persons. — And suddenly he threw him into the water.

This taught the merchant that the insult he had given was now avenged with a pleasant laugh and quite peacefully: and, half-blushing with shame, he endured the reprisal. (*C. A. 147, v.*)

### *From a painter to a priest.*

705. — A priest was passing through his parish on <term>Holy Saturday</term> <note>The day before Easter, often associated with the ritual blessing of homes.</note>, giving, according to custom, holy water in the houses; he entered the workshop of a painter where he sprinkled some paintings with water.

This painter, turning toward him in a rage, said:

— "Why are you sprinkling my paintings in this way?" — Then the priest said that it was a customary thing, that it was his duty to do so, that he was doing good, and that he who does good should expect good and better; that it was a promise from God and that for every good deed done on earth, one would receive a hundredfold in heaven.

Then the painter waited for the priest to leave and positioned himself at the window; throwing a large bucket of water over him, he said: — "Here is what comes to you from above for your 'hundred for one,' just as you said would happen from the good you did me with your holy water, which has half-ruined my paintings." <note>Original: "du cent un cent." This anecdote highlights Leonardo's frequent friction with the clergy and his protective nature over his artistic labor.</note> (*C. A. 117, r.*) <note>C. A. refers to the Codex Atlanticus, the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings.</note>

### *A witty remark from an artisan to a lord.*

706. — An artisan went to see a lord, with no other purpose than to see him; the lord asked him why he had come.

The other said that he came to have a pleasure that the lord could not give himself; because he [the artisan] gladly looks upon men more powerful than himself, as common people do, whereas the lord could not see men less important than himself, and thus lords lacked this specific pleasure. <note>Leonardo often explored the paradoxes of social status and the unique perspectives of the working artist.</note> (*R. 1283.*) <note>R. refers to the Richter edition of Leonardo's literary works.</note>

### *A painter’s retort.*

707. — A painter was asked why he, who created such beautiful figures that were not even alive, had produced such ugly children. The painter replied that painting was done by day and children were made by night. <note>This is a classic Renaissance joke often attributed to various artists, including Giotto, highlighting the distinction between intellectual artistic creation and physical procreation.</note> (*R. 1283.*)

### *A joke played on a braggart.*

708. — While someone was arguing and boasting of knowing how to perform many beautiful and varied tricks, one of those present said: — I know how to do a trick; it consists of making anyone I please pull off their sleeves. <note>The original French "tirer les bras" likely refers to the act of pulling off sleeves or stockings, as evidenced by the wager that follows.</note> — The braggart, finding himself without sleeves <note>original: "sans bras"</note>: — Not at all, he said, you won't make me pull them off. I bet a pair of stockings. — The one who had spoken of this trick accepted the challenge, approached, then took the stockings and wrapped them around the face of the one who put on the stockings and took the prize. <note>The humor relies on a physical prank or a play on words regarding the "pulling" of garments.</note> (*C. A. 75, v.*)

### *A retort to a witty remark.*

709. — Someone said to his companion: — Your eyes have all changed to a strange color. The other replied after a moment: — But you can change nothing about them. — And since when has this happened to you? And the other: — Every time my eyes meet your strange face, by the violence of the displeasure I feel, they grow pale and take on a strange color. (*C. A. 75, v.*)

710. — One said to another: — Your eyes have completely changed to a strange color. And the other replied: — It is because my eyes are looking at your strange face. <note>Leonardo often sketched "grotesques"—exaggerated or ugly faces—and these jokes reflect his interest in how the sight of ugliness or beauty affects the viewer.</note> (<term>C. A. 75, v.</term> <note>original: "Codex Atlanticus, folio 75 verso"</note>)

->*A witty remark.*<-

711. — Someone was saying that in his country were born the strangest things in the world. The other replied: — You, being here, confirm the truth of your assertion by the strangeness of your stupid presence. (<term>C. A. 75, v.</term>)

->*A friend to a slanderer.*<-

712. — Someone stopped associating with his friend because that man often spoke ill of his friends. The one who had been abandoned in this way was one day complaining and grieving, and he begged his former companion to tell him the reason that had caused him to turn his back on such a friendship. The other replied to him: "I no longer wish to associate with you because I wish you well; and I do not want you to speak ill of me, your friend, to others, nor for others to have as sad an impression of you as I do when you speak ill of me, your friend. Now, by no longer associating, people will believe we have become enemies; in that case, you may speak ill of me according to your habit, and you will not be as blamed for it as you would be if we were still intimate." <note>This anecdote highlights a sophisticated social logic: speaking ill of a friend ruins the speaker's reputation more than the victim's, so the "true" friend leaves to protect the slanderer from their own bad habit.</note> (<term>C. A. 300, v.</term>)

->*A retort to a witty remark.*<-

714. — Someone sees a large sword at another's side and says: — O poor soul, I have seen you bound to that weapon for a long time: why do you not untie yourself, since your hands are free and you are able to do so? — To which the other replies: — It is none of your business, and it is my habit. — The first man, feeling stung, replies: — I know you to be so ignorant of everything in the world that I thought the slightest thing revealed to you would seem new.

### *A saying about an invalid.*

716. — An invalid finding himself at the point of death, having heard a knocking at the door, asked one of his servants who was knocking at the entrance; and this servant replied that it was one called Madam Good <note>original: "madame Bonne"</note>. Then the invalid stretched out his arms, giving thanks to God in a loud voice, and told his servants to let this lady in immediately, so that he might see a "good woman" before dying, for in his life he had never seen one. (*R. 1290.*) <note>Richter edition, 1290</note>

### *A witty word said by a youth to an old man.*

717. — An old man was scorning a youth in public, showing audaciously that he did not fear him; the youth replied "that his great age served him as a better shield than his tongue or his strength." (*T. 8. r.*) <note>Manuscript T, folio 8 recto</note>

# XIX

## LETTERS

The journey to the East. — The preaching and persuasion of the faith. — The sudden flood until its end. — The ruin of the city. — The death of the people and their despair. — The pursuit of the preacher, his liberation and his benevolence. — The harm caused by the flood. — The wreck of the ship. — The invention of the prophet. — His prophecy. — The flooding of the lowlands of Western Armenia, which drained through the gap of Mount Taurus. — How the new prophet shows that this ruin was brought about for his sake. »

### I

#### DESCRIPTION OF MOUNT TAURUS <note>1</note> AND THE RIVER EUPHRATES

719. — *To the Devatdar <term>Devatdar: originally a "pen-case carrier," this was a title for a high-ranking chancellor or lieutenant in the Mamluk Sultanate</term> of Syria, lieutenant of the sacred Sultan of Babylon <note>original: "Sultan de Babylone." In the Renaissance, "Babylon" often referred to Cairo (Babylon of Egypt), the seat of the Mamluk Sultan.</note>.*

The recent catastrophe that occurred in our

<note>1. The description of the Taurus mountains, as seen at night, does not seem to me to be borrowed from Aristotle’s *Meteorology*; I share the opinion of Mr. Edmondo Solmi, who maintains that Leonardo actually traveled to Asia. This journey was not crowned with the expected success, and the master kept it silent as much as he could.</note>

The northern region, which has terrified the whole universe as much as yourself, shall be described to you in order, showing first the effect and then the cause.

Finding myself in this part of Armenia, to devote myself with love and care to the work for which you have called me; and to begin in those parts which seemed to me most suited to our purpose, I entered the city of Calindra <note>Modern Kelenderis or Gilindere</note>, near our borders.

This city is situated on that part of the Taurus range which is divided by the Euphrates and looks toward the peaks <note>original: "cornes" (horns)</note> of this great mountain, toward the west <note>original: "ponant"</note>.

These peaks are so high that they seem to touch the sky, and there is no terrestrial point in the universe higher than its summit; and always four hours before daybreak it is struck by the rays of the rising sun; and as the stone is by its nature very white, it shines brightly and serves the Armenians as a beautiful moonlight in the midst of the darkness; its elevation exceeds the greatest height of the clouds by a distance of four miles in a straight line. From many points in the West, this summit is seen illuminated by the sun from sunset until the third part of the night; and it is this which, in your country during clear weather, we had first taken for a comet, and which appeared to us in the darkness of the night to change shape, and sometimes

## II

### ASPECT OF MOUNT TAURUS

**720. — To the same.**

I am not, O Diodare <note>Likely a fictional or real official, possibly a "Diodorus" or Lieutenant of the Sultan of Egypt</note>, accusable of
laziness as you reproach me, but the
unbridled ardor created by the benefit I
hold from you, has constrained me, with great
application to seek and to discover with diligence
the cause of so great and so surprising an effect;
and this cause I could not discover, without
taking the time.

Now to satisfy you well on the origin

<summary>Leonardo explains how the high peaks of Mount Taurus reflect sunlight long after dark, creating optical illusions that observers mistake for comets, and addresses a letter to Diodare regarding his scientific investigations.</summary>
<keywords>Mount Taurus, Diodare, comet, west, east, clouds, optical illusion, solar rays, Codex Atlanticus, Armenia</keywords>

--- Page 355 ---
<meta>This page continues the correspondence addressed to Diodare, moving from the description of the atmospheric "comet" effect to a geographical description of Mount Taurus and the Caucasus region. It continues the sentence started on page 344: "Now to satisfy you well on the origin..."</meta>

<page-num>345</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

of such a phenomenon, it is necessary that I describe the shape of the site to you; and then we will come to the effect, and I believe you will be satisfied.

Do not complain, O Diodare <note>Diodare is addressed as a "Devatdar" or lieutenant of the Sultan of Egypt; scholars debate whether these letters describe real travels or are a fictional literary exercise by Leonardo</note>, that I have delayed in answering your urgent request, for what you ask of me is of such a nature that it cannot be well expressed without the necessary time; and above all, if one wishes to show the cause of a great effect, it is necessary to describe in proper form the nature of the site, and through this knowledge, you will easily be able to satisfy the aforementioned inquiry.

I will leave aside the description of the shape of Asia Minor and the seas and lands that determine the appearance of its surface, because I know that the activity and care of your studies have already given you knowledge of them: the task then is to characterize the true figure of Mount Taurus which caused this surprising and calamitous wonder <note>original: "prodige," meaning an extraordinary or supernatural event</note>, which we must concern ourselves with together.

This Mount Taurus is the same that many call the summit of Mount Caucasus: but having wished to be well-informed, I made a point of speaking with some of those who live above the Caspian Sea. They prove that although their mountains have the same name, they are higher, and they confirm that theirs is indeed the true Caucasus, because "Caucasus" in the Scythian language <note>The Scythians were ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia</note> means supreme altitude.

And truly, there is no knowledge

neither the East nor the West possesses a mountain so high; the proof of this is that the inhabitants of the countries located to the west <note>original: "ponant," a nautical term for the west</note> see the solar rays illuminating a portion of its summit until the fourth part of the night; and the same appears to the countries located in the East. (*C. A. 145, r.*) <note>Reference to the Codex Atlanticus, folio 145 recto</note>

### QUALITY AND QUANTITY OF MOUNT TAURUS

The shadow of the peak of the Taurus is so high that in the month of June, the sun at midday projects its shadow as far as the border of Sarmatia <note>An ancient region encompassing parts of present-day Poland, Ukraine, and southern Russia</note>, which is twelve days' journey away; and in mid-December, this shadow extends as far as the Hyperboreans <note>In Greek mythology, a people living in the far north "beyond the North Wind"</note>, a journey of one month toward the north. And the slope opposite the blowing wind is always full of clouds and snow, because the wind that strikes the rocks—which subsequently halt it—carries the clouds with it from all sides and leaves them there upon impact; and being constantly stripped by the strike of lightning due to the multitude of clouds that conceal it, the rock is entirely shattered and full of great ruins.

This mountain, at its base, is inhabited by wealthy peoples; there are beautiful springs, rivers, and a fertility and abundance of every good thing, especially in the southern part; when one ascends for three miles, one begins to encounter the

<vocab>Mount Taurus, Sarmatia, Hyperboreans, East, West, West (Ponant), Slope</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the staggering height of Mount Taurus, claiming its summit remains illuminated long after sunset and its shadow reaches as far as Russia and the Arctic circle. It also details the violent weather patterns and lightning strikes that shatter the mountain's upper peaks while contrasting them with the fertile, inhabited base.</summary>
<keywords>Mount Taurus, geography, atmospheric phenomena, shadows, Sarmatia, Hyperboreans, lightning, mountain climate</keywords>

--- Page 357 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical description of Mount Taurus, detailing the transition from forested slopes to the desolate, frozen peaks above the clouds, followed by the beginning of a letter to an unknown correspondent.</meta>

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

forests of great trees: firs, pines, oaks, and other similar species. Then, after a distance of another three miles, there is a zone of meadows and immense pastures. All the rest, up to the summit of Mount Taurus, is nothing but eternal snow which never disappears at any time; it extends to a height of about fourteen miles in total. From there, at the height of one mile, the clouds never rise—and these clouds themselves span fifteen miles. At about five miles of altitude in a straight line, and as much again or nearly so, we find the peak of the "horns" of the Taurus, where one begins to encounter the air that grows cold; one no longer feels the breath of the winds, and nothing could live there, nor be born there, except for some birds of prey that nest in the high fissures of the Taurus and then descend below the clouds to seek their prey among the grassy mountains.

Here, on all sides, is bare rock with the clouds above; and the rock is very white, but one cannot reach the summit, as the climb is too arduous and perilous. (*C. A. 145, r. and v.*) <note>Reference to the Codex Atlanticus, folio 145 recto and verso</note>

# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

...lord, by the much-desired recovery of your health, so much so that my own illness has, so to speak, left me. But I have been greatly tormented by my inability to fulfill my duties to Your Excellence <note>Likely Giuliano de' Medici, Leonardo's patron in Rome</note>, due to the malice of that German liar for whom I did everything possible to please. And afterwards, I invited him to live and eat with me, so as to compel him to continue his work and easily correct his errors; furthermore, I taught him Italian so that he could speak without an interpreter: finally, his wages were never delayed. Since then, his obsession was to have finished models in wood, just as they were to be in iron, in order to carry them away to his own country. I refused, saying that I would give him in a drawing the width, length, thickness, and shape of what he had to make: and since then we have been on bad terms.

The second matter was that he set up another workshop for himself with a workbench and tools; and there he slept or worked for others; afterwards he went to dine with the Swiss Guard <note>The Pope's military guard</note>, an idle lot, and for that reason well-suited to our man. And more than once he went off with two or three of them, and with their guns they shot at birds among the ancient monuments, and this sometimes lasted until evening.

And if I sent Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo Lotto or perhaps Leonardo's pupil, Lorenzo</note> to beg him to work, he would become angry, saying that he did not want so many masters over his head, and that his work was

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci writes to his patron expressing frustration with a dishonest German assistant who neglected his duties, socialized with the Swiss Guard, and attempted to steal Leonardo's mechanical designs.</summary>

Two months passed in this manner, and afterward, finding Gian Niccola <note>Gian Niccola was likely an assistant or official in the Medici household</note> in the wardrobe, he asked if the German had finished the work for the Magnificent <note>original: "Magnifico" — a title for Giuliano de' Medici</note> and he told me no, that he had only cleaned two rifles for him. Since then, I have had him urged to work; and he abandons the workshop and wastes time making another workbench and files and other instruments to manufacture reels for twisting silk, which he would hide with a thousand blasphemies and recriminations whenever one of my people entered, so that no one wanted to go there anymore.

In the end, I discovered what the truth was regarding this Master Giovanni of the Mirrors <note>original: "Jean des Miroirs" / "Giovanni degli Specchi"</note>. He has done all this for two reasons: the first that he stated was that my arrival had deprived him of the conversation of Your Lordship. The other is that he claims the room where these men stay is suitable for him to manufacture mirrors; of this he has no proof, except as an act of enmity. He has had his belongings sold and in his shop he works with many laborers to send numerous mirrors to the fairs. (*C. A. 243, v*) (1).

# APPENDICES

## THE TRUE PORTRAIT OF LEONARDO

The Italian masters left few portraits of themselves. We only know of Leonardo's through the one in the Uffizi <note>The Uffizi Gallery in Florence</note> which is not entirely by his own hand, the red chalk drawing <note>original: "sanguine"</note> at Windsor, and its copy in the ***Am-***
***brosiana*** <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan</note>.

This Windsor drawing, reproduced here from the Braun photograph, shows us the master with authenticity, but treated by the pencil of a pupil. Until the publication by Professor Giulio Carotti (¹) and the distribution of the postcard from the Montabone house reproducing the ***Self-Portrait*** <note>original: "Autorittrato"</note> (²), a head of an old man found in the Royal Library of Turin was given as the true image; while it is indeed by the hand of da Vinci, it cannot represent him.

The deep wrinkles of the forehead, the sinking of the eye beneath the arch, the bristling of the eyebrows, the curve of the nose, and the widening of the nostrils, the line

---

1. *The works of Leonardo, Bramante, and Raphael* <note>original: "Le opere di Leonardo, Bramante et Raphaello"</note> with 188 figures. Milan, Hoepli, 1905: 9 francs.
2. The Montabone house of Milan has published heliotype <note>An early photographic process using gelatin-coated plates</note> postcards based on the drawings of Leonardo, which are small marvels despite the extreme modesty of the price.

<summary>This appendix examines the authenticity of various portraits of Leonardo da Vinci, arguing that a famous drawing in Turin is a work by the master but not a likeness of himself.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, art history, portraiture, Windsor collection, Giulio Carotti, Uffizi, iconography</keywords>

--- Page 370 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the iconography of Leonardo da Vinci, specifically critiquing a supposed portrait in Turin before listing his scientific precursors to modern discoveries.</meta>

sorrowful and peevish about the mouth, and everything in the construction and expression is inconsistent with Leonardo's physiognomy and the Uffizi portrait, as well as the red chalk drawing <note>original: "sanguine"</note> at Windsor, which are authentic images.

Beltrami <note>Luca Beltrami (1854–1933), an Italian architect and art historian</note> wanted to see Leonardo in the *Plato* of the *School of Athens*: I would see him much rather in the *Aristotle* of the same fresco, and not because Leonardo is intellectually closer to the Stagirite <note>A common epithet for Aristotle, born in Stagira</note>, but because Raphael's character bears a striking resemblance to the Windsor red chalk drawing.

## DISCOVERIES OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

Copernicus: *Law of gravitation.*
Leonardo: *On the fall of bodies combined with the rotation of the earth.*

Kepler: *Fixed light of the stars.*
Leonardo formally states that the twinkling is in our eye.

Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), a German astronomer and mentor to Kepler</note>: *Solar reflection.*
Leonardo considers that the earth, illuminated by the sun, is also a "land" in relation to the moon, with the same phases; and if at the new moon we see the dark part, it is because the earth reflects the solar rays <note>This phenomenon is now known as "Earthshine" or the "Da Vinci Glow"</note>.

Halley: *Atmospheric movement and trade winds.*
Leonardo thinks that the action of the sun raises the level of the equinoctial ocean and that equilibrium is restored when the waters fall back toward both sides of the pole.

<summary>The author disputes traditional identifications of Leonardo's face in art and provides a comparative list showing how Leonardo's scientific observations predated famous discoveries in physics and astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Raphael, School of Athens, Aristotle, Luca Beltrami, astronomy, gravitation, Earthshine, trade winds, scientific history</keywords>

--- Page 371 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparative list of Leonardo da Vinci’s scientific observations, demonstrating how his insights in physics, anatomy, and engineering predated famous discoveries by later scientists like Galileo, Pascal, and Lavoisier.</meta>

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

---

Galileo: *Theory of varied movements and accelerating forces.*

Leonardo: *Experiment on the descent of two balls. (Manuscript M. 61, recto.)*

Pascal: *Equilibrium of liquids.*

Leonardo: *Found throughout his writings.* <note>original: "Passim"</note>

Archimedes: *Theory of the lever.*

Leonardo: "The weight attached to the end of a lever will lift at the end of the counter-lever a weight greater than itself, by as many times as the counter-lever goes into the lever." (*Manuscript A. 47.*)

Commandino and Maurolyco <note>Federico Commandino and Francesco Maurolico, 16th-century mathematicians who worked on centers of gravity</note>: *Center of gravity of the pyramid.*

Leonardo finds it at one-quarter of the straight line joining the apex to the center of gravity of the base. (*Manuscript F. 31, recto.*)

The anatomist Knox <note>Robert Knox (1791–1862), a Scottish anatomist</note> affirms that Leonardo’s drawing representing the valves of the heart assumes a correct idea of their function.

The architectural drawing (*Manuscript B. 18, verso*) commented on by Mr. de Geymüller <note>Heinrich von Geymüller, a pioneer in the study of Renaissance architecture</note>, proves that Leonardo would have been a great architect since the classical style of Bramante is characterized there just as it appears at *San Pietro in Montorio.* <note>The Tempietto in Rome, a masterpiece of High Renaissance architecture</note>

The mausoleum from the Vallardi collection at the Louvre, a masterpiece.

System of the main sewer. (*Manuscript B. 15 and 16, verso.*)

Castelli <note>Benedetto Castelli (1578–1643), a student of Galileo</note>, founder of hydraulics.

"Da Vinci had noticed everything that Castelli said, later after him, on the movement of waters, but the first seems to me much superior to the second."

Lavoisier: *Combustion and respiration.*

Leonardo (*Manuscript E. 70, verso*). (*Manuscript I. 69 recto.*)

It highlights his contributions to architecture and his early understanding of the mechanics of the heart and the laws of the lever.</summary>

> “I am entirely persuaded that Leonardo was the best <term>anatomist</term> <note>a specialist in the structure of living things</note> that existed in the world in his time; he was the first to introduce the practice of anatomical drawings.”

## TESTAMENT OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

Be it manifest to every person present and to come, that in the court of the King our lord, at Amboise, before us personally appeared, Master Leonardo da Vinci, painter to the King, currently residing at the place called Cloux <note>Now known as the Château du Clos Lucé</note> near Amboise, who, considering the certainty of death and the uncertainty of its hour, has acknowledged and confessed in the said court before us—to which he has submitted and does submit regarding his actions—the tenor of this present testament and his last will and testament as follows:

Firstly, he commends his soul to our sovereign Master and Lord God, to the glorious Virgin Mary, to Monsignor Saint Michael, and to all the blessed angels, saints, and holy women of Paradise.

Also, the said testator wishes to be buried in the church of Saint-Florentin of Amboise, and that his body be carried there by the chaplains of the same.

Also, that his body be accompanied from the said place to the said church of Saint-Florentin by the college of the said church and also by the rector and prior, or by the vicars and chaplains of the church of Saint-Denis of Amboise, as well as by the Friars Minor <note>The Franciscans</note> of the said place.

And, before his body is carried into the said church, the testator wishes that there be celebrated in the said church of Saint-Florentin three high masses with

Also, in the said church of Saint-Denis, a similar service shall be celebrated, and also in the church of the aforementioned brothers and religious minors <note>The Franciscans</note>.

Also, the aforementioned testator gives and grants to Master Francesco de Melzi, gentleman of Milan, in gratitude for the services he has rendered to him in the past, each and every one of the books that the said testator now possesses, and other instruments and drawings concerning his art and the profession of painter.

Also, the testator gives and grants forever, and in perpetuity, to Battista de Villanis, his servant, one half of a garden that he owns outside the walls of Milan, and the other half of this garden to Salai, his servant; in which garden the aforementioned Salai has built and constructed a house, which shall likewise be and remain forever the property of the said Salai, his heirs, and successors; and this in reward for the good and pleasant services that the said de Villanis and Salai, his said servants, have performed for him before this day.

Also, the same testator gives to Mathurine, his servant, a garment of good black cloth lined with fur, a cloth cap, and ten <term>ducats</term> <note>A gold coin used throughout Europe during the Renaissance</note> paid once only; and this likewise in reward for the good services of the said Mathurine up to this day.

Provided, however, that the said de Villanis survives the aforementioned Mr. Leonardo da Vinci.

In the presence of the said Mr. Francesco da Melzi and of me, the notary.

### *Works of the Florentine Period.*

The Angel in Verrocchio’s *Baptism of Christ* at the Academy of Florence.
The *Annunciation*, a predella <note>A small painting or series of paintings along the frame at the bottom of an altarpiece</note> at the Louvre.
Portrait of a woman, Liechtenstein Gallery, Vienna <note>Likely the portrait of Ginevra de' Benci</note>.
Sculpture: Madonna and Child, terracotta (South Kensington) <note>Now the Victoria and Albert Museum</note>.
St. John the Baptist, terracotta (South Kensington).

### *Milanese Period (1483-1499).*

1483. At the court of the Duke of Milan.
Equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza.
Portrait of Cecilia Gallerani (the woman with the ermine <note>original: "la femme à la fouine" - literally "the woman with the beech marten," though the animal is an ermine</note>) at the Krakow Museum.
1489. February 2, organizes the festivities for the marriage of Duke Gian Galeazzo with Isabella of Aragon.
1491. June 18, wedding of Ludovico <note>Ludovico "Il Moro" Sforza</note> with Beatrice d'Este; a new model of the horse <note>for the Sforza monument</note>.
1492. Paintings at the Castle of Milan <note>Sforza Castle</note>.
1495. December 8, wedding of Bianca Sforza with the Emperor Maximilian.
1498. Has finished the *Last Supper*.
1499. Has finished the model for the equestrian statue; receives from the Duke a vineyard near the Vercellina Gate.
September 2, flight of Ludovico.
September 7, entry of Trivulzio <note>Gian Giacomo Trivulzio, an Italian aristocrat and military commander</note> into Milan.
— entry of Louis XII <note>King of France</note>.
