### LEONARDO’S DRAWINGS

We owe as much to photography as to printing when it reproduces and multiplies masterpieces: we even owe it more, for one can copy a manuscript, but not a drawing.

The work of art is unique, and unfortunately perishable.

The lens multiplies it and saves it from oblivion. Thus, according to the beautiful phrase by Paul de Saint-Victor:

> "Every drawing by Raphael has henceforth acquired the publicity of a page of Virgil; every thought of Michelangelo is popularized like a verse of Dante; a small coin pays for such a sketch by Leonardo da Vinci that all the gold in the world could not have pulled from its display case or its portfolio."

Since the time when the author of *The Two Masques* <note>original: "Les Deux Masques" - a major work of criticism by Paul de Saint-Victor.</note> wrote, true humanists have had their picture galleries <term>pinacothèque: a gallery or collection of paintings</term> just as they have their libraries, that is to say the

<summary>This page completes the inventory of Leonardo da Vinci's library and argues that photography is essential for preserving and sharing unique artworks like his drawings.</summary>
<keywords>library inventory, Vitruvius, Albert the Great, anatomy, Dante, photography, art reproduction, Raphael, Michelangelo, Paul de Saint-Victor</keywords>

--- Page 383 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how photography has revolutionized the study of art by allowing for accurate facsimiles of Leonardo da Vinci's drawings, which were previously only available through the interpretive lens of engravers.</meta>

<page-num>373</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

photographs of masterpieces alongside printed editions; the facsimile henceforth takes its place among books.

People began by engraving the drawings: but engraving is an art which, like all arts, lives only through interpretation, and it transposes what it imitates. Etching <term>eau forte: a process using acid to bite into a metal plate, known for its tonal, painterly qualities</term>, with its play of coloration, achieves no more fidelity than the methodical burin <note>A handheld steel tool used for traditional copperplate engraving.</note>. Many admirable prints exist, but no truly valid reproduction ever emerged from the hand-press; rather, it was one work of art created after another. It is thanks to photography that we have on our desks the fourteen manuscripts of the Institute <note>Referring to the manuscripts held by the Institut de France in Paris.</note> and the complete *Codex Atlanticus* <note>original: "Codex Atlantico" — the largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings, covering a vast range of subjects.</note>, conforming to the originals even down to the yellow tone of the paper, the stains, and the creases.

Leonardo da Vinci’s manuscripts do not only contain his discoveries and his thoughts; these five thousand pages are interspersed with several thousand curious drawings and sketches, most of them quite beautiful and sometimes sublime. A single volume would not suffice to describe this pandemonium <note>Used here to mean an overwhelming, diverse, and chaotic collection.</note> where angels and sorcerers, machines and architecture, canals and cannons, flowers and anatomical studies, halberds and physics apparatuses, caricatures and celestial faces, optics and ballistics, the flight of birds and the movement of water, the proportions of man and those of the horse, stereotomy <term>stéréatomie: the technical art of cutting three-dimensional solids, such as stone or wood, into complex shapes for construction</term> and hydraulics, all mingle in a dizzying kaleidoscopic motion. Dreams are superimposed upon experience, and technical blueprints upon arabesques. Here are flowers more precise than those of Dürer <note>Albrecht Dürer, the German Renaissance master famous for his detailed studies of nature.</note>; there is an anatomical drawing better than any found in textbooks. Even when providing a mechanical diagram, even

<summary>This page discusses the superiority of photography over engraving for accurately reproducing Leonardo’s manuscripts and describes the immense, diverse range of subjects found within his thousands of sketches.</summary>
<keywords>photography, engraving, Codex Atlanticus, facsimiles, Dürer, anatomy, technical drawing, manuscript preservation</keywords>

--- Page 384 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the intrinsic value of drawings versus finished paintings, focusing on the distinct geniuses of Leonardo, Raphael, and Michelangelo.</meta>

<page-num>374</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS</header>

In a factory sketch, Leonardo reveals himself, and his line, even when applied to the lowest practical necessity <note>original: "basse contingence"</note>, never ceases to be aesthetic. The artist is always behind the engineer, and it happens that one falls into ecstasy before a bombard <note>A type of early cannon or mortar</note> simply for its proportions.

It requires more culture to take pleasure in drawings than in paintings: the proof is found in the prices of the past and the present. In no sale has a drawing ever reached the price of a painting by the same master, which it often surpassed in quality.

Drawings are most often considered as elements of study, as stages of a conception like the various successive states of a print; I view the drawings I cite as entirely realized works, complete masterpieces; admirable in themselves, without any relation to an existing or lost painting.

Leonardo, judged by his drawings alone, would still be the master of the face. Raphael organizes just as well and even more easily; therein lies his true genius.

The Rooms <note>The Stanze di Raffaello in the Vatican</note>, the 54 pages of the Loggias, the tapestry cartoons make Sanzio <note>Raphael's surname, Sanzio</note> the most perfect of organizers. Michelangelo possesses the lyrical and passionate rhetoric of the body to an incomparable degree. If the Titans had drawn, they would have done so like him, the creator of the plastic superhuman <note>original: "surhomme plastique"</note>, the unrivaled amplifier of organic movement. Leonardo, Raphael-esque in the *Last Supper*, Michelangelo-esque in the *Battle of Anghiari*, reveals himself completely and without rival in the human head: he truly gave masks to mystery. No one has expressed intelligence like him; and in many heads, his ge—

<meta>catchword: ge- (likely "génie" or genius)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that drawings should be viewed as complete masterpieces rather than mere sketches, comparing the specific strengths of Leonardo (the face), Raphael (composition), and Michelangelo (the body).</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Raphael, Michelangelo, art criticism, drawing vs painting, aesthetics, Renaissance masters</keywords>

--- Page 385 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leonardo da Vinci’s mastery of the human head, contrasting his sublime "paradise heads" with his famous caricatures and providing a bibliographic overview of his drawings.</meta>

<page-num>375</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...expressed it entirely: it is these heads that I designate for the devotion of cultivated beings. I do not think of contemporary artists, because that would be to condemn them to despair. Is it not already playing a diabolical trick on them to invite aesthetes to the contemplation of these heads of paradise, where intelligence and mystery radiate through forms of eternity?

A characteristic thing regarding the baseness of taste even among the most cultivated: the caricatures were published before the other drawings.

Hollar (1645-1650) engraved those from the Arundel collection (re-engraved in 1786, quarto, 16 plates, 64 heads).

Caylus (1730) engraved the Mariette collection under the title *Collection of Character Heads and Caricatures* <note>original: "Recueil de têtes de caractères et de charges"</note>, quarto (59 plates). Gerli (1784). These collections no longer hold interest now that we have facsimiles.

It must be admitted, Leonardo's caricatures do not deserve such enthusiasm: most of the time, they play upon the Jewish type <note>A reflection of the era's common physiognomic prejudices</note>; and if they please, it is because it is more natural for most men to recognize themselves in ugliness, which seems to absolve them, than in the terrible beauty that accuses them.

Leonardo's drawings are so numerous, and too many of them are still unpublished, for one to dream of cataloging and describing them; the *Codex Atlanticus* <note>original: "Codex Atlantico"</note> alone contains 1,700 of them across 339 leaves.

To be useful to the reader, I will cite only a selection among those I consider the most beautiful, but only from published series that one can obtain, as a matter of practical information; first in the Braun collection, the most numerous as...

<summary>The author critiques the historical preference for Leonardo's caricatures over his sublime "paradise heads," arguing that people prefer ugliness to the "accusing" nature of beauty. He begins a practical guide to Leonardo's vast corpus of drawings, including the Codex Atlanticus.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, aesthetics, caricatures, Arundel collection, Codex Atlanticus, art history, Wenceslaus Hollar, Anne Claude de Caylus</keywords>

...the most perfect, followed by the Alinari collection in Florence, the Montabone collection in Milan, and finally that of Brogi. <note>These firms were the giants of 19th-century art photography, making museum masterpieces accessible to the public for the first time.</note>

## EDITIONS OF THE DRAWINGS

### I

The house of Braun and Clément <note>Adolphe Braun’s photography firm was famous for its high-quality "carbon prints" which captured the texture of original drawings.</note> has assembled, in addition to Leonardo's paintings, about five hundred drawings, admirably reproduced, with prices varying according to the format from 2 to 9 francs. These prints, produced in color on tinted paper, often achieve a perfect imitation of the original if they are framed right to the edge of the photograph.

->*At the Louvre:*<-

62, 162. Cartoon for the portrait of Isabella d’Este <note>original: "Isabelle de Gonzague"</note>, entirely perforated <term>pounced: the process of pricking holes along outlines to transfer a design to canvas by dusting it with charcoal powder</term> along the contours for transfer to canvas.
62, 163. The Woman with the Horizontal Gaze, an ideal type of malice, a splendid figure of implacability, still little known.
62, 165. Head of Bacchus, facing forward.

62, 174. Youthful head with very thick hair, in red chalk <term>sanguine: a drawing technique using reddish-brown iron oxide crayons</term>.

# ON LEONARDO DA VINCI

One must first mention the portrait of an old man with a furrowed brow by the hand of Leonardo, which has been quite wrongly identified as a self-portrait (1058); it is a red chalk drawing <note>original: "sanguine"</note> held at the Royal Library of Turin.

The woman in a turban, with a pleated bodice and a long chain, her hands crossed with a singular sense of reality, which is a portrait without idealization (91).

The beautiful bent arm (1115).

The page of the twelve apostles in red chalk (1086), which shows the realistic starting points for the *Last Supper* <note>original: "Cenacolo"</note>.

The beautiful profile of a man in a beret (107).

The battle outside the ramparts, a long and curious piece notable for the number of figures and costumes (1110).

The three prodigious sketches (1083) for the episode of the battle standards, the most beautiful "scribbles" in existence—a melee contained within the space of a five-franc coin <note>A silver coin approximately 37mm in diameter; the drawings are in Venice</note>.

The Virgin with closed eyelids (6), a pure masterpiece; the woman with smooth hair (69) drawn from life, and likewise from a model, the woman with the long nose (1114); the battle of the dragon and the lion (34).

The landscape dated 1473; the staircase with horses (181).

The red chalk drawing of the <term>putti</term> <note>Plural of putto; Italian for "boys," referring to the chubby infant figures common in Renaissance art</note> of Venice (1064), the drapery of a standing figure (150) and that of a seated figure (151), and the sheet of the man inscribed simultaneously within a circle and a square (1035) <note>The famous "Vitruvian Man," which illustrates the proportions of the human body</note>.

The curly-haired youth in profile facing the bust of an old man (167).

The angel with lowered eyes (270), the head of Bacchus facing forward on green paper; the Christ of Venice, the head of Saint Anne, the young woman in profile with a veil (3), the young girl with the strong nose (1063) and above all the profile in <term>red chalk</term> <note>original: "sanguine"; a drawing technique using reddish-brown natural pigments</note> of Isabella d'Este (24) are worth as much, to a cultured man, as the paintings of the distinguished master.

### III

Carlo Fumagalli (Montabone photography) in Milan, Piazza Turini, 7.

One must mention *Drawings of Leonardo and his school at the Ambrosian Library*, text by Luca Beltrami, twenty-six plates by Carlo Fumagalli. These <term>heliotypes</term> <note>an early photographic process for high-quality mechanical reproduction</note> in the format and color of the original are truly beautiful.

I will point out the head in the antique style bent forward, black pencil, of a character as pure as a Phidias <note>referring to the famous Greek sculptor of the 5th century BCE</note>; the <term>red chalk</term> of the Virgin from the Saint Anne, the magnificent blue paper of the woman with the necklace—an ideal princess from a Shakespearean drama; the stout boy in profile with pricked outlines <note>pouncing or "pouncing marks" used to transfer a drawing to another surface</note>, the bald old man with the kind smile, the head used to develop the nude Mona Lisa of the Hermitage in Saint Petersburg, the old warrior in profile; a beautiful <term>academy</term> <note>original: "académie"; a formal study of the human figure, usually nude</note> of a man, gray paper heightened with white; two adorable studies of female mouths; the famous Christ with lowered eyelids from the Brera.

I invite the attentive to study the fragment of the <term>cartoon</term> <note>original: "carton"; a full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco or painting</note> of the School of Athens which depicts Aristotle and Plato,

<page-num>381</page-num>
<header>ON LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

to add their voice to this most curious question: Can one recognize Leonardo in Raphael's Plato? <note>Referring to the figure of Plato in Raphael's fresco "The School of Athens," often thought to be modeled on Leonardo.</note> Mr. Beltrami asserts it is so, based on the resemblance to the <term>red chalk</term> <note>original: "sanguine"</note> drawing at Windsor. For my part, it is Aristotle who resembles the famous <term>red chalk</term> drawing that a pupil made (as the eminent Carotti rightly says) from life.

->IV<-

### Brogi Collection, in Florence.

This collection includes 52 items from the *Ambrosiana* and the *Uffizi*, all printed in black. One finds there the characteristic pieces already enumerated: such as the profile of the man in the <term>beret</term> <note>original: "toque"</note> (1865), Isabella of Mantua (1867), the woman with the double necklace (1162), the woman with flat hair (1873), the bowing Virgin (1868), the combat of the lion and the dragon (1620), the angel with lowered eyes (1878), and a selection of caricatures. Among the latter, I will point out the peasant woman with the "cooking-pot-handle" nose, the Audrey of *As You Like It* (1165) <note>A reference to the rustic character in Shakespeare's play</note>. I recognize the style of the *Droll Dreams* <note>original: "Songes drôlatiques"; referring to the "Droll Dreams of Pantagruel," a series of 16th-century woodcuts</note> in number 1168, which is not by Leonardo, nor is number 1169, so curious for its variations on the Jewish type.

One finds at Brogi's some pages torn from notebooks, which are so strange due to the mixture and superposition of <term>expressive heads</term> <note>original: "têtes d'expression"; character studies focusing on intense facial emotions</note> and machines (1622), or by the clash of a bat's wing with a winch and a full-length figure rising up among the sketches.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Leonardo da Vinci's drawings in the Brogi collection, transitioning from the discussion of character studies to technical sketches and drapery studies used for his major paintings.</meta>

<page-num>382</page-num>
<header>SELECTED TEXTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

of machines and tools. One must mention the studies of draperies of incomparable nobility (1618) which served for the *Saint Anne* <note>Leonardo’s "The Virgin and Child with Saint Anne," famous for its complex arrangement of figures and fabric</note>, another standing figure (1870) which the master did not use, and number 1869 which served for the *Adoration of the Magi* <note>Leonardo's large-scale, unfinished early painting depicting the biblical scene</note>. Certain photographs bear the label *attributed* <note>original: "attribuito"</note> with great wisdom; neither the profile 1163, nor the old man 1171, nor the peasant-like type (1180), nor the hand holding the rod (1172) are by his hand.

But these are works of his school <note>The "Leonardeschi," or the circle of pupils and followers who worked in Leonardo's style</note> and are highly instructive for measuring how much an intelligent imitation can absorb from an incomparable model.

<term>Calibration</term> <note>The process of adjusting a piece of equipment to ensure its accuracy—in this case, ensuring the camera captures true colors and correct physical dimensions.</note>

<term>Color Checker</term> <note>A standardized grid of 24 colored squares used by archivists to ensure that the digital image's colors are faithful to the original object.</note>

<term>Measurement Ruler</term> <note>A physical scale (produced here by the Oregon Rule Co.) placed alongside an object to provide a permanent record of its size in the digital file.</note>

<term>GretagMacbeth</term> <note>The company that originally developed the ColorChecker standard, a vital tool in professional photography and digital preservation.</note>
